#694. Germany news: Emergency at Leipzig airport sparked by novice driver

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Two men breached a fence at the Leipzig/Halle airport while practicing for a driving test. Meanwhile, German Chancellor Friedrich Merz announced an industrial AI partnership with Nvidia. DW has more.

This blog is now closed. Below is a roundup of the stories from and about Germany on Monday, October 19, 2026.

Berlin's Left Party, the Greens and the Social Democrats are set to move ahead on talks to form the next coalition government in the capital city.

The SPD's state leadership approved the motion with a wide majority in an online session, and the Greens voted overwhelmingly in favor of talks at a small party convention.

The Left Party has not scheduled a conference until October 31 because members of the party may be away because of school holidays.

The Left Party is hoping to have Elif Eralp lead the state government.

The coalition talks are set to open after weeks of tense negotiations and a dispute over alleged antisemitism within the ranks of the Left Party.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz and Nvidia chief executive Jensen Huang unveiled a new artificial intelligence (AI) cooperation at a glitzy investment summit in Berlin on Monday evening.

"Germany is open for business," said Merz at the Invest in Germany event, adding that the combination of German engineering and cutting-edge AI technology would help strengthen the country's economy in the long term.

"Germany is open for investment and Germany is ready to grow at a faster pace," the chancellor continued, before joking: "If I were Jensen Huang, I would put on my leather jacket, take a flight to Berlin, meet the German Chancellor, and tell him, 'I'm ready to invest.'"

The partnership with Nvidia is intended to speed up the digital transformation of German industry and secure the country's technological sovereignty.

According to the company, Nvidia invested nearly $1 billion in Germany last year and holds stakes in numerous start-ups and established companies.

But Europe's largest economy has nevertheless struggled to attract foreign investment after years of weak growth, with high energy prices and labor costs continuing to deter businesses.

German officials were hoping that Monday's summit, which was attended by more than 200 domestic and international investment executives, would showcase Germany as an attractive investment destination, highlighting its sound public finances, status as the only G7 country with a triple-A credit rating, its universities, skilled workforce and tariff-free trade within the EU single market.

Further behind-closed-door talks are set to take place on Tuesday.

Do you remember those arrests in Germany last week, connected to an arson attempt at a Starlink control center in Poland?

The government in Warsaw called it a hybrid attack and accused Russia of recruiting organized crime elements for the alleged crime.

It's much too early to say for sure, but Bulgaria has now issued a pair of European arrest warrants against German citizens in a case that seems rather reminiscent in several regards.

The two 19-year-olds are accused of driving in from Serbia claiming to be tourists, setting fire to a series of antennae south of Sofia, and leaving the country again within a matter of hours.

Hermann Höllenreiner, a Sinti German raised in Bavaria who survived the Nazis' Auschwitz concentration camp as a child, has died aged 92.

Munich's Hanser Verlag publisher and the International Auschwitz Committee said that he had passed away last Friday, October 16, in Mettenheim in Bavaria, surrounded by his family.

Höllenreiner, known as "Mano" to his large Bavarian Sinti family, spent time at the Auschwitz-Birkenau, Ravensbrück and Sachsenhausen camps as a child.

"Only later in his life did he start to tell his story and became a major witness of the era, who hauntingly reported on the horrors of Auschwitz, the perpetrators and the victims, and above all of the still-persistnent discrimination of Sinti and Roma in Europe," Christioph Heubner, executive vice president of the International Auschwitz Committee, said.

"Mano Höllenreiner's biography and tales of suffering were enough for more than one life. What he saw with his own eyes as a child in Auschwitz and what was done to him — for many decades he didn't know how to speak out about it," Heubner said, thanking Höllenreiner for his "courage and humanity" in ultimately telling his story.

Höllenriener managed to flee one of the so-called "death marches" fleeing extermination camps in as Allied armies approached.

Author Anja Tuckermann told his story in a book whose title roughly translates as: "Mano. The boy who didn't know where he was."

He was initially found on a roadside and rescued by liberated French prisoners, who took him home with them. In 1946, he was reunited with surviving relatives in Germany.

Two men have admitted to breaking through a fence at Leipzig/Halle airport while one of them was practicing for a driving test, an incident that triggered a major security operation, police said on Monday.

The two, aged 20 and 32, handed themselves in to the police on Sunday evening, following the incident. The broken fence was first discovered by airport staff late on Saturday.

Police said the 20-year-old had been at the wheel despite not having a valid license.

An investigation was opened into the learner driver for fleeing the scene after breaking through the fence.

The mass-circulation Bild newspaper reported that the car involved, of which fragments were found at the site, was an Audi.

The airport was the site of a security scare in August, when an explosives-laden drone was found there near Ukrainian cargo planes.

German officials accused Russia of having planned an attack, something Moscow denied.

Germany's Rheinmetall defense group on Monday announced that it had acquired Romania's Mangalia shipyard at auction in partnership with Swiss shipping giant MSC.

Rheinmetall said it had paid approximately €138 million ($154 million) for the facility.   Acquisition is expected to be finalized within a few weeks.   Rheinmetall and MSC plan to develop the Black Sea shipyard near Constanța into a dual-use site for civilian and military shipbuilding for both domestic and international markets.

Rheinmetall says its Naval Systems division will use the facility to build four vessels commissioned by the Romanian government, including two 96-meter (305 foot) offshore patrol vessels and two 43-meter diving support vessels.   The company also intends to modernize the shipyard's infrastructure and expand its capacity.

"In doing so," said the company, ​"Rheinmetall ⁠is creating a scalable foundation for future ⁠projects in ​southeastern Europe and strengthens its position in a strategically important market."

The Foreign Ministry has summoned Iran's ambassador after two men were convicted of preparing attacks on Jewish figures and pro-Israel targets in Germany at the behest of Tehran.

"We vehemently condemn Iran's activities. We will not allow Iran to threaten our liberal society," the ministry wrote on X.

"We do not tolerate attacks on Jewish life and those who defend it anywhere, and particularly not in Germany," it wrote.

A Hamburg court on Thursday sentenced a 54-year-old man and a 43-year old man respectively to 8 1/2 and 4 1/2 years in prison for having spied on the president of the Central Council of Jews in Germany, Josef Schuster, and Volker Beck, the president of the German-Israeli Society (DIG).

In the court's opinion, their actions were carried out in preparation for terrorist attacks, including the planned murders of  Schuster and Beck.

The court said the 54-year-old, who was said to have also done the groundwork for an arson attack on a kosher food store in Berlin, received his orders directly from officers in the Quds Force.

The Quds Force is the branch of Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) that is in charge of extraterritorial operations.

German industrial businesses employed some 496,000 fewer people at the end of 2025 compared to the start of 2019, according to a survey conducted by the Ifo Institute economic think tank.

Worst-hit has been the automotive industry, a key sector of the German economy, where 123,500 jobs were cut both at carmakers and suppliers, a decline of 13%.

But the metals sector also saw a marked decline in the number of jobs available, dropping 115,600 workers in the period, while the machinery sector bled 64,500 positions.

At the same time, more than 2.2 million additional jobs were created in the service sector, according to Ifo.

It said 391,000 new jobs had been established in the health sector and another 357,000 in social services, including care homes.

Joachim Ragnitz, managing director at Ifo Dresden, said the figures showed an "accelerated structural shift" in Germany.

"Ironically, the very sectors that have sustained our prosperity up to now are shrinking," he said.

"The new jobs are for the most part not being created where Germany is especially productive and innovative. That may slow down our growth potential in the future."

A German woman has died in a climbing accident in the Italian Alps, mountain rescuers said on Monday.

The 32-year-old woman, who was an experienced mountaineer, fell more than 50 meters (164 feet) to her death while on the Cengia dei Camosci, a difficult route on the 2,323-meter Grande Nabois mountain near the Slovenian border.

The rescue service said the accident was caused when a climbing hold gave way under the woman, who was accompanied by an Austrian fellow climber at the time.

Investigations are underway to clarify the exact circumstances of her death.

Carmaker Volkswagen intends to freeze the salaries of some 660,000 employees worldwide until 2030 as the company, along with the entire car industry, faces a range of challenges that have impacted its profits.

Trade union IG Metall confirmed earlier media reports of the plans, citing information given to employees by the works council.

The works council described the plans as unrealistic and said there would be considerable resistance to them.

If the company does implement them, employees could lose an estimated 10% of their purchasing power by the end of the decade.

The news comes after VW decided in September to cut 50,000 jobs worldwide in the coming years in addition to the reduction of 50,000 positions that had already been agreed.

Four plants in Germany are also at risk of being closed between 2031 and 2034.

Less than a third of leadership positions in Germany are held by women, a lower level than the EU average, statistics released on Monday showed.

According to the Federal Statistical Office (Destatis), citing figures from the EU statistics authority Eurostat, women held just 30.3% of leadership positions in Germany, compared with 35.9% in the whole of the EU.

Only Cyprus, Italy and the Netherlands have lower rates than Germany in the bloc, while Sweden has the highest, at 44.4%, followed by Latvia, Estonia and Poland.

"The low proportion of women in leadership positions in Germany is particularly striking, as almost as many women as men are employed here," Destatis said, comparing the situation with Italy, which has almost the same level of female leadership but a much lower rate of female employment overall.

Destatis defines leadership positions as those involving the management of small companies, the management of large companies or departments within them and high administrative posts.

A two-day conference entitled "Invest in Germany Summit" is to begin on Monday evening in Berlin as the government seeks to shore up public-sector spending with private money.

Chancellor Friedrich Merz will deliver the opening speech at the conference, which will be attended by an expected 200 representatives from large German and international companies.

The CEO of the US chip manufacturer Nvidia, Jensen Huang, is to be one of the key guests at the conference.

The German government is looking to cooperate strongly with the company, whose products are central to the boom in artificial intelligence, in a bid to help Germany catch up in the sector.

The German Economic Institute (IW) has, however, warned against any too high expectations from the summit, saying that high costs for labor, energy and bureaucracy in Germany were a deterrent to investment in the country.

"It would be naive to believe that the summit will change that in the short term. Companies don't choose Germany because of a promotional event. What matters is whether they can make money here," IW director Michael Hüther told the DPA news agency.

He said it would be more effective if the government were to swiftly implement its reform agenda.

A study by Allianz Research has shown that investment in Germany fell by 6.4% between 2019 and 2025, while it grew by 6.7% in the rest of the eurozone and a whole 20.2% in the US.

The DW newsroom in Bonn bids guten Tag to all its faithful readers at the start of a new week!

And the Leipzig/Halle airport has resumed normal operations after a security incident involving a breached fence triggered a large-scale police operation.

#683. Bulgaria seeks suspects in Germany after Starlink sabotage

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A Bulgarian court has issued arrest warrants for two young German nationals suspected of setting fire to Starlink antennas. The case is reminiscent of another in Poland, which Warsaw says was orchestrated by Russia.

Bulgaria on Monday issued European arrest warrants against two 19-year-old German nationals, saying it suspected their involvement in the sabotage of a series of Starlink antennas near the capital Sofia.

"The evidence gathered makes it possible to conclude that there are reasonable grounds to suspect the involvement of the two defendants," the court in Sofia rules, granting a request from prosecutors.

Ukraine's military uses Starlink's military-grade satellite internet service Starshield for battlefield communications amid Russia's invasion.

Bulgaria does not directly border Ukraine, but it is on the Black Sea, one of the flashpoints of the conflict. A similar case in Poland, which does directly border Ukraine, led to a total of four arrests last week, also in Germany.

The two teenage suspects are accused of deliberately setting fire to the antennas on the evening of October 9.

Of 18 in a mountainous region south of Sofia, three were destroyed entirely, two were partially burned and three more were damaged, according to prosecutors.

Prosecutors say the suspects entered from Serbia in a car with German license plates, claiming to be tourists, staying just a few hours around the time of the alleged crime.

Plamen Tonchev, the head of Bulgaria's domestic intelligence agency DANS, said he was working on the assumption that it was deliberate sabotage and said other such acts could not be ruled out in future.

News of the arrests follow just a week after a total of four people were arrested in Germany, accused of sabotaging a Starlink satellite communications station in central Poland in late September.

German police detained three resident men and a woman, two of them Turkish nationals, early last week.

Polish officials said they believed that Russia was behind the sabotage attempt, with Prime Minister Donald Tusk calling it an act of "hybrid warfare."

The domestic intelligence agency also claimed that Russia had cooperated with organized crime elements in Germany on the alleged operation.

Meanwhile, in the Baltic, Lithuania's prime minister on Sunday said that Russia was likely behind an attempted arson attack on a company hosting communication hardware for Starlink satellites.

#642. Germany news: Hackers release AfD party emails

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Germany's far-right AfD has launched an inquiry after the release of thousands of emails following a cyberattack. Also, investors pledged billions for Germany at a major summit in Berlin. DW has more news from Germany.

This blog is now closed. Below is a round-up of top stories from and about Germany on Tuesday, October 20, 2026.

Alice Weidel, the co-leader of Germany's far-right AfD, is expected to present her party's policy for the EU at the European Parliament on Wednesday.

According to a report from news outlet Politico, the AfD policy paper on the EU does not seek to exit the bloc or the eurozone at first.

The party rather wants to downsize the EU's powers and budget.

Instead of direct European Parliament elections, the AfD would like to see delegates appointed by national parliaments. The AfD would also limit the scope of the European Commission to purely administrative tasks and supplant the European Court of Justice with an arbitration tribunal.

The AfD would threaten to slash Germany's contributions to the EU budget.

Germany under the AfD would seek exemptions from EU regulations regarding issues such as migration and social policy. The AfD may also seek opt-outs of other areas such as agriculture and financial policies.

If the EU would not bend to the AfD's demands for reform, the party would then seek a German referendum on EU membership.

A referendum would be unrealistic, as it would require a constitutional amendment to the German Basic Law to take place. An amendment would require a two-thirds majority vote in both the Bundestag and upper house Bundesrat — an extremely high threshold to meet.

The AfD hopes that it can implement its EU policies in 2029, when the next German and European elections will take place.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Recent German federal election surveys show the AfD in first place but it would have to form a coalition with another partner to establish a ruling majority government. That's difficult when other parties such as the conservative Christian Democratic Union refuse to have any cooperation with the AfD as part of a "firewall" policy.

More than 421,000 emails linked to Germany's far-right AfD have been published following a cyberattack (see additional entry below).

The alleged material includes private communications, sensitive personal data and reported references to racism, antisemitism and Nazi ideology, although much of the trove has not yet been independently verified.

Could this become one of the biggest political data leaks in modern German history?

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

A German supermarket is getting attention online after responding to a customer who objected to its diverse workforce.

Nine pro-Palestinian protesters seeking an end to Western arms exports to Israel glued themselves to the ground in front of a Berlin facility belonging to German weapons manufacturer Rheinmetall.

Berlin police responded to the incident at 5:30 a.m. (0330 UTC) with 11 protesters at the site. The "Peacefully Against Genocide" group was behind the action.

The factory began arms production in June. It is involved in the production of shell casings for artillery ammunition and parts for kamikaze drones, according to local public broadcaster RBB.

It is unclear how much the facility in Berlin's Wedding neighborhood is involved with weapons for Israel. Rheinmetall, however, is Germany's largest arms manufacturer.

In the western German state of Hesse, seven people also blocked the entry to the Rheinmetall facility in the city of Kassel, according to local police.

In addition, demonstrators against weapons exports glued themselves in front of a weapons company in the western city of Kaiserslautern.

Germany's far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) said on Tuesday that it was looking into the theft and subsequent online publication of emails stolen by unidentified hackers during a cyberattack last month.

An AfD spokesperson said the hackers had targeted a hosting provider used by the party and appeared to have copied data from a small number of mostly inactive email accounts.

"The recent online publication of what is presumably a portion of this data is currently being investigated," the spokesperson said. "A preliminary assessment can only be provided once this investigation has been concluded."

Although the spokesperson did not say how much data was stolen, an earlier report by the mass-circulation daily Bild put the number of AfD-linked emails published at more than 420,000.

According to Bild, the material published included not only emails sent by party figures such as co-leader Alice Weidel but also bank statements, academic certificates and membership lists.

The website hosting the material was offline by Tuesday afternoon.

However, archived versions showed a volume of 281 gigabytes of data under the headline "The party's own records, unedited."

The emails and other materials published have not yet been certified as authentic by any independent body.

The AfD, several state chapters of which have been classified as "confirmed right-wing extremist" by regional branches of Germany's domestic intelligence agency, has been topping recent national opinion polls and made gains in recent state elections.

Some politicians and civil society groups have called for it to be banned, arguing that the party is a threat to democracy, an accusation the party has rejected.

While Berlin hosts a summit on investment in Germany, an increasing amount of US capital is flowing into Germany's traditional industrial firms and family-owned businesses.

As many business owners struggle to find successors, more companies are becoming acquisition targets.

Read all the details here: Why US investors are snapping up Germany's famed Mittelstand

German economic confidence — like that across the eurozone in general — deteriorated in October, according to experts at the Mannheim-based Center for European Economic Research (ZEW).

The ZEW's monthly economic confidence index fell to a three-month low on Tuesday, down to 28.1 from 34.7 in September. That is nearly two points lower than forecast.

"Private consumption and investment remain weak because of high energy prices: They put real incomes under pressure, increase production costs and keep inflation higher for longer," said ZEW President Achim Wambach.

"The German economy," added Wambach, "is slowly emerging from stagnation, but the recovery is not self-sustaining."

The biggest losers in this latest index were banks, insurers, the service sector and the telecommunications industry.

Winners were seen in the metal manufacturing and electronics industries.

ZEW compiles its monthly surveys from interviews with banking, insurance and financial industry experts and businesses on their expectations for economic and capital market developments.

The ZEW's index is considered a key indicator for Germany's economic development.

Tuesday's survey was based on interviews with 178 analysts between October 12 and 19, 2026.

Chancellor Friedrich Merzhas indulged in an abundance of the most common cliches about his country and compatriots while trying to portray Germany as the ideal place to do business at an investment summit.

"Germans are hardworking, going to bed early, rising before dawn," he told the domestic and international guests at the Invest in Germany Summit.

"Worst traffic is in the morning between 6 and 7," he said.

"On holidays, we are first on the beach, spreading towels before breakfast. If we agree on delivery dates, we stick to it. If you invite friends from Germany for dinner at 7, we will arrive at 6:58.

"We are reliable, predictable, precise, and unsurprising," the chancellor continued. "We are, in short, what investors like most."

Finance Minister Lars Klingbeil also used blunt humor in his appeal to potential investors, saying in his speech simply: "I want your money."

Both pitches appear to have succeeded, with investment commitments worth billions made at the summit.

More than €225 billion ($252.5 billion) has so far been pledged in investments in Germany at a summit in Berlin attended by top business leaders, German Economy Minister Katherina Reiche said on Tuesday.

Reiche did not give any details about the commitments made so far, but told broadcaster Deutschlandfunk that the figure only included deals above a volume of €100 million.

Among those planning to invest in Europe's largest economy is online retail giant Amazon, which said it intended to pour some €16 billion into Germany next year for various schemes.

The company said these would include "the expansion of data centers and AI infrastructure, high-speed satellite internet through Amazon Leo, new logistics projects across the country, and a major new production slate by Prime Video."

"Germany is a hub of innovation and one of the most attractive investment opportunities in Europe," David Zapolsky, Amazon's chief global affairs and legal officer, was quoted as saying in a statement.

"That is why we have invested more than €100 billion here since 2010, and why we plan to invest an additional €16 billion in 2027. We want German businesses, communities, and creators to have the infrastructure and tools they need to compete and grow."

More than 200 German and international executives from large corporations, investment banks and funds are attending the gathering, entitled the Invest in Germany Summit.

The Left Party in Berlin, which has faced accusations of being antisemitic, is holding a meeting to discuss how to deal with its parliamentarian Vanessa Emde, who has raised controversy with pro-Palestinian statements.

Among other things, Emde has supported the idea that there is no right to exist for states, but only for people — thus seemingly going against Germany's firm support of Israel's right to existence.

Emde has refused to retract any of her statements.

She has also criticized a statement on antisemitism that she had signed along with all other members of the Left's parliamentary party in which the signatories committed to upholding Israel's right to existence.

This comes as both the Berlin Green Party and the Social Democrats (SPD) confirmed that they were ready to begin coalition negotiations with the Left Party on forming a joint government in the city-state.

The Left Party is due to decide on the issue on October 31 at a party conference.

The three parties held preliminary discussions on the accusations of antisemitism and links to organized crime leveled at the Left Party before beginning the exploratory talks that precede final coalition negotiations.

The Left Party emerged from September 20 elections in Berlin as the strongest party with 25.7% of the vote.

Both the Greens (14.3%) and the center-left SPD (12.1%) came in behind the conservative Christian Democrats (18.8%), who led a previous coalition in Berlin with the SPD.

The far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) received 16.3% of the vote, but so far, all other parties are officially refusing to enter into any form of cooperation with it at either federal or state level.

The Invest in Germany Summit in Berlin entered its second and final day on Tuesday after opening the previous evening with a speech by Chancellor Friedrich Merz that included the words: "Germany is open for business."

The summit has already yielded an agreement to deepen cooperation on artificial intelligence (AI) in German industry between Germany and US tech giant Nvidia.

Merz and Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang said the combination of Germany's engineering expertise and cutting-edge AI technology would help strengthen the country's economy over the long term.

More than 200 German and international executives representing large corporations, investment banks and funds are attending the event, with closed-door talks scheduled for Tuesday.

The summit aims to attract foreign investment to Germany. Organizers highlight the country's sound public finances, triple-A credit rating as the only G7 nation to hold one, strong rule of law and leading position in European patent filings.

Despite these advantages, Germany has struggled to attract foreign investment in recent years, with high energy prices and labor costs acting as deterrents.

Government sources say investment will be vital to help bridge gaps in public funding as the country seeks to improve infrastructure and support companies' transition to climate-neutral production.

The summit is also being attended by Economy Minister Katherina Reiche and Finance Minister Lars Klingbeil, who also serves as Merz's deputy.

Nine protesters have glued themselves to the road outside a plant run by defense contractor Rheinmetall in Berlin's Wedding district, police said on Tuesday.

Officers have been trying since the early morning to detach the group, a police spokeswoman said.

She said as many as 90 officers had been deployed to the site, where altogether 11 protesters were gathered.

Rheinmetall is Germany's largest weapons manufacturer.

The plant in Wedding, where production began earlier this year, has been the target of several protests, with climate and social justice activist Greta Thunberg among those participating.

German industrial giant Siemens has warned against eliminating entry-level jobs that are now increasingly left to artificial intelligence (AI), saying young workers could be left without essential skills, including those needed to monitor AI-generated work.

Judith Wiese, the company's chief people and sustainability officer, said that handing certain routine tasks to AI meant that young employees would not gain hands-on experience that could benefit them later in their careers.

Wiese said it was necessary, for example, for software developers to be able to write their own code so they could critically examine the code produced by AI.

"The crucial question is always: What do I need to have done, checked and seen myself to avoid being misled by AI?" she said.

However, Wiese said AI could also help employees acquire skills more quickly, while older colleagues could learn from younger ones who had grown up using AI.

Wiese said it was still too early to say how AI would impact employment.

Noting that Siemens has some 30,000 software engineers, she said: "You can use the additional productivity either to do the same work with fewer people or to do more with the same number of employees."

Wiese also pointed out that AI was speeding up work processes, enabling the exploration of new ideas and creating projects that would not have been possible without it.

DW''s newsroom in Bonn bids a warm guten Tag to all its readers as a previously balmy October decides to move toward more wintry chill.

As Chancellor Merz hosts an investment conference in Berlin where developments in AI is taking center stage, industrial giant Siemens has warned that the technology does not only bring advantages.

The controversy over alleged antisemitism in Berlin's Left Party, which might well soon lead a leftist coalition in the city-state, continues, with the party itself debating what to do about a member who has expressed strong anti-Israel views.

And people in Germany are likely to face much longer waits for doctors' appointments from next year, according to an organization managing family doctors and therapists across Germany.

#631. Rising emissions and temperatures: The cost of Trump's climate retreat is now clear

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The world is set to breach the critical 1.5-degree temperature limit, with Donald Trump's US withdrawal from climate action worsening the outlook, according to a UN report. But there is a small silver lining.

For over a decade, scientists have warned the world must limit global warming to 1.5 degrees Celsius (2.7 Fahrenheit) to avoid the most severe consequences of climate change.

Decades of "insufficient" action on cutting the fossil fuel emissions heating the planet means overshooting 1.5-degree temperature threshold is now "imminent and unavoidable," according to the United Nations Environment Program's (UNEP) annual Emissions Gap Report.

If current climate policies continue, temperatures will rise by an average of 2.8 degrees Celsius above pre-industrial levels within this century. But even if countries meet their new climate pledges, global warming is still projected to reach 2.6 degrees Celsius. Both scenarios would tip the world into a period of catastrophic weather extremes.

The projections reflect a gap between what governments promise to do and the action they are taking to curb emissions. Some 167 countries have submitted climate action plans to the UN, covering 83% of global greenhouse gas emissions. But those pledges to cut emissions fall far short of what is needed to limit warming.

Among the world's largest emitters, Indonesia, China and the United States saw emissions rise in 2025, according to preliminary estimates. The increase in the US "far exceeded that of all other countries," driven by a resurgence of coal — the dirtiest fossil fuel source — and rising electricity demand, partly linked to massive AI data center expansion.

The outlook has worsened in part because of US President Donald Trump's withdrawal of the United States from the historic Paris climate accord in which the world came together in 2015 to agree to halt climate change. The US is the second-largest annual emitter of climate-warping greenhouse gases.

Since Trump entered the Oval Office for a second time, his administration has taken a sledgehammer to climate policy, gutting environmental regulations and expanding oil and gas production.

"The main takeaway is simple — governments need to get on with enacting policies that cut fossil fuel use as soon as possible," said Bill Hare, CEO of the global science and policy institute Climate Analytics, of the report.

Exceeding 1.5 degrees Celsius even temporarily sets humanity on a dangerous course, as it increases the risk of triggering potentially irreversible or difficult-to-undo "tipping points," like the loss of coral reefs, long-term retreat of ice sheets, and the Amazon Rainforest turning into a grassland.

As ecosystems disappear and sea levels rise, these changes could trigger a deadly chain reaction, releasing more greenhouse gases and accelerating warming, dramatically increasing the likelihood of deadly extreme weather.

This summer alone, Europe recorded more than 32,000 excess deaths related to heat and nearly 1,500 people died in a flash floods in Nepal and Tibet, after climate change laid the groundwork for a glacier to collapse.

But scientists maintain 1.5 degrees Celsius is not a magic number, and that every fraction of a degree of warming brings escalating risks. To reduce the risk of future damage, countries should still focus on rapidly transitioning away from fossil fuels, the largest contributor to global climate change, and toward renewable energy like solar and wind.

Now the best-case scenario is overshooting 1.5 degrees Celsius for a short time before bringing temperatures back down quickly.

"Overshoot is not surrender," UN Secretary-General Antonio Guterres said in a press statement. "We can still keep it as small and as short as possible — and bring temperatures back below 1.5 degrees before the end of the century."

UNEP lays out a scenario in which the world sees warming peak at 1.7 to 1.8 degrees Celsius around mid-century, before falling back to 1.5 Celsius by 2100. Getting there would require steep emissions cuts of 69% by 2040, relative to 2025 levels.

It would also require removing 330 billion metric tons of carbon dioxide from the atmosphere and storing it in the ground. But recent studies have cast doubt on the feasibility and costs of the technology, as well as the nascent carbon removal industry's ability to scale as quickly as required to meet climate goals.

The UN warns any delays will make the challenge harder, saying that every five years that emissions remain at current levels adds about 0.1 Celsius to projected peak warming.

"We must pull out all the stops to minimize peak warming and return to 1.5 Celsius by the end of the century," Inger Andersen, Executive Director of UNEP, said in a press release. "This is possible."

Still, climate policies have proven effective in reducing emissions. The UNEP report draws on more than 200 studies that analyze how measures like carbon taxes, government climate targets, building codes and subsidies for low-carbon technologies are working.

Carbon-pricing schemes have been the most widely studied so far. China's pilot schemes reduced emissions by about 11%, while the European Union's emissions trading system saw an 8% cut, compared with projected levels without these policies.

"There are more opportunities than ever to accelerate action," according to the report, pointing to the boom in renewable energy since the adoption of the Paris Agreement a decade ago.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Global renewable electricity capacity has grown three times faster than anticipated, and solar build-out grew seven times faster. In 2024, more than 90% of renewable projects were cheaper than fossil fuel alternatives.

While total energy supply from coal, oil and gas has continued to rise, that's happened at a slower rate than was projected in 2015. And coal use is likely to peak and decline by the end of the decade.

"We have a wealth of success stories showing that emissions can be cut quickly," Andersen said. "It is now up to nations to urgently put in place policies that spark fast, deep and sustained emissions reductions, and put the world on track for a more stable climate and more stable economies and societies."

#552. Germany news: AfD's Weidel calls for major EU reforms

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Far-right Alternative for Germany co-leader Alice Weidel reveals the party's plan to reform the EU. Meanwhile, Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang was in Berlin to talk to developers and investors. DW has more.

One of Weidel's reforms is for European parliamentarians to no longer be directly elected

Nvidia's Jensen Huang had words of encouragement for Europe's growing AI economy during a conference in Berlin

Germany's Finance Ministry has erroneously introduced an approved tax hike on tobacco products months before it was scheduled to come into effect

Germany's Bundesbank has forecast growth for the fourth quarter after a dip in the third quarter

This blog is now closed. Below is a round-up of top stories from and about Germany on Wednesday, October 21, 2026.

We'll be back with the latest headlines out of the country on Thursday. Tschüss!

Alice Weidel, the co-leader of the Alternative for Germany (AfD) party, has presented her party's proposals for a European Union that she said was "in severe need of reform" during a visit to the European Parliament in Strasbourg, saying they rested on three core pillars.

"We would like to rebuild and reform the European Union," she said. "We would like to found the European Union on three major principles: the first one is subsidiarity, the second one is responsibility, and the third one is sovereignty."

On subsidiarity — the idea that power should reside as close to the grassroots level as possible as much as possible — she said it was important to put the "competences" on the "lowest level of decision-making," saying "we don't want the state, or the [European] Commission — that is not elected — to intrude and disturb your private life in decision-making."

"I'm an economist, and for me, the European Union is not a group of sovereign states any more," Weidel said. She said that in sovereign states' parliaments, leaders were elected, "and so the decision-making on the sovereign level should be made in the national parliaments and not here, to be very clear."

This is already the case in the vast majority of core EU public policy areas. National governments decide foreign and defense issues, finance and health policy, education policy and much more. The notable exceptions Weidel objects to are fiscal policy and the primacy of EU law, but only after sovereign member states agree to them.

She said the European Commission was not elected but had a "legislative right of initiative," saying this was a "huge disturbance of checks and balances" and of a "democratic decision-making process."

Weidel accused the EU of failing to follow its own rules, for instance on sovereign debt and whether the European Central Bank should or can guarantee it, citing issues like this as questions of responsibility.

Finally, she reiterated her desire for "sovereign states to protect their borders," saying that "if the European Union is not capable of protecting our borders, the outer borders ... then it is a decision of the sovereign states to protect our national borders."

"If Schengen is over, if Schengen doesn't work, then we need to get out of Schengen and protect our borders," she said of the open-border zone within much of the EU.

She said that if the EU fails to protect the bloc's external borders, "we should have a veto right to opt out of this chaos."

While the EU maintains a border management agency, Frontex, national authorities are responsible for the external borders of the Schengen zone and the EU.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz criticized China for "unfair practices" in trade, and said he wants EU member states to balance out their trade deficits.

That elicited a bold comparison from a journalist who said, "You sound very much like Donald Trump."

A massive stage, all in black. A colorful laser light show. Upbeat music. AI-generated videos on a gigantic screen. On stage, just one man in a black leather jacket: Jensen Huang, the 63-year-old CEO of Nvidia, which manufactures superfast computer chips needed for AI data centers and happens to be the world's most valuable company.

The relaxed Nvidia boss set out his vision of the upcoming AI revolution and, of course, the products Nvidia has developed for the "second wave" of AI. The 4,000 attendees at Berlin's Tempodrom arena were thrilled by what Jensen Huang had to say (see entry below).

Huang said Germany and Europe could be part of the next industrial revolution — not as developers of AI language models or computer chips, but as producers of robots, machines, tools, and entire factories controlled by AI applications.

"You have to transfer AI into the real world of production," he said. Europe could use its data to train AI and develop customized models for automakers, pharmaceutical companies, and many small and medium-sized businesses.

Unsurprisingly, Nvidia offers all the AI software and hardware needed to get in on the action. A new addition to the Nvidia universe is a "Giga-Factory," which is housed in modular shipping containers and can be set up in just a few days or weeks anywhere with a suitable power grid.

There was also something for consumers: a high-performance laptop with a built-in AI agent that acts as a "friend and helper," capable of taking over tasks and programming new agents. Huang went on to demonstrate four small robot ducks that had been trained by this AI agent to perform cute tricks, which were met with laughter and applause.

But it wasn't all cheers. A woman in the audience interrupted a product demo to point out that AI is also used to control Russian drones. She was escorted out by security guards, but Huang assured the audience that he would speak with her later. He was keen to add that Nvidia has also developed a security system designed to keep AI agents in check and prevent misuse.

Nvidia is now valued at €5 trillion ($5.6 trillion) on the stock market. That is roughly equivalent to the total economic output of all of Germany in 2025.

The German military, known as the Bundeswehr, has rated the threat of sabotage against it as "significant," according to a reports by the Süddeutsche Zeitung (SZ) and public broadcaster WDR.

A spokesperson for the Defense Ministry pointed to several recent cases, including the discovery of a drone close to an airbase in Wunstorf and acts of sabotage against Navy ships.

The military is looking to increase security to counter the threat, with the ministry spokesperson naming increased surveillance at airports and military docks by soldiers or infrared cameras.

"The incidents highlight the ability that Russia has almost certainly had for a while now, as well as very likely the increased intention, to act against Bundeswehr forces and facilities, as well as partners such as Ukraine in Germany," SZ reported based on internal military analysis.

The aim is therefore "very likely an intention to degrade the current political and society-wide German support for Ukraine."

The ministry spokesperson also pointed out that the incidents did not mark a major change, with the military facing similar challenges over the past decades.

He said "automatic defense concepts" are in place for acute terror threats, but "we are not at that point."

German trade with Russia collapsed following the 2022 full-scale invasion of Ukraine, especially due to the decision to cut all imports of Russian gas. However, some trade has continued.

According to the German Federal Statistical Office (Destatis), German imports of Russian goods were down by 96.4% in the first half of 2026 compared with the same period in 2021.

This amounted to €500 million ($561 million) in 2026, down from €15 billion. German exports to Russia were also down, but still significant, reaching €3.4 billion in the first six months of 2026. However, this was about a quarter of the exports in the same period in 2021.

Pharmaceutical products made up more than 40% of German exports to Russia, with machinery, food and animal feed also being exported in smaller quantities.

Jensen Huang, the head of chip manufacturing giant Nvidia, was speaking at a company conference for developers and investors in Berlin's Tempodrom auditorium on Wednesday.

He was seeking to encourage Europe to build out its data center infrastructure and touted Nvidia's modular tools to help speed up that process.

"Europe's AI economy is starting, and it's accelerating, and it's great to see," Huang said.

He promoted a new design for a ready-made modular computing center that comes in a box akin to a shipping container and that could make it possible to construct a data center in a matter of days. Huang said it "will change the way people build AI centers."

Nvidia has invested $10 billion (€8.9 billion) in European startups over the past year, the Nvidia boss said.

Europe has fallen behind the US and China in terms of building up data centers and is currently home to around 5% of the world's AI computing capacity in comparison to 75% in the US. This capacity is required to train AI models and its lack is seen as a major obstacle for European developers.

Most current data centers are reliant on Nvidia GPUs to run.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

A spokesperson for Germany's Federal Financial Ministry on Wednesday addressed confusion surrounding the implementation of a new tobacco tax saying, "The tax increase in the law's wording was approved to go into effect on January 1, 2027, that remains the case."

The spokesperson confirmed that the mix-up over when the new law would take effect resulted from a technical error that put the law on the books immediately rather than January 1 as intended.

The issue stemmed from a mistake in the government's original writing of the law.

Customs officials have reportedly been informed and the Financial Ministry's website makes clear that the price increase will not be charged until the beginning of the new year.

"Gen Z regelt" — or Gen Z's Got It — a youth initiative, has called for mass protests in 21 cities across Germany on Saturday, October 31; including Berlin, Cologne, Hamburg and Munich.

Thousands are expected to participate in demonstrations protesting "broken schools and austerity," as well as the cost of living, social injustice, military conscription and climate change.

French protests that exploded in violence and rampant destruction of private and public property this weekend also grew out of student frustration over the national school system and soon included numerous unions, groups and individuals voicing anger over a number of issues.

Gen Z regelt's website clearly states that the initiative is "against all violence," whether directed at people or property.

Police say they are "vigilant and relaxed," adding that they intend to protect people's right to congregate and express their opinions.

Still, images of massive damage and public violence from France, Italy and Spain have raised concerns among domestic policymakers and police, who have warned against allowing the protests to be hijacked by left-wing extremists.

Andreas Roßkopf, head of the German Federal Police's GdP union, made clear that officers would deal harshly with riots and rioters, telling the Rheinische Post newspaper on Wednesday, "State, but also federal police, will offer a firm and decisive response" to any trouble.

Roßkopf also warned that radicals and extremists could insert themselves among demonstrators in order to "attack police" or "simply be destructive."

The Bundesbank, Germany's central bank, has said it is expecting to see an uptick in growth for Europe's biggest economy in the fourth quarter.

"German economic output is likely to improve again markedly in the fourth quarter," the bank said in its monthly report on Wednesday. "However, the future economic recovery depends, not least, on the development of the war in the Middle East."

The optimistic outlook follows a dip in the third quarter, which the bank chalked up largely to this summer's drought that caused such low water levels in the Rhine River, an artery in western Germany for industry, that most ships could not travel.

"The low water levels are also likely to continue to have a negative impact on foreign trade via European ports as a result of restrictions on inland water transport," the bank wrote.

The forecast will be welcomed by the Merz government as it has struggled to boost the economy after two consecutive years of recession followed by weak growth in 2025.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz has said he does not think Europe is on course for a trade war with China while acknowledging what he called "unfair practice" on the part of Beijing.

Speaking at a business conference in Berlin, Merz said the imbalance did not mean "the beginning of a war, of a trade war with China. We are looking for peaceful solutions, but the level for that is fair trade."

The German chancellor has been pushing for the EU to be better prepared for trade disputes with China, including calling for a means to quickly exclude non-EU countries from the EU's single market if they flood it with cheap goods.

Merz said the EU needs to be able "to protect our markets and defend ourselves against those who don't play by the rules."

"Anyone who disrupts the functioning of the markets through unfair subsidies, overcapacities, aggressive pricing, or artificially undervalued currencies must expect a response," he said.

That includes tools that "enable us to respond swiftly and vigorously to targeted attacks on our industry and supply chain, with the idea being that in the best case we will not have to use it."

Germany has a rather generous parental allowance system in comparison to many countries, with parents being able to take off 14 months, if each parent takes at least two months off, with the government paying up to €1,800 (just over $2,000) a month during that period.

However, this system has become the subject of a planned reform under the government of Chancellor Friedrich Merz.

The plan is to reduce the maximum amount of full-time paid parental leave to 12 months. With three months each reserved for both parents and the remaining six months free to be divided up however the parents choose. It also raises the minimum payment from €300 to €330 and the maximum from €1,800 to €1,900.

The government's aim is to make cuts to help the Ministry for Family Affairs reach its savings target of €1.6 billion, while also encouraging more fathers to take parental leave. Currently, fewer than half of fathers in Germany take parental leave.

Now the Ifo Institute for Economic Research has cast doubts on the government's savings plan.

"The savings target and the goal of increasing fathers’ participation in the planned parental allowance reform are at odds with each other. The higher the number of fathers who are motivated to participate by the reform, the less the government saves," Ifo researcher Kyra Puteanus said.

The institute estimates savings of around €880 million from November 2027, when the reform comes into effect. However, if more fathers, who generally earn more and thus are able to request the highest payment, do take up the parental leave, this will quickly reduce the savings, with Ifo predicting that even if the uptake by fathers only increases by 1%, savings will fall to €597 million. An increase of 3.1% would undo any savings from the reform, according to the institute.

"Since 2010, however, the share of fathers receiving parental allowance has almost doubled. A rapid increase can therefore not be ruled out, especially if months are otherwise forfeited," Ifo researcher Franziska Nuernbergk said.

The Israel Defense Forces (IFD) said in a post on X that they have killed Tamer Mohammad Ibrahim Al-Zabaq, a member of the Hamas terror group who was involved in the October 7 attacks.

The post said that al-Zabaq had been one of the men responsible for the abduction of Shani Louk, a dual German-Israeli national.

Louk was initially thought to have been taken hostage following the Hamas-led attacks but it was later confirmed that she had been killed.

In the immediate aftermath of the terror attacks, pictures and videos shared online showed a young woman, believed to be Shani, lying face down in a pick-up truck driven by militants. It was unclear from the footage whether she was alive.

The IDF also accused Al-Zabaq of operating to "restore Hamas' military capabilities, in systematic violation of the ceasefire."

"The terrorist was eliminated in order to remove the threat he posed," the IDF said.

The claims in the X post could not be independently verified.

Israel has carried out numerous deadly strikes on Gaza since the ceasefire came into effect just over a year ago.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz, addressing the Business Global Dialogue summit in Berlin on Wednesday, renewed calls for US President Donald Trump to invite South Africa (a G20 member) to the upcoming G20 summit that he will host in Miami this December.

"Whoever talks about the future of the global economy must include all important partners. Of course Africa belongs at the table," he said, adding: "The African Union is a G20 member, as is South Africa. That is why I urgently insist that South Africa be represented at the G20 summit."

Merz made a similar plea last year, when Trump angrily insisted that he would not invite South Africa to the annual summit of the 20 leading industrial and emerging economies.

Trump has accused South Africa of committing genocide on white farmers and dismissed South African pushback against his repeated claim. The anti-immigrant president also took the unusual step of offering South Africans political asylum in the US — as long as they are white.

The US President is apparently less bothered by wars of aggression, extending an invitation to President Vladimir Putin to come to Miami, despite Russia being thrown out of the G8 — now G7 — over its 2014 invasion and annexation of Ukraine's Crimea.

Beyond his plea for South Africa's inclusion, Merz said he would travel to the US with what he called a "Miami Growth Agenda" aimed at slashing bureaucracy, pushing structural reforms and making investment easier.

"Those" he said, "are the prerequisites for new economic growth and prosperity, not only in Germany, but globally."

Russian FSB agents have arrested a German national, accusing him of being a mercenary under contract with the Ukrainian army.

"The Federal Security Service of the Russian Federation has detained a German citizen who is a serving member of the Armed Forces of Ukraine," said a statement cited by Russian news agencies.

The FSB says the man, whom they described as a sniper, previously fought in Afghanistan and Syria as well as in Ukraine between 2016 and 2018.

They say he arrived in Ukraine last November and signed a three-year contract with the Ukrainian army before deserting over unpaid wages.

Last week, the FSB arrested a Swedish national whom Russian authorities also accused of fighting on Kyiv's behalf.

Russia has arrested hundreds of foreign nationals that it accuses of "espionage," "sabotage" or "treason" to long prison sentences since it began its invasion of neighboring Ukraine.

A number of foreign nationals have also been jailed over supposed "mercenary activity."

#526. Can Germany revive its economy before the far-right AfD gains ground?

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Conflicts, rising costs and Chinese competition are testing Germany's economic model like never before. Can new growth sectors create jobs and ease voters' economic fears, thereby helping contain the far-right AfD?

Germany's export-led economic model faces unprecedented strain just as support for the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) party reaches a new peak.

Chinese rivals are steadily eroding the market share of major German industrial players, while persistently high energy prices, elevated labor costs and excessive red tape are adding to the structural crisis.

After two years of contraction, real GDP edged up only 0.2% last year, according to the Federal Statistical Office, Destatis. Exports to China fell 9.7% in 2025, withGerman vehicle and part shipments plunging by a third.

While there are signs the German economy is turning the corner, the government's latest forecast of 1.3% growth in 2026 is still modest compared with the pre-COVID boom years.

The AfD is currently polling ahead of all other political parties in Germany. The country's many economic challenges have been a major factor in the right-wing populists' rise.

Germany's domestic intelligence agency sees reliable indications that the AfD is right-wing extremist in its entirety.

The party has vowed to put Germany back on a growth path by ending the transition to renewable energies, lowering taxes and renegotiating European Union treaties, while also reforming welfare and sharply restricting immigration.

As Chancellor Friedrich Merz's coalition searches for a fresh growth strategy to fend off the rise in populist politics, the government has set out its high-tech agenda focusing on six priority sectors.

These are artificial intelligence (AI), quantum computing, microelectronics, biotechnology, clean energy (including nuclear fusion) and climate-neutral mobility.

Irene Bertschek, head of the Digital Economy research unit at the Leibniz Center for European Economic Research (ZEW), argues that Germany's research in these "key enabling technologies is very strong, particularly in the area of climate-neutral technologies."

Yet, the results are often commercialized abroad, Bertschek told DW, with the United States leading in AI and China closing the gap quickly.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

A report published Monday by the Made for Germany initiative — a group of 147 companies and investors — builds on the government's plan, estimating that these and other new technologies could attract up to €4.1 trillion ($4.6 trillion) in investment by 2035.

Realizing that potential, however, will depend less on today's industrial giants, like Volkswagen, Siemens and BASF.

"It will not primarily be the established companies that conquer these new fields, but innovative newcomers — startups," Oliver Falck, director of the ifo Center for the Economics of Innovation and Digital Transformation, told DW.

The task, Falck said, is to create the right conditions for startups to emerge, including proximity to top universities, access to private capital and enough freedom to experiment.

He argues for state financing during the early-stage "valley of death" — the risky phase between research and commercial viability when private financing is often scarce.

There are clear signs this shift is already happening. Data from Deutsche Bank and startupdetector, a German startup database and market intelligence platform, show more than 3,000 startups were founded in Germany in the first half of 2026, up 88% from the same period last year. One-third of the new ventures have an AI focus.

Venture-capital investment soared 44% to €5.8 billion in the first half of 2026, with firms such as Neura Robotics and drone manufacturers Stark and Quantum Systems benefiting.

US investors, meanwhile, are increasingly buying up Germany's famed Mittelstand — its traditional small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) — with an eye on their specialized technology.

"The German Mittelstand is highly innovative. Now it is essential to invest in key technologies such as artificial intelligence and to tap into the potential that arises at the intersection with established technologies," Bertschek stressed.

The expert, who is also chair of the German government's Commission of Experts for Research and Innovation (EFI), thinks SMEs should make better use of their huge amounts of production data and collaborate with AI startups to help those young firms grow.

Another idea for accelerating the shift to new high-tech sectors comes from Sven Gabor Janszky, chairman of the Leipzig-based futures-research institute 2b AHEAD. He argues that Germany needs special economic zones to help catch the next wave of economic growth.

"For a few specific technologies, you lower the regulations, you recruit ambitious workers and pump money into these zones," Janszky told DW.

This, he added, would follow the examples of Silicon Valley and Chinese mega-cities Shenzhen and Shanghai's Pudong district, and echoes the conditions that helped rebuild the German economy after World War II.

In Janszky's view, these zones would help Germany position itself as a first mover in emerging fields rather than remaining tied to older industrial strengths.

"EU regulations may try to prohibit this move, but Germany will have to overcome them so we can grow the economy again," he added.

The sharp rise in Germany's defense spending will also help several of the high-tech sectors now being prioritized.

According to the International Institute for Strategic Studies (IISS), the defense budget is expected to reach around €120 billion in 2026 — roughly 2.6% of GDP. The government wants this figure to hit 3.5% by 2029.

The resulting procurement will provide a major boost to the production of drones, sensors, communications systems and other dual-use technologies that have both civilian and military applications.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Yet, with major rival China benefiting from much lower energy and worker costs, along with huge government subsidies, Germany will continue to face similar price disadvantages when exporting its new high-end tech. But that doesn't have to spell failure, said Falck, the ifo expert.

"Take the semiconductor industry as an example. It is probably unrealistic to aim for leadership along the entire value chain. But it is enough to lead at individual choke points," he pointed out.

"And that is exactly where Germany is strong: as a supplier of equipment for state-of-the-art chip fabs and as a supplier to the world's AI gigafactories, with power semiconductors."

In other words, with a first-mover advantage, decades of high-quality production data and a focus on specialized niches, Germany can remain a major force in advanced industrial and engineering technologies.

#393. In Bulgaria, record low voter turnout triggers a November 1 presidential run-off in a poll colored by divisions over Ukraine war

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Low voter turnout has triggered a run-off for the Bulgarian presidency. The vote was colored by strong divisions over Russia's war in Ukraine, and Sofia's relations with the Kremlin.

Record low turnout in Sunday's Bulgarian elections mean voters are expected to head to the polls on November 1 to cast ballots in a presidential run-off.

Polling stations opened at 7 a.m. and closed at 8 p.m. (0500-1800 GMT), with official results expected within 48 hours, but voter turnout was less than 40%, far short of the 50% required.

Voters were choosing between candidates with vastly different views of the war in Ukraine and relations with Moscow and the vote came as drone attacks on ships in the Black Sea off the Bulgarian coast brought home the significance of the conflict in Ukraine for the former Soviet satellite state.

Although the presidency is largely ceremonial, the head of state in Bulgaria can appoint interim governments during political crises, is commander-in-chief of the armed forces and is able to veto legislation.

Frontrunner in the elections is incumbent President Iliana Yotova, who took over the position this year after current Prime Minister Rumen Radev stepped down to take part in the parliamentary elections that led him to power.

Yotova, 62, who surveys show as having 41-47% of the vote, favors dialogue with Moscow as the way to bring peace to Ukraine and opposes military aid to Kyiv.

A win for Yotova would give a boost for Radev, who also advocates dialogue with Moscow. Victory would make Yotova Bulgaria’s first elected female president.

Her main challenger is Andrey Gyurov, a former caretaker prime minister, who is polling at 28-31%.

Gyurov, 50, who is backed by the pro-Western opposition, is an outspoken critic of Radev's foreign policy and strongly supports the Euro-Atlantic ties of Bulgaria, a European Union and NATO member state. As caretaker prime minister, Gyurov signed a security cooperation agreement with Kyiv.

The leader of the pro-Russian far-right Vazrazhdane party, Kostadin Kostadinov, is in third place on 10-13%.

Altogether 27 pairs of candidates for president and vice president are competing in the first round, with nearly half of them openly pro-Kremlin.

After casting her ballot in Sofia, Yotova called for peace, stability and unity, urging an end to divisions among Bulgarians. Her main challenger, Gyurov, voted in Blagoevgrad, calling for a Bulgaria that looks ahead and offers families greater economic security. Turnout was estimated at 15.1% by 3 p.m. local time (1300 GMT), according to polling agency Myara, Bulgarian media reported.

To win outright, a candidate must secure more than half the valid votes, with more than half of eligible voters taking part. Otherwise, the top two candidates advance to a runoff on November 1.

The war in Ukraine triggered by Russia's unprovoked invasion is one of the central themes in the election, particularly after four merchant ships were allegedly hit by drones off Bulgaria's coast this month in suspected spillover from the conflict. The attacks have prompted Bulgaria to seek assistance from its allies to strengthen maritime security.

Satellite internet antennas from the Starlink network — an important part of Ukraine's defense capabilities — were also set on fire near the capital, Sofia.

While Radev has said that the attacks seemed to be to the advantage of Russia and summoned the Russian ambassador, his government has declined to blame the incidents directly on Moscow.

The strikes have nonetheless raised concerns over Russia’s intentions to escalate the conflict.

Rising energy prices and inflation that have caused economic hardship to many in Bulgaria, the EU's poorest member state, are also a main issue addressed by candidates.

Many voters are also looking to stop government corruption, a persistent concern in the country, and hope for a stable government after multiple elections and coalition breakdowns in recent years.

#319. Germany's Merz says erosion of global order means middle powers in Europe and Asia must be more assertive

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

During a visit to Singapore, German Chancellor Friedrich Merz said Europe and Germany want to increase free trade with open competition, reliability and a rules-based order.

This blog is now closed. Below is a roundup of the top stories in Germany on Tuesday, October 27, 2026:

Don't want to miss our real-time updates? Click here to add us as a Preferred Source on Google.

Thank you for joining our coverage of German news on Tuesday.

We look forward to bringing you the latest again on Wednesday in a fresh blog.

On the first day of his farewell tour in China, German President Frank-Walter Steinmeier urged the German business community to act with confidence in the Chinese market.

"When you travel in China, you're amazed at how highly Germany and German technology are still valued here," he said in Hefei. "That's why we can act with confidence in these markets."

Steinmeier did not bring any companies with him to China as part of his delegation, as his final trip was intended to focus on culture and science.

Nevertheless, the first day of the trip in Hefei, the capital of Anhui Province, revolved primarily around the economy. Steinmeier visited the Volkswagen China Technology Company (VCTC). About three years ago, Europe's largest automaker opened a research center for electric mobility there, which today employs 3,000 people.

Johannes Volkmann, a lawmaker accompanying Steinmeier in China, warned that the German economy must not "lose its leadership in innovation."

"I'm taking this call to action with me from here to do more to improve competitive conditions at home," he said, adding that Germany must avoid creating new dependencies "by allowing innovation and value creation to take place only outside Europe."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Steinmeier called for Europe to reposition itself and reorganize its economic instruments with its growing trade deficit with China in mind.

"The trade surplus that China is accumulating naturally causes us concern because the successful model of economic cooperation is becoming unbalanced," he said. "We must strive to ensure that economic cooperation does not come to an end, but that it continues — to the mutual benefit of both sides."

Germany's trade deficit with China amounted to just under €90 billion ($100 billion) in 2025. According to the Federal Statistical Office, China exported goods worth €170.6 billion to Germany, and German exports to China amounted to €81.3 billion.

Across the 27-member EU, the deficit amounted to almost €197 billion in the first half of 2026, according to EU Trade Commissioner Maros Sefcovic.

Additional reporting by Rosalia Romaniec in Hefei.

A German court denied a defense request to end the trial of a former Ukrainian army officer who stands accused of sabotaging the Nord Stream gas pipelines.

Serhii Kuznietsov, 50, went on trial this month at the Hamburg state court, charged with causing an explosion, damaging property, disrupting public services and being an "accomplice to war crimes" by attacking civilian infrastructure.

Prosecutors accuse him of being the ringleader of the destruction, considered one of Europe's biggest acts of post-World War II sabotage. He is alleged to have carried out the explosions together with six others.

His lawyers said the charges should be dismissed, arguing that, as a former soldier, Kuznietsov was obligated by Ukrainian law not to speak for national security reasons and could face severe penalties in Ukraine if he did. They argued that, under German law, anyone who cannot speak on his or her own behalf cannot be convicted.

The Hamburg court ruled Kuznietsov's defense was not limited under German law and that freedom to testify is not limited by a foreign country's conditions of service.

The undersea explosions on September 26, 2022, damaged the Nord Stream 1 pipeline, which brought Russian natural gas to Germany until Moscow cut off supplies in August 2022.

The blasts also damaged the Nord Stream 2 pipeline, which never went into service. Germany suspended the pipeline's certification process shortly before Russia’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022.

Italian authorities detained Kuznietsov last year. He was publicly named in extradition proceedings that led to his transfer to Germany in November.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy has denied allegations that his country was involved in the pipeline blasts.

German carmaker Mercedes-Benz has cut its forecast for 2026, with difficult market conditions in China and higher fourth-quarter cost expectations set to negatively impact profits, the company said on Tuesday.

Markets were quick to take note of the announcement, and Mercedes shares dropped 3.4% in trading on Tuesday morning.

The move makes Mercedes the third big German carmaker to lower its outlook for 2026 after BMW and VW took similar steps.

Mercedes-Benz CEO Ola Kaellenius has called on German employees to work more without pay while also expanding manufacturing operations in Hungary, where he said costs are about 70% lower than in Germany.

Mercedes currently expects an adjusted car profit margin of 2% to 3% in 2026, compared to the 3% to 5% in the previous guidance.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Like many German automakers, Mercedes has faced weak sales in China. German carmakers have struggled in the world's largest car market with sluggish demand and increased competition from local manufacturers.

Mercedes vehicle deliveries in China — last year already at their lowest level since 2016 — fell a further 29% in the nine months to the end of September.

Mercedes also released third-quarter figures earlier than planned, which showed that the company generated just €172 million ($191 million) in free cash flow from its vehicle business, down on the €422 million forecast by financial market experts.

Before interest and tax, Mercedes posted an operating profit of €1.77 billion — more than double the €750 million recorded a year earlier, according to the preliminary figures. But last year's stats were significantly impacted by one-time expenses, especially related to job cuts and legal disputes.

Adjusted for one-off items, the operating profit margin in the passenger car business stood at 2.5%, down on the 4.8% recorded a year earlier.

The German agency responsible for finding a final repository for nuclear waste (BGE) has eliminated five states from its search.

Berlin, Hesse, Rhineland-Palatinate, Saxony and Saarland were deemed unsuitable for long-term radioactive waste storage.

In a report issued Tuesday, the BGE said it had limited its search to 5% of Germany, down from 25% in 2025. The federal agency aims to suggest suitable locations for final evaluation by the end of 2027.

Regions in northern, northwestern, southern and eastern Germany remain under consideration as locations for final storage facilities.

Germany's nuclear waste is currently stored in 16 above-ground interim storage facilities.

The BGE assessments evaluate regions' geological conditions and their ability to safely contain highly radioactive material. Granite, salt, and clay are generally considered potentially suitable types of rock and areas with damaged or compromised subsurface conditions, places with mines or volcanos have been active, are not eligible as final disposal sites.

Germany needs to find a repository for approximately 27,000 cubic meters of highly radioactive nuclear waste generated over more than 60 years of nuclear power generation. The site is expected to ensure safety for 1 million years. The last German nuclear power plants were decommissioned in April 2023.

BGE said its goal remains finding the site that offers the highest level of safety for highly radioactive waste.

The agency's recommendations, however, are not legally binding and it will be up to German lawmakers to determine the final disposal site.
