Convicted right-wing terrorist Beate Zschäpe's request for early release from prison was denied with a court ruling that she would remain behind bars for at least nine more years.

Munich's Higher Regional Court on Tuesday set her minimum term at at least 24 years and refused to suspend the sentence to probation. By determining her guilt to be particularly severe, the court effectively ruled out her release after 15 years, which, including her time in pretrial detention, would have expired this year.

The Munich court said Zschäpe, currently 51, still had a good chance of leaving prison alive.

The decision is not yet legally binding, and the defense and the Federal Prosecutor's Office can appeal.

Zschäpe had been sentenced to life in prison in 2018 as an accomplice in the National Socialist Underground (NSU) murders that killed 10 people, mainly individuals with an immigrant background as well as a police officer.

The terror group went undetected for a decade. Zschäpe's fellow NSU members, Uwe Mundlos and Uwe Böhnhardt, injured several people with bomb attacks in Cologne and killed themselves to avoid arrest in 2011.

Under normal conditions, a convict who receives a life sentence in Germany can apply for parole after 15 years.

Bayern Munich striker Harry Kane has won football's most prestigious individual prize, beating Kylian Mbappe and Lamine Yamal. Kane has developed even more in the Bundesliga despite an uneven league.

German Defense Minister Boris Pistorius, along with his counterparts from France, Italy, Poland and the UK, authored an op-ed calling Russia "the most acute and long-term threat to Euro-Atlantic security and stability."

In the article, published online and in Tuesday's Tagesspiegel newspaper, the defense ministers wrote they "share a deep conviction" that European countries need to do more to ensure their own security and aim to build "a stronger Europe within a stronger NATO."

The ministers, however, added that the United States would remain an "indispensable ally."

The pronouncement comes as many in Europe are wary of US President Donald Trump's commitment to the NATO military alliance.

The US leader has threatened to leave the group and to seize control of Greenland, a territory of fellow NATO member Denmark. US presidents have repeatedly called for European NATO members to contribute more to the alliance's military capabilities.

As US support for Ukraine also appears to waver, the five defense ministers reiterated their political, military and financial support for Kyiv as it fights of Russia's full-scale invasion.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

In Singapore, to start a four-day business-oriented trip that will also bring him to Japan and South Korea, Chancellor Friedrich Merz said it was up to European and Asian nations to find their spot amid a changing global order.

"We must assert our interests in an international environment shaped by renewed great-power rivalry," Merz said.

With a view to unpredictable tariffs imposed by the United States under President Donald Trump and trade restrictions from China, Merz emphasized the stability offered by Germany and the rest of Europe.

"This is a deliberate counterpoint to what we are currently seeing. We want to do things differently, and we want to show that there is a better way," Merz said, adding that Europe sought to expand partnerships to increase free trade with open competition, reliability and a rules-based order.

He also called for increased international investment and cooperation.

"Under the federal government I lead, investment opportunities will be continuously improved," Merz said following a meeting with Singapore Prime Minister Lawrence Wong. "I encounter a great deal of confidence here in Germany and in Europe as locations for investment."

The German leader called for a new free-trade agreement between the 27-member European Union and the Trans-Pacific Partnership (CPTPP), comprising 12 countries across five continents, including Australia, Canada, Japan, Mexico and the United Kingdom. Neither the United States nor China is part of the CPTPP.

For his part, Wong said investment in Germany and Europe would continue.

"Many of our investors, including our sovereign wealth funds, have been investing in Germany for a long time," Wong said. "They will continue to invest because they are confident that, in the long term, Germany and Europe have potential."

Merz and Wong agreed their nations would work to expand cooperation in security and defense as well as trade, investment, sustainable energy and artificial intelligence.

#189. Germany news: Chancellor Friedrich Merz accuses North Korea of threatening Europe's freedom

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Friedrich Merz, who is visiting Seoul, said North Korea is putting Europe's security at risk by sending soldiers to fight for Russia in Ukraine. DW has more.

This blog is now closed. Here are the main headlines from and about Germany on Thursday, October 29, 2026.

The biggest news story out of Germany this evening had to do with the Left candidate Elif Eralp having sparked controversy with internal remarks to Der Spiegel.

A German news magazine's decision to publish private interview notes has sparked a controversy surrounding Berlin's potential next mayor.

On Thursday, Der Spiegel published internal comments on an interview by Elif Eralp, who led the socialist Left Party (Die Linke) to victory in September's state election. Her office had accidentally included the remarks while approving an interview for publication.

In the notes, Eralp expressed fears for her family's safety if she publicly named Ibrahim Ibrahim, a pro-Palestinian activist who is reported to have links to the Popular Front for the Liberation of Palestine (PFLP), which the EU designates a terrorist organization. Ibrahim had attended the Left Party's election-night celebrations and was photographed with prominent Left Party Bundestag member Ferat Koçak.

Eralp also cited advice she received from Berlin police against publicly naming individuals linked to organized crime, though that advice was reportedly related to a separate case. Der Spiegel questioned whether her fears could affect her independence as mayor. Eralp rejected suggestions that she could be intimidated and pledged firm action against terrorism and organized crime.

She is reportedly considering legal action against the magazine and a complaint to Germany's Press Council. The magazine has also faced criticism for publishing remarks intended for internal use, raising questions about the balance between public interest, privacy, and journalistic ethics.

The timing is significant. Eralp, the daughter of Turkish immigrants, could become Berlin's first Left Party mayor and the first woman of Turkish descent to hold the office. Her party won 25.7% of the vote in September's election and hopes to form a government with the center-left Social Democrats (SPD) and the Greens. Both prospective partners have approved formal coalition negotiations.

The Left must decide whether to follow suit at a party conference this Saturday, October 31. The far-left party is also facing accusations of antisemitism involving some of its members. Newly elected lawmaker Vanessa Emde faces possible exclusion from the Left's parliamentary group following comments questioning whether states, including Israel, have an inherent right to exist. The Left has been part of Berlin coalition governments before but has never led one. The controversy comes at a pivotal moment in its effort to do so.

Germany's leaders are on a foreign relations push, with Chancellor Friedrich Merz on a trip to South Korea, German Foreign Minister Johann Wadephul in Panama and German President Frank-Walter Steinmeier in China.

Merz spoke of the threat North Korea posed to Europe's freedom by supplying troops to fight in Russia's invasion of Ukraine. He wraps up his visit with a trip to the Demilitarized Zone (DMZ) on Friday.

German Foreign Minister Johann Wadephul, meanwhile, is in Panama where he is visiting ahead of traveling to Atlanta for the G20 meeting.

Wadephul is there to promote free trade and also handed over humanitarian supplies to his Panamanian counterpart Javier Martinez-Acha Vasquez.

And then German President Frank-Walter Steinmeier ditched the formalities in China and picked up a ping-pong paddle for a game with Germany’s most famous table tennis player, Tino Boll, who is accompanying Steinmeier as a special guest on his state visit.

German Foreign Minister Johann Wadephul landed in Panama on Wednesday evening for a visit.

After his arrival at Panama City airport, Wadephul handed over humanitarian supplies to his Panamanian counterpart Javier Martinez-Acha Vasquez.

The aid shipment is made up of about 20 tons of supplies. Panama was hit by a powerful earthquake earlier this month.

The supplies include water filters, blankets, tarpaulins, cots and kitchen sets worth over €150,000 ($167,000).

More than 5,000 people are still being housed in emergency shelters, weeks after the magnitude-7.7 quake on October 9, which resulted in injuries and damaged hundreds of buildings.

Wadephul also thanked the Central American country for maintaining the Panama Canal "so responsibly and ensuring it is fit for the future, as well as more generally focusing on preserving balance in the region."

Restrictions on other sea routes due to the conflict in the Middle East has meant that the Panama Canal is more important to global trade than ever.

Wadephul is due to hold talks on Thursday with Martinez-Acha Vasquez and President Jose Raul Mulino.

Wadephul will travel to the US state of Georgia for the G20 summit in Atlanta on Friday and Saturday.

Several names are being floated to replace Frank-Walter Steinmeier as Germany's ceremonial head of state — including some that could be the first woman to hold the post.

After delivering his speech at Peking University, German President Frank-Walter Steinmeier ditched the formalities to play ping-pong with Germany’s most famous table tennis player, Tino Boll.

In the university sports hall, the two faced each other, accompanied by two Chinese participants — a referee and the university president. Right from the start, Steinmeier downplayed his chances, and subsequently lost 1–7.

Boll, who won silver at the Beijing Olympics in 2008, is the most successful German table tennis player of all time.

He won several Olympic medals, as well as titles in World Cups and World Championships during his storied career, which saw him ranked as the number 1 player in the world in 2003, 2011 and 2018.

To this day, Boll remains one of the best-known Germans in China, where ping-pong is unofficially considered the national sport.

Boll is accompanying Steinmeier as a special guest on his state visit.

Germany's President Frank-Walter Steinmeier on Thursday called on China to reduce economic imbalances between the two countries, as well as between China and Europe.

Steinmeier, who was speaking at the renowned Peking University, also urged China to work with Germany and other European nations to strengthen the international, rules-based order and "to advocate for peace and free global trade."

The German president, who holds a largely ceremonial role, spoke of his respect for China's rapid development in its economy, technology and politics, calling China a "defining world power."

But Steinmeier added, "With greatness and power comes great responsibility."

"We are not concerned about China's rise. We are watching how China rises," he said.

"We do not want to decouple from China; on the contrary, we want to structure trade in such a way that it continues to benefit both sides." This, he said, requires fair and uniform rules.

Steinmeier was referring to the high barriers European companies face when trying to operate in the Chinese market. Since China heavily subsidizes its exports to Europe, the European market comes under pressure too quickly.

This poses "the danger of one-sided vulnerability," Steinmeier said. Europeans cannot want this for their economy, just as "China does not want it for its economy."

He also called on China to exert more influence on Russia to bring the war in Ukraine to an end.

According to Steinmeier, China's interests are also at stake in this war, just as they are in the Middle East. As a major trading power, China also needs free sea lanes, intact supply chains, and a secure energy supply.

"The longer these conflicts persist, the weaker the global economy as a whole becomes — and with it, one of its largest economies: China," the president said.

Steinmeier addressed future issues in which China should take a particular interest: climate change and artificial intelligence. He appealed to his hosts, saying that when it comes to AI, "we should jointly establish international guidelines so that the technology remains under human control," he said.

On the final day of his state visit, Steinmeier will meet with China's leadership and hold talks with, among others, President Xi Jinping.

Steinmeier is making his first visit to China in eight years, as he seeks to boost bilateral ties.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz on Thursday said North Korea was threatening "freedom throughout Europe" by sending soldiers to fight for Russia in its war against Ukraine.

After a meeting in Seoul with South Korean President Lee Jae Myung, Merz highlighted that Russia was supplying weapons to North Korea.

The chancellor said this arrangement therefore shows how "Korea's security affects Europe's security. And Europe's security also affects Korea's security."

North Korea has boosted its military ties with Russia since the start of the full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022.

Pyongyang has provided Moscow with artillery ammunition and missiles. It has also, according to South Korean intelligence services, sent about 15,000 soldiers to fight alongside Russian forces.

In exchange, Russia provides financial backing, military technology and security guarantees to North Korea.

Before Merz's arrival on Thursday, North Korea fired about 10 short-range ballistic missiles toward the Sea of Japan, according to the South Korean military.

At the start of his visit, Merz said he hoped that Korea would eventually be unified. The peninsula has been split along the 38th parallel since the end of hostilities in the Korean War in 1953.

Merz also agreed with Lee to deepen their two nations' strategic and security ties, which includes joint military exercises and exchange of intelligence.

The chancellor is expected to conclude his trip to South Korea with a visit to the Demilitarized Zone (DMZ) on Friday.

Germany is looking for new partners in Asia. As Chancellor Friedrich Merz travels to Singapore, Japan and South Korea, Berlin is trying to reduce its economic dependence on China while adapting to a changing global order. But can countries in the Indo-Pacific, besides China, help Germany diversify its trade and security partnerships?

This week on Berlin Briefing, DW's Giulia Saudelli speaks with DW Business Reporter Steven Beardsley and DW Chief Political Correspondent Nina Haase, who is traveling with the chancellor.

They discuss Germany's push to "de-risk" from China, growing security cooperation in the Indo-Pacific, and whether middle powers can work together to shape the future global order.

Germany and South Korea on Thursday pledged to work together on the economy, energy and security and high-tech industries, South Korean President Lee Jae Myung said.

The South Korean leader made the announcement after a meeting with German Chancellor Friedrich Merz in Seoul.

At a joint press conference, Lee told reporters that he had asked Merz for Germany's backing in securing favorable treatment for South Korea as part of the EU's Industrial Accelerator Act.

The two leaders also agreed ​to ⁠improve bilateral ties to ⁠an "enhanced ​strategic partnership," Lee said.

An eight-year-old girl sustained serious injuries after she was attacked by at least one wolf at a wildlife park in Brandenburg in east Germany.

The child was brought to hospital by a rescue helicopter on Wednesday.

Local officials said a zookeeper had helped prevent other children from being attacked.

Police are investigating how and why the attack occurred, which took place during a special program for children.

Police on Thursday said their investigation was centered on a staff member who was in the enclosure with the children, a spokeswoman said.

The Teltow-Fläming district administration said the incident happened before the wildlife park's regular opening hours as part of an "Animal Keeper Day" for children.

The administration also said the attack had taken place in the polar wolf enclosure.

"While the animals in the wildlife enclosure were being watered, the wolves kept there attacked a girl and seriously injured her," the district administration said.

The zookeeper's quick action prevented "further harm to two other girls" who were also at the scene, the district added.

The park will remain closed for the time being, officials said, as authorities launch a safety review at the wildlife park, which is about 60 kilometers (37 miles) south of Berlin.

Police are still probing the circumstances that led to the children being inside the enclosure.

The Johannismühle Wildlife Park said it was cooperating with authorities but could not provide further details due to the open investigation.

"We deeply regret the incident, and the affected family has our full support," the park said.

The wildlife park was closed until further notice "due to technical reasons," a sign at the entrance said on Thursday.

A wolf bit a woman in a busy shopping area in Hamburg this year, in what is thought to be the first such attack since wolves were reintroduced to the country in 1998.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz, who is visiting Seoul, said at the start of his visit that he hopes North and South Korea will eventually be reunited.

The Korean Peninsula has been divided into two states since the Korean War ended in 1953.

Merz pointed to his own country's division between West and East Germany for much of the second half of the 20th century.

"Korea and Germany are united by the painful experience of division," he wrote in the guest book during his meeting with South Korean President Lee Jae Myung.

"I also wish for the people of Korea what we are fortunate to experience in Germany: unity in freedom."

North Korea is a largely isolated dictatorship ruled by Kim Jong Un, while the South is a democracy that has close ties with the West.

The two Koreas technically remain at war since a truce ended hostilities.

Merz is set to visit the demilitarized zone (DMZ), which divides the two countries, on Friday.

The German chancellor's visit to South Korea is the last stop on his tour of Asia this week, which has also seen him visit Japan and Singapore.

Bayern Munich head coach Vincent Kompany has just returned home from the hospital, admitting that he hadn't listened to his body, "and then at some point, my body just said, 'Enough is enough!'"

Read our full report on Kompany's health and the need for better support for coaches at the highest level of sport here.

They were once top sellers in China. Now the likes of Volkswagen, Toyota and Honda are losing ground to a new generation of Chinese carmakers. What happened?

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Volkswagen (VW) reported third quarter losses of €5.8 billion ($6.5 billion) on Thursday, as it faces rising competition from China.

Germany's biggest car maker had reported a loss of just over €1.07 billion in the same period last year.

Volkswagen, like other car makers, has struggled to keep pace with fierce competition from China, the world's biggest car market. VW has also struggled with the transition to electric vehicles and with declining demand.

Volkswagen last month warned that its 10-brand group, which includes makers such as Audi, Seat and Skoda, was facing a 10-billion-euro hit to its annual profits.

The most significant hit came from Porsche, Volkswagen's luxury sports car and SUV subsidiary. Porsche, which was once a major driver of profits for the VW group, was also one of the main factors behind VW's quarterly losses in 2025.

The luxury brand has been hit by a sales slump in China, US tariff policy and its electric vehicle strategy.

VW announced this year that it plans to cut over 100,000 jobs across the group by the end of the decade as it tries to reduce costs. It announced on Thursday that its restructuring would cost the company 31 billion euros.

The company is considering or has decided to shut down multiple German factories — including plants in Zwickau, Emden, Hanover, and Neckarsulm.

"What matters now is swift and consistent implementation," Arno Antlitz, VW's chief financial officer said in a statement. "We cannot lose any time and need to act quickly and decisively."

For more on this story, read our full report by DW's Mark Hallam.

#80. EU seeks 'highest level' ties with Canada in Montreal summit

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen said EU seeks "association without accession" for Canada, after EU members snub "associate member" designation.

European Union and Canadian leaders are set to meet at a special Canada-EU summit to discuss critical policies like artificial inteligence (AI), climate, energy and ⁠security.

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen said on Thursday, ahead of the summit, which begins on Friday, that the EU will continue to explore Canada's prospects for becoming the bloc's first "associate member."

But despite her comments, the summit's declaration is expected to exclude the term "associate member," a move pushed by Germany and other members.

"Our idea is simple: it is an association without accession," Von der Leyen clarified. "We can build a partnership that comes closer to membership than anything we have done before."

"We both want to bring our relationship to the highest level possible," von der Leyen added.

Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney had said this week he wasn't concerned about the  "associate member" status, or "specific names" to be given to the new alliance with the EU.

"What matters is the difference that it will make to the lives of Canadians," Carney added.

Ottawa's quest for deeper ties to Europe is seen as a move to reduce its reliance on the US since President Donald Trump imposed tariffs on a range of Canadian goods.

Frederic Merand, political expert on EU-Canada relations at the University of Montreal, spoke to DW about how the summit serves as "political impetus" to improve the bilateral trade relationship and to free Canada of its reliance on the US.

"It's not going to happen, over time, overnight. And every time we move closer to the Europeans, we realize how dependent we are on the US and how also vulnerable we are to, for example, us retaliation," Merand said.

But he added that courting the EU is also part of a broader strategy "to reinvent the Canadian economy, to create a single market, to attract more investment," as well as to diversify our partnerships with China, India and also other parts of the world.

The EU is "a natural partner" to work with, Merand said, given its existing free trade agreement with Canada, in addition to the close historical and cultural ties between the two partners.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

An EU official, speaking on condition of anonymity, told AFP that Canada and the EU sought to align policies in areas such as climate, energy and artificial intelligence, allowing Ottawa to participate in some EU programs.

Canada has critical minerals needed for green technologies such as batteries and wind turbines, something for which Europe normally relies on China.

But Canada is in need of major infrastructure investment to develop its mining industry.

There are hopes that the summit can result in a plan for the European Investment Bank to back Canadian critical mineral projects.

EU and Canadian leaders are also looking into a digital trade deal that can allow for faster and cheaper online transactions.

Additionally, Canada could be allowed to participate in the EU's Erasmus youth program, which allows university students to spend a semester abroad.

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ashley Paul Griffith's matter was heard in a NSW court on Friday. (Supplied: Queensland Corrective Services)

Ashley Paul Griffith has been charged with more than 200 child abuse offences in NSW.

Griffiths, who is serving a life sentence in Queensland over similar offences, was extradited from Queensland to NSW on Wednesday.

Notorious childcare paedophile Ashley Paul Griffith has been charged with more than 200 additional child abuse offences, allegedly committed against nearly two dozen children in NSW.

NSW Police allege Griffith sexually abused 23 children while he was working in Sydney's inner west between July 2014 and December 2017.

Griffith was extradited from Queensland to NSW on Wednesday and was arrested at Silverwater Correctional Facility on Friday morning.

The Queensland man is facing 202 offences in NSW relating to allegations of child sex abuse committed against children between the ages of one and five.

The charges include 68 counts of sexual intercourse with a child under the age of 10 and 42 counts of aggravated sexual intercourse with a child under the age of 10.

He has also been charged with 23 counts of producing child abuse material and aggravated indecent assault relating to children.

The 48-year-old has been sentenced to life in jail in Queensland after admitting to offences against 69 girls at early learning centres in Brisbane and Italy over two decades.

NSW Police said its State Crime Command's Child Abuse Squad and the Australian Federal Police had been investigating the Sydney-based allegations since November 2022.

It said the "lengthy and complex investigation" involved forensically examining thousands of digital files and extensive victim identification inquiries.

After his extradition earlier this week, Queensland Corrective Services said Griffith would remain in NSW until the conclusion of the legal process and would go on to serve his jail term in the state that imposed the harsher sentence.

Griffith was sentenced for his Queensland offences in 2024, where the judge ordered Griffith to serve 27 years before being eligible for parole, almost double the state's statutory minimum sentence.

The matter was heard in a in NSW bail court on Friday morning, where legal aid lawyer Bethany Burns did not apply for bail on Griffith's behalf and it was formally refused.

Griffith was not required to appear, and Judge Daniel Covington adjourned the matter until January.

#11. How AI and 'doorbell cameras' can reduce growing risk of human encounters with polar bears

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

The new tool is among several solutions being developed to protect both people and polar bears as the impacts of climate change increase the likelihood of dangerous encounters

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Artificial intelligence-powered "doorbell cameras" are being trialled by an international team of specialists to alert local communities to approaching polar bears amid the growing threat of human conflict.

Created by the conservation charity Polar Bears International, the initiative is part of a broader push to safeguard both human lives and wildlife as global warming escalates the frequency of hazardous interactions.

Known as Bear-vision, the technology evolves from earlier "Bear-dar" systems, which integrated radar with video equipment to spot movement across the Arctic landscape and track nearby animals.

According to the organisation, Bear-vision provides a cost-effective and accessible warning tool designed to notify residential properties and commercial premises whenever a bear draws near.

Researchers are setting up the initial Bear-vision unit at a waste site in Churchill, a northern coastal town situated along Hudson Bay in the Canadian province of Manitoba.

This deployment is intended to assist local residents in overseeing the site during a period when polar bears congregate to await the freezing of Hudson Bay so they can hunt seals, a gathering that also draws thousands of visiting tourists.

Krista Wright, executive director of Polar Bears International, said: “We’re grateful to the Churchill community, who we’ve been honoured to work with over the decades on human-polar bear co-existence, ensuring that conservation benefits not just wildlife but the community too.

“We’re experiencing first-hand how co-existence is becoming part of everyday life in more places.”

Elsewhere, the conservation group is exploring other novel methods to safely ward off bears, including drones.

Polar Bear International and Brigham Young University are collaborating with Churchill’s Polar Bear Alert Programme to test drones as an affordable and non-lethal way to move bears away from communities safely.

This deterrent research project is also testing ultrasonic sound, conductive materials, and a new odour-based bear spray formula.

Polar Bear International said the doorbell camera technology was being tested at the Churchill waste dump alongside to keep polar bears away from the facility.

It comes after a fire destroyed the site in 2024, which led to bears pawing through the rubbish to find food and causing concerns around hungry polar bears not only ingesting toxic rubbish but then being attracted by the smell of food waste.

The team has also installed new electric fences, deployed its Bear-dar technology and sent a group of its experts from Churchill to tour a similar waste management facility in Greenland.

The annual Polar Bear Week 2026, organised by Polar Bear International, begins this Sunday to mark the seasonal gathering of the animals.

The group highlighted that melting sea ice driven by climate change now leaves these animals stranded waiting on land for approximately one month longer than their grandparents did.

This means measures to ensure safe co-existence between the town and the animals are becoming increasingly important, it added.

#41. Psychologists issue warning over ‘harmful’ mental health advice from AI chatbots

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

A new survey conducted by the BPS found nine in ten psychologists are concerned over their patients’ use of AI

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Psychologists have sounded the alarm over AI chatbots providing mental health advice to people, dubbing the information “unqualified and harmful”.

A survey conducted by the British Psychological Society (BPS) found that psychologists have noted a 49% increase in the number of patients seeking support from chatbots in the past year.

The poll, involving 197 participants comprised of psychologists, counsellors and psychotherapists, found that seven in 10 are somewhat or very concerned about the use of AI for mental health advice.

Nearly 88% (nine in 10) sounded the alarm that patients are relying too heavily on AI, while around 76% of survey participants said the technology is negatively affecting the mental wellbeing of their clients.

Some 80% told the survey they are concerned clients are becoming overly reliant on the technology, while around three quarters (76%) said it is negatively affecting client wellbeing.

Dr Laura Dean, president of the BPS, said: “This research shows there is real cause for concern for those that use AI for mental health therapy, and that concern is coming directly from psychological practitioners on the frontline providing vital in-person support.

“The risk of unregulated open access AI chatbots providing unqualified and harmful advice remains very real.”

Dr Dean warned that the integration of AI into healthcare “can only work with the right safeguards that protect both psychological professionals and the people they support”.

She added: “Although AI offers many benefits, it cannot replace the vital human element in mental health care.

“More research and insight from psychological practitioners, and people seeking support using AI tools, is needed to understand how and where human-led therapeutic services and AI can be safely integrated to deliver the right care and outcomes.”

One practitioner surveyed told the BPS: “I am concerned about not understanding exactly what AI does, how it works, and how it comes up with its answers.

“I am also concerned about people turning to AI because it feels “safer” (avoiding the relational risk of building trust with another human being), and we therefore increase the isolation and lack of social skills and contact in society, which is directly linked to quality of life and wellbeing.”

Another called for “accountability”, adding: AI often is seen as “free” but because of this, like with many social media platforms, the companies that run them have free rein to do as they please with little to no legal responsibilities.”

The BPS poll comes after a survey of 2,000 children aged 11-17 by Barnardo’s and the National Counselling and Psychotherapy Society (NCPS) found more than four in 10 (43%) had used AI for advice or emotional support.

Rosie Weatherley, information content manager at the charity Mind, said: “These survey findings echo what we are hearing from people and seeing through emerging evidence – that even casual use of generative AI can cause harm. So much is unknown about this new and ever-changing technology, particularly when it comes to the impact of long-term use.

“We’re supportive of digital tools that help people navigate information to make more sense of mental health and the services they can access, but these must not become a replacement for great quality care, where people feel properly heard and seen.

“We know that people still value human support over AI tools and we must not lose sight of this.”

#647. Breaking: Mushroom murderer likely to die in jail after failed appeal

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Erin Patterson was resentenced to life in prison without parole. (NewsWire: Jason Edwards)

Victoria's highest court has increased the jail term for mushroom cook Erin Patterson.

The court removed a non-parole period that was set last year when Patterson was sentenced to life in prison for triple murder and attempted murder.

It is unclear whether Patterson's legal team will mount a challenge in the High Court.

Erin Patterson has lost her appeal against her murder convictions over the deaths of three relatives who ate beef Wellingtons that contained death cap mushrooms.

A separate appeal by the prosecution to impose a harsher sentence was upheld and Patterson will be re-sentenced.

Erin Patterson appeal live updates: For all the latest news after the mushroom killer's failed appeal, read our blog.

The decision today by the Court of Appeal means it is likely Patterson will die in jail.

Patterson was last year sentenced to life in prison with a non-parole period of 33 years for the murders of Don and Gail Patterson, and Heather Wilkinson, along with the attempted murder of Heather's husband Ian.

She had mounted an appeal on seven grounds, all but two of which were dismissed by the Court of Appeal judges.

The appeal was heard on the remaining two grounds, but was dismissed.

Patterson, who watched proceedings on a video link from a women's prison, sat back in her seat and appeared to stare blankly when the decision was announced.

It is unclear whether her legal team will mount a challenge in the High Court.

She is being held in the maximum security wing of the Dame Phyllis Frost women's prison.

A non-parole period off 33 years included in Erin Patterson's original sentence has been set aside. (AFP: Martin Keep)

The Court of Appeal judges upheld the appeal by prosecutors over the non-parole period that was included in her original sentence.

Delivering the court's judgment, Justice Stephen McLeish said the crimes involved "shocking criminality" and Patterson's conduct deserved to be "denounced in the strongest terms".

He said Patterson's decision to plan and then poison her relatives with a beef Wellington laced with poisonous mushrooms involved an "elaborate series of deceptions".

Victoria's highest court has upheld Erin Patterson's convictions for the murder of three relatives and the attempted murder of a fourth. Few legal options now remain for the killer.

"The offending was carefully planned and deliberate," he said.

Justice McLeish described Patterson's crimes as "extraordinarily cold and calculating."

"The applicant's intent to murder all four victims was sustained for nearly three weeks from when she invited them to lunch, through to preparing and serving the poisoned meal and then throughout the protracted suffering and ultimate deaths that resulted," he said.

It was therefore inappropriate for parole to be an option, he said.

The decision to reject Patterson's appeal leaves her with few legal options, with an appeal to the High Court of Australia her only avenue.

Patterson's leave to appeal was granted by the judges on two of the seven grounds her lawyers put forward in arguments.

The first of those grounds related to a mistake that meant the jury hearing her case stayed at the same hotel as a police witness.

However, the appeal judges said today there was no reason to think the jury wouldn't have carried out its sworn duty.

The second related to the use of expert evidence derived from Patterson's mobile phone data used by the prosecution to show she had visited areas identified in internet posts as death cap mushroom sites.

The court decided that this evidence was admissible as part of the wider circumstantial case put forward by the prosecution.

#658. Youth want a voice amid controversial fracking and mining proposals

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Jazmin Bingham is concerned about what resource extraction could mean for the region.  (ABC South East SA: Sam Bradbrook)

The proposed introduction of mining and fracking to a South Australian food bowl has young people considering what it could mean for their future.

The Limestone Coast's economy has been dominated by agriculture since early European settlers dug drains to create some of the most productive farmland in SA.

But mining and fracking are positioning to become a part of the region's economic future.

Gomoroi woman and climate and youth advocate Jazmin Bingham, 19, said it represented a change in what the future could hold for local jobs, communities and the environment.

Jazmin Bingham is a member of Seed, an Indigenous youth-led climate advocacy group.  (ABC South East SA: Sam Bradbrook)

"Young people are generally not at the table when decisions are being made and that's just the way it is, and things just need a change," she said.

"Our lands are so sacred and so rich. That's why the agriculture and primary industries are so strong down south.

"But to strip that from us and not even just First Nations communities, but farmers. As soon as fracking gets underway and rare earth mining gets underway, you can't restore the land to what it was."

Fracking, or hydraulic fracture stimulation, has proved controversial on the Limestone Coast over its potential effects on the region's groundwater system.

Conventional gas extraction has occurred in the South East for more than a century.

But in May, the SA government announced legislation to repeal a 10-year moratorium on fracking in the region, introduced in 2018.

The bill will fail without support from state parliament's crossbench, but Premier Peter Malinauskas has made it clear the moratorium will lapse in 2028.

Millicent teenager and Wattle Range Council Youth Advisory Council chair Zoe Moore, 16, wrote a letter to the premier against the fracking ban ending.

Zoe Moore says young people should be engaged in proposed change for the region.  (ABC South East SA: Sam Bradbrook)

She said it was an important time for young people to be engaged in the decisions being made around them.

"If we always have the same older generations making decisions for us, it will continue to impact us longer than it will impact them," she said.

An alternative Greens bill to ban fracking permanently in the region has passed the Upper House, but will be defeated in the Lower House by Labor's sizeable majority.

Mining and energy minister Tom Koutsantonis said the state needed cheap gas to meet its energy needs and grow the economy.

Tom Koutsantonis has backed gas as a way to grow the economy.  (ABC News: Ashlin Blieschke)

"If we want to make things here, we need the cheap inputs of energy and one of those cheap inputs of energy is gas," he said.

"The idea of locking it up and importing it from somewhere else, given that we've got it here, is the antithesis of what young people need in this state.

"Long-term jobs require cheap energy and cheap energy comes from an abundance of gas."

Naracoorte farmer Joe Schultz's land falls inside the exploration area for a proposed rare earth mine in the Wrattonbully region.

Joe Schultz is the third generation of his family to farm in the region.  (ABC South East SA: Sam Bradbrook)

Developer Australian Rare Earths (AR3) is expected to finalise an application to dig for the critical minerals in the coming months.

The 28-year-old is a member of the Limestone Coast Sustainable Futures Group, set up in opposition to the proposal.

Mr Schultz said the possibility of mining had him thinking twice about investing in the land and his future.

"I feel like a young farmer here trying to carve my way out and carve my life into a beautiful farming career, and I've got so many other things that I never thought I would even consider," he said.

Joe Schultz is thinking twice about investing in his farming future. (ABC South East SA: Sam Bradbrook)

The mining and processing of rare earths is dominated by China, with the federal government making moves to ensure domestic supply.

The minerals are used in a range of products including electronics, batteries and renewable energy technology such as wind turbines.

AR3 director Travis Beinke said he believed rare earth mining and farming could work together.

Travis Beinke says the proposed Koppamurra project could create stable future jobs and complement farming.  (ABC News: Lincoln Rothall)

"We believe that by introducing a new industry like mining in the region, there is a great opportunity to establish new jobs in the region where the youth of today may have to sometimes leave their home towns for study and ongoing work opportunities," he said.

The project has an initial expected operating time of 12 years, and the company planned to create about 100 jobs.

"Mining provides an alternate economic contributor to the region in times where farming can go through tougher times depending on seasons," Mr Beinke said.

The Cheesecake Factory finds itself in the middle of a presidential tussle, and it’s doing just fine.

After former President Barack Obama compared President Donald Trump’s White House decor to The Cheesecake Factory, the restaurant announced during an earnings call Tuesday that sales soared 15.3 percent from July to September. Those months were before the two leaders started their public feud that sucked in the restaurant.

Obama’s comments were met with outrage from MAGA, who called them “elitist.” Trump’s Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent responded by ordering a French dip cheeseburger at a Cheesecake Factory in Washington, D.C. Monday. Obama defended his comments at a Wednesday campaign rally in Georgia, saying the restaurant was his “jam” while he raised his daughters.

Though The Cheesecake Factory was swept up in controversy without asking, it’s a valuable win for the company, Marin Richardson, CEO of publicity firm Disrupt PR, told The Independent in an email.

“Both sides are fighting over who loves it more,” Richardson said. “Most brands would pay a fortune for that.”

Ethics experts have accused some of President Donald Trump’s Cabinet of violating federal law by joining him on the campaign trail to rally for Republican candidates ahead of the midterm elections.

At least nine of Trump’s 15 cabinet secretaries have been accused of violating the Hatch Act —a federal law prohibiting federal employees from engaging in certain partisan political activities in their official capacity — over the past few months, according to The New York Times.

The president and vice president are exempt from restrictions under the 87-year-old law, but it applies to all other civil employees of the executive branch.

An attendee at a Donald Trump rally in South Carolina trolled a Fox News reporter by comparing the MAGA faithful to Jim James and the Jonestown suicide cult.

The pro-Trump station’s reporter Lucas Tomlinson was interviewing members of the audience gathered in Rock Hill on Thursday to hear the president stump for Darline Graham, the sister of the late Senator Lindsey Graham, who is facing off against Democrat Annie Andrews.

Tomlinson asked the man, who gave his name as Tom, why he was at the rally.

“Well, in the late '70s, I was a little too young to get involved and go to South America for the Jonestown Massacre, but this is as close as I'll ever get, so that's why I'm here,” Tom replied.

When a confused Tomlinson asked him to clarify he meant the Jonestown massacre, in which 900 members of the Jonestown cult committed suicide in Guyana, Tom replied, “ there's nothing like seeing a cult right up front, man.”

President Donald Trump has continued to attack Democratic Representative Ilhan Omar of Minnesota at his South Carolina rally.

Trump repeated his claim that Omar was sworn into office under a different name than her real one — therefore, she should be immediately expelled from the House and her seat forfeited by the Democrats.

“You know what I hated about her? Life is life and you do your thing—but she comes from a shithouse country. Why can't we take people from good countries?” the president ranted.

President Donald Trump has canceled an appearance at a Kansas rally on Saturday, his only scheduled campaign-trail appearance the weekend before Election Day.

The president will instead participate in a series of virtual rallies. It’s unclear whether he will reschedule the Kansas event.

#109. Scientists for the first time injected an AI-created virus into a human

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

The announcement has not been verified in a peer-reviewed study

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Scientists have given a human patient a virus designed by artificial intelligence for the first time, in an effort to fight infections that no longer respond to antibiotics.

California-based AI startup Radical Numerics said a patient with a serious bacterial infection received an experimental treatment containing viruses created by its AI model, Omnii-1.

The model designs bacteriophages, or phages, which are viruses that infect and kill bacteria without targeting human cells. Doctors already use phage therapy as an experimental treatment for serious bacterial infections, particularly when patients have few other options.

Rather than searching for a virus that could kill the patient's bacteria, Omnii-1 redesigned part of a virus so it could better recognize and attack the specific bacterial strain causing the infection. The company says the AI-driven process could eventually take just days.

The treatment was administered through a special program that allows doctors to try experimental therapies when conventional options are limited.

Radical Numerics said this was the first time AI-designed phages had been used to treat a human patient. The announcement was published on the company's website but has not been confirmed in a peer-reviewed study, and the company has not said whether the treatment helped the patient.

The approach could offer another option for treating hard-to-treat infections. Doctors typically have to find a naturally occurring virus that can target the specific bacteria causing a patient's illness.

Radical Numerics hopes its AI model can speed up that process by designing viruses to target a patient's particular infection, potentially helping doctors find a treatment faster.

The company said it tested its AI-designed viruses against bacteria taken from the patient before administering the treatment. The virus mixture eliminated the patient's antibiotic-resistant bacterial sample in laboratory testing, according to the company.

However, it has not released details about the patient's diagnosis, the treatment's outcome, or whether the infection was ultimately cleared.

The development comes amid growing interest in using AI to accelerate medical research, alongside concerns about how the technology could be misused.

In August, researchers at Stanford University and the Arc Institute announced that they had used AI to design previously unseen viruses capable of killing E. coli bacteria in laboratory experiments. Of nearly 300 AI-designed viral genomes synthesized and tested, 16 produced functional viruses. Those experiments were conducted in bacteria, not humans.

Researchers have warned that AI systems capable of designing new viruses raise biosafety concerns, particularly if similar methods are adapted to create viruses that infect humans.

The Stanford team deliberately focused on viruses that attack bacteria rather than humans or animals, but scientists have called for stronger safeguards as AI-driven biological research advances.

#163. More than half of Victorian lawyers use AI – but only a quarter have cut client fees as a result

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Research finds 55% of lawyers are using AI in legal practices, up from 37% last year, though efficiency gains aren’t as high as expected

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

More than half of Victoria’s lawyers have reported using AI in their work, according to a new study, but just over a quarter say they have reduced fees as a result of efficiency gains.

They also say clients seeking advice from chatbots have added to their workload, forcing them to correct errors made by AI.

The 2026 census of 2,221 of the state’s lawyers, conducted by the Victorian Legal Services Board and Commissioner’s legal services research centre, found a big increase in lawyers using AI in their practices, rising from 37% to 55% in the last year.

While the 2025 report found AI use was highest among those under 30 years of age, in 2026 this disparity had largely disappeared, although usage remained low for those aged 60 and over.

AI use was most common in trade practices (85%), banking and finance (78%) and information technology and telecommunications (78%), while it was used much less in criminal law (29%), family law (45%) and administrative law (46%).

While there are AI tools designed specifically for legal services, 79% of lawyers reported using consumer chatbots such as ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini or Copilot. Legal-specific tools alone were used by 22% of respondents.

The increased use in AI has not translated to a reduction in fees for most, the survey found, with just 26% of lawyers responding that their use of AI had resulted in lower client fees.

Reduced fees were more likely to be reported by those working for business clients (34%) compared with individual fee-paying clients (23%).

Some reported AI had enabled them to take on work that would have previously been uneconomical (18%).

The principal researcher and report co-author, Dr Georgie Rychner, said fee reductions in corporate and big firms could be attributed to the fact clients in those settings were repeat users of legal services.

“They have a high level of legal capability,” she said. “They also are perhaps using AI in their own work, so they have a better understanding and are able to advocate for lower prices … whereas we’re not seeing that as much in the community legal system sector.”

Rychner said the efficiency gains of using AI were not as high as expected, but that in 2025 the survey found 89% of practitioners said they had taken steps to verify the accuracy of AI output, which might have negated the gains.

In 2026 there had been a rise in clients using AI for their own legal research, she said, with 46% of lawyers reporting their clients were arriving with inaccurate or misleading information, and 41% presenting AI-generated documents that required correction.

Nearly half of lawyers said there was an increase in time and effort required if their clients used AI, compared with 19% who said it led to a reduction in time and effort.

“What we are hearing is that clients are more frequently arriving with misleading or inaccurate information about their legal problems, more so than they’re arriving with a better understanding of legal problems,” she said, adding that more research was needed to understand client perspectives.

Courts and legal practitioner bodies across Australia have been grappling with the rise in AI use among lawyers and the public since 2023.

A Victorian lawyer last year was the first in the country to face sanctions for false citations discovered, being stripped of his ability to practise as a principal lawyer. Investigations were also launched by regulatory bodies in Western Australia and New South Wales in similar cases.

The federal court in April issued new rules around using generative AI, while the chief justice of the high court, Stephen Gageler, said last year the use of AI-generated content in legal cases had reached an “unsustainable phase”.

#449. US agency votes to ban all Chinese labs from testing US electronics

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sign up now: Get ST's newsletters delivered to your inbox

US Federal Communications Commission Chairman Brendan Carr said that in 2025, 82% of electronics were tested in China, which does not have an agreement with the US.

WASHINGTON - The Federal Communications Commission (FCC) on Oct 29 voted to bar all Chinese labs from testing electronic devices including smartphones, cameras and computers for use in the US, expanding a previous agency action targeting Beijing.

The FCC said it will prohibit test labs and certification bodies in countries including China that deny reciprocal treatment to US-based test labs. The FCC prohibitions are slated to take effect in December 2028. FCC Chair Brendan Carr said that in 2025, 82% of electronics were tested in China, which does not have an agreement with the US.

“These labs are a critical gateway into American communications infrastructure – and their privileged role gives them access to sensitive and confidential information about electronic products often months before the devices enter the market,” Carr said, adding they could “allow insecure gear into the US market, disrupt communications networks, or even steal intellectual property”.

The Chinese Embassy in Washington did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

In 2025, the FCC banned testing of US electronics by labs owned or controlled by the Chinese government, and several dozen labs were barred.

About 4% of all electronic devices are tested in labs inside the US, Carr said.

Separately, the FCC streamlined its approval process for devices tested in US labs or labs from countries not posing national security risks.

This is the latest move by Washington to crack down on China over technology issues and equipment.

In December, the FCC banned the import of all new models of Chinese drones and also has banned imports of new models of Chinese-made consumer routers, the boxes connecting computers, phones and smart devices to the internet.

In July, the FCC voted to bar sales of devices ​in the US that contain key hardware from Chinese companies deemed to ‌be national security risks.

The FCC maintains a list of firms, including Chinese telecom company Huawei and ZTE, whose equipment is barred for sale in the ​US due to national security concerns.

In June, the FCC banned the import of more equipment from a group of Chinese manufacturers including Huawei and ZTE and has proposed to ​ban the import ⁠of most military-grade drones. REUTERS

#469. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang says ties to company under spying probe were ‘normal’

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sign up now: Get ST's newsletters delivered to your inbox

Nvidia chief executive Jensen Huang said he hadn’t been informed by the US government of its suspicions about Alice Huang, a top executive of Megaspeed.

NEW YORK – Nvidia’s Chief Executive Officer Jensen Huang said the chipmaker’s dealings with Megaspeed International were “normal and not unusual” following a New York Times report that one of the Singapore-based firm’s executives is under investigation for spying on behalf of China.

Nvidia’s relationship with Megaspeed’s officials was proper, and the chipmaker has checked to ensure that Megaspeed didn’t illegally send its artificial intelligence processors to China, Huang said in an interview on Oct 29. That country is barred from buying Nvidia’s powerful AI chips – known as graphics processing units, or GPUs – under US export rules.

“We’ve studied all their deployments of our GPUs and counted every one,” he said in the interview. “And I think the US government also has as well.”

The remarks follow a Times story that the US government is investigating an executive from Megaspeed for allegedly providing information to the Chinese government. Huang Le, also known as Alice Huang, has previously been pictured with Nvidia’s leader on social media, and the story reports that she may have been serving as a Chinese agent aiming to evade chip export controls.

In the interview, Huang said that his interactions with her were typical of those with Chinese customers and executives. She’s no longer with Megaspeed, he said.

“Our relationship, our interactions, have been related to Megaspeed, and it was all completely normal and not unusual,” Huang said. The CEO said he hadn’t been informed by the US government of its suspicions about Huang Le.

The growing geopolitical rivalry between the US and China has led to increasing clampdowns by Washington on exports of key American technology. That has cut off Nvidia from a lucrative source of revenue in China, the largest market for chips.

The company has received some latitude to ship a limited amount of older AI-related products to the Asian country under the Trump administration. That approval hasn’t resulted in a material amount of shipments, at least in part because Beijing has sought to limit the deliveries itself in an effort to foster its own chip industry.

Nonetheless, the restrictions on exports have led to investigations and charges of smuggling of Nvidia’s best products to China – something the company has said is an insignificant issue. Megaspeed is one of a group of companies that Bloomberg News and others have reported may have tried to illegally divert shipments of chips intended for data centers outside of China into that country. #732. World’s largest crypto exchange investigates suspected multibillion-dollar hack

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The world’s largest cryptocurrency exchange, Binance, has launched an investigation into a possible cyberattack after billions of dollars in digital assets were reportedly transferred from wallets linked to the platform.

Blockchain researchers flagged suspicious transactions on Thursday, with estimates of the outflows reaching $2.77 billion across five blockchains. Binance said it was investigating reports of a potential security issue but insisted that customer funds were safe. The exchange has not confirmed a hack or disclosed any financial losses.

“Our team is conducting security checks. Withdrawals will resume as soon as the security issue is fully resolved. User funds remain safe,” the company said on X on Thursday.

Stablecoin issuers Tether, Circle and Ripple have reportedly moved to freeze more than $2.5 billion in digital assets linked to the suspected hack.

Binance later confirmed that $2.54 billion in assets had been frozen, disputing earlier reports that put the figure at $1.8 billion. The exchange said the amount could rise as it worked with law enforcement, stablecoin issuers and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and freeze additional funds.

Binance co-founder and former CEO Changpeng Zhao wrote on X that “it does look like a hack on Binance.”

The suspected attack comes amid a series of major cryptocurrency thefts this year. In September, hackers stole approximately $388 million from the Bitget exchange, while attacks on the Drift Protocol and Kelp DAO platforms in April resulted in losses of roughly $285 million and $292 million, respectively. In February 2025, suspected North Korean hackers stole approximately $1.5 billion from Bybit in what was widely described as the largest cryptocurrency heist on record at the time.

Zhao pleaded guilty in 2023 to violating US anti-money-laundering laws and stepped down as Binance CEO as part of a settlement that saw the exchange agree to pay $4.3 billion in penalties. He subsequently served a four-month prison sentence.

US President Donald Trump pardoned Zhao in October 2025. The White House defended the decision, claiming the Binance founder had been unfairly prosecuted under former President Joe Biden as part of a broader crackdown on the cryptocurrency industry.

#223. Trump administration attempts to take down leak of ICE personnel data

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An Icelandic web company, FlokiNET, has defended the data’s publication as satisfying a ‘legitimate’ public interest.

The United States Department of Homeland Security (DHS) has asked an Icelandic web hosting company to take down a site with information about tens of thousands of its employees.

On Thursday, the Icelandic company FlokiNET posted a letter it received from the cybersecurity firm ZeroFox, on behalf of DHS.

The letter accused FlokiNET of violating its terms of service by publishing a “stolen” employee dataset and asked it to “remove or disable” the information.

“The content is live and places more than 40,000 identifiable individuals at risk of harassment, stalking, and physical harm,” the letter said.

FlokiNET said the letter concerned one domain, icepi.cx, which has compiled information about employees from a range of DHS agencies, including Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) and Customs and Border Protection (CBP).

Those agencies have come under intense scrutiny since the start of President Donald Trump’s mass deportation campaign in 2025.

But the Trump administration has often refused to identify DHS agents involved in specific operations, citing safety risks.

Though FlokiNET said it refused to comply with DHS’s request, it noted that the website’s registrar ultimately suspended the domain. The employee information has since moved to a different domain.

In a statement posted online, FlokiNET defended its decision not to remove the site.

“Federal officers carrying out an enforcement programme are not private individuals while they are carrying it out,” the company said. “The public has a legitimate interest in knowing who is doing that work, under whose authority, and in which office.”

The database was first posted online on Wednesday by Distributed Denial of Secrets (DDoSecrets), a group that showcases leaked government data.

In the past, DDoSecrets has released emails from convicted sex offender Jeffrey Epstein, as well as information from the Israeli Ministry of Justice and police departments in the US.

A hacker group calling itself the “Department of Peace” has claimed responsibility for obtaining and sharing the DHS data.

Since the database was first published, other groups have spread the information elsewhere, including on sites like icepi.cx. As a result, the information is unlikely to be entirely scrubbed from the internet.

The Trump administration, however, has taken an aggressive approach to online platforms purporting to contain information about ICE employees and other agents involved in immigration operations.

Last year, for instance, the US Department of Justice pressured tech giants like Apple to remove apps that track ICE agents, including one called ICEBlock.

The move sparked backlash from Democrats and free-speech advocates, who argued that the public has a right to report on ICE’s movements.

“Blocking access to these apps at the request of the federal government raises the distinct possibility that Apple is working with government to violate its customers’ First Amendment right to record and report on the activities of their government,” Democratic Representative Jamie Raskin wrote in a letter last year, asking Apple to review its decision.

Immigration agents, meanwhile, have taken several steps to conceal their identities during operations, including wearing face masks or street clothes.

That lack of transparency has spurred criticism from human rights groups and immigration advocates, who argue that they have little recourse to pursue allegations of excessive force or impropriety during ICE raids.

Since Trump was sworn in for a second term, at least 25 people have faced gunfire from immigration agents. Six of the shootings have resulted in deaths.

Other cases have involved alleged violations of civil liberties – including unlawful arrests and racial profiling – as well as controversial tactics like car ramming and tackling.

#168. Google stock price crashes and wipes $500 billion off company amid panic over AI

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Google’s share price has dramatically plunged amid a panic over AI.

Parent company Alphabet finished Thursday down more than 12 per cent, wiping $500 billion from its value. It represents the biggest ever crash in Google’s history, and second only among US listed companies to a $593 billion plunge in Nvidia’s value last year, after the rise of China’s DeepSeek AI brought concerns that US artificial intelligence companies could lose the race.

It comes after Alphabet’s latest results showed disappointing earnings in its search advertising business and the full scale of its vast spending on artificial intelligence. But shareholders are concerned that those huge bets may not come off, and that the company and the rest of the tech industry may not recover its investment in AI.

The results did show that Google is already earning from the AI boom, with revenues in its cloud business more than doubling. But investors are concerned that in the search business that still represents a large part of its earnings, Google is yet to see its embrace of AI results turning into profit.

Google and its chief executive Sundar Pichai have looked to aggressively embrace AI, after a relatively slow start against other companies such as OpenAI. It has begun to use its Gemini model to answer many search queries, for instance – despite the fact that advertising in search results has been a reliably huge business for parent company Alphabet.

"In a market ⁠worried about AI cannibalising the franchise, investors need to see search accelerating, not decelerating," said Dave Wagner, portfolio manager at Aptus Capital Advisors.

"Cloud is carrying the story, but until search shows AI is additive, the stock's probably going to trade lower."

The Mountain View, California company reported negative free cash flow of $9.1 billion, versus analyst expectations that it would come close to breaking even.

Aggressive efforts to boost cloud capacity and fund AI development led Alphabet to burn $5.9 billion last quarter, the first time it went into the negative in 22 years as a ⁠public company.

Total revenue missed Wall Street targets: revenue growth for Google Cloud outpaced expectations but was ​not enough ⁠to offset a $1.4-billion miss in advertising.

Alphabet's second straight quarter of negative cash flow was a sharp turn for a company that was long one of the tech sector's most prolific cash generators.

Moreover, Alphabet still plans to significantly increase its 2027 capital expenditures from this year's spending forecasts of $195 billion ⁠to $205 billion, Chief Financial Officer Anat Ashkenazi told analysts on a conference call.

While executives did not put a number on 2027 outlays, investors were nervous about ​a big jump.

"A $300-billion (capex) ⁠number could potentially scare the market because free cash flow now ‌is finally negative," said Jeremy Saltsburg, senior investment officer at Check Capital.

In contrast, rival Microsoft, which also reported results on Wednesday, saw shares rise more than 5%, fueled by positive free cash flow that outstripped analyst estimates.

Ashkenazi added that she expected near-term margin pressure from the use of third-party cloud capacity.

Net income available to ‌common Alphabet shareholders fell 25% to $26.17 billion, and adjusted earnings per share of $2.67 missed Wall Street targets ‌of $3.02.

The results reflect the huge costs of the AI buildout, with investors concerned both by potential overspending and the risk of falling behind rivals by not spending enough.

Total third-quarter revenue of $127.6 billion fell short of analysts' estimate of $128.2 billion, LSEG data showed. Revenue from ads came in at $82 billion, missing expectations of $83.4 billion.

Chief business officer Philipp Schindler attributed a larger-than-expected deceleration in search advertising growth to greater use of AI Mode, a chatbot interface ⁠for the search engine with which the company is still experimenting on ads.

Google would begin rolling out advertisements in AI Mode to international markets, Schindler told a conference call.

Alphabet attempted to offset the disappointing advertising performance by playing up its cloud computing unit, most notably by debuting Thomas Kurian, CEO of GoogleCloud, as a speaker on the earnings call.

Revenue at Google Cloud exceeded estimates and more than doubled to $30.71 billion in the third quarter ended September 30.

Kurian said sales of Google's tensor processing units (TPUs), which compete with Nvidia's GPU chips, had become a meaningful contributor to his business unit.

Alphabet started recognizing revenue from direct sales of what it called its "TPU Systems", spanning not only the chips but also associated hardware and software, last quarter.

But Ashkenazi cautioned that the buildout of TPU inventory would further place pressure on free ‌cash flow.

Google Cloud's backlog had grown to $561 billion and Alphabet expected to recognize more than 55% of it as revenue in the next 24 ​months, she added.

The third-largest cloud services provider globally after Amazon Web Services and Microsoft's Azure, Google Cloud has expanded enterprise commitments, supported by multi-year cloud and ‌AI infrastructure deals across cybersecurity, retail and financial services.

Alphabet, Microsoft, Amazon, and ⁠Meta are projected to collectively spend well over $700 billion in 2026 to expand AI data center footprint, chips and high-performance networking capacity.

In the face of supply ⁠bottlenecks for advanced silicon and data center power, Alphabet's investments in custom tensor processing units alongside third-party GPUs have helped expand its service delivery capabilities.

Earlier on Wednesday, Google sought to preempt the results and counter a perception ‌of losing out in the AI race ​by unveiling the new Gemini 4 Carbon model.

This meant the company was again "leading at ‌the frontier," CEO Sundar Pichai said on the call.

Afterwards, analysts at Bernstein asked ​in a note, "Did Google rush the deployment of Gemini 4 models as an offset to softer financial metrics? And are the models ready for commercial deployment?"
