#733. IN BRIEF: Putin hails Russia-Africa Summit, outlines prospects for cooperation

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

MOSCOW, October 29. /TASS/. The Third Russias-Africa Summit was a success and will give a strong impetus to the development of ties between Moscow and the African continent, Russian President Vladimir Putin said at a press conference following the summit.

The head of state highlighted the significant potential for the further development of relations between Russia and African countries, noting that Moscow welcomed the continent's aspiration to play a greater role in international decision-making. He also outlined his views on global security, dialogue with Europe and the lessons of world wars.

TASS has compiled the key takeaways from the Russian president’s statements.

The Third Russia-Africa Summit was a success and "will undoubtedly give a powerful impetus to the development of Russia-Africa cooperation."

"I would like to point out that this is the third Russia-Africa Summit. Holding such large-scale events has become a good tradition, which clearly indicates the determination of both Russia and African nations to continue boosting and strengthening multipolar ties in the spirit of true partnership and mutual respect for the sake of common growth and prosperity."

The motto of the Moscow-hosted summit was "For Peace, Security and Development," which set the tone and defined the topics of discussions.

"It is certainly satisfactory that the Moscow summit brought together 21 leaders of African nations, along with high-level delegations from most countries of the continent, and the heads of almost all leading African integration structures."

Russia and African countries adopted the 2027-2029 action plan for the Russia-Africa Partnership Forum: "The joint action plan through 2029 that we approved today contains an extensive list of specific goals aimed at developing Russia’s ties with the countries of the continent in the economic, trade, social and humanitarian spheres, as well as at implementing the decisions made at this summit."

As for economic goals, the action plan calls for increasing the volume of mutual imports and exports and improving their structure: "We are confident that the current level of the Russian-African trade turnover, which stands at $27 billion, is far from the limit. It’s safe to say that we are just starting out. However, if we look at our investment, it has reached billions of dollars, so we will definitely be able to increase trade."

The results of productive work at the Russia-Africa Summit are reflected in the Moscow Declaration: "This comprehensive document, designed for the long haul, reaffirms the commitment of all our countries to establishing a fair and democratic multipolar world order based on generally recognized principles of international law and the United Nations Charter."

It has been decided to continue holding Russia-Africa summits on a regular basis: "It’s important that all leaders have unanimously agreed to continue the practice of regular Russia-Africa summits."

The dates and venue of the next Russia-Africa sumit "will be agreed on in due course."

"It has become clear that the positions that Russia and Africa hold on most global and regional issues are not only very close but often coincide."

"Russia sincerely seeks to build a truly strategic, multifaceted partnership with Africa. And it’s important for us that we see the same constructive approach on behalf of our African friends to work together, hand in hand."

Russia has never been a colonial power, so the question about African territory potentially being used as an area for competition with other powers should not be directed to Moscow: "Throughout history, Russia has always viewed African countries only as friends. We have never pursued colonial policies; on the contrary, we helped African nations to break free from colonial dependence."

Russia and African countries "are natural allies in building a new, fairer multipolar world order."

"Of course, African countries seek to play a greater role in international decision-making, and we support that."

The African continent should definitely be represented on the UN Security Council: "Our African friends should figure out the way Africa will be represented there, either as a single voice, or African countries will rotate on the UN Security Council."

The UN needs reform but the process requires broad consensus: "The only question is that these decisions should be made on the basis of, as we say, broad consensus, and that we should not throw the baby out with the bathwater. Not to destroy universal international instruments, such as the United Nations, which are perhaps rightly criticized today, but are singularly unique."

Russia values the trust of African nations and seeks to take a careful approach to problems facing the region, including by "holding consultations with all parties involved in a conflict."

"Africa needs development and, as my colleagues have said, asks for nothing, only makes proposals and offers cooperation options. We are ready to boost our traditionally friendly relations on a completely new, modern soil."

At the meetings in the Kremlin, the African leaders supported the idea of "expanding the network of branches of Russian universities in their countries and creating new educational centers that promote the Russian language, science and culture: Several colleagues talked about opening Russian cultural centers."

Russia will increase the number of students from African countries enrolled in its universities, "including on a free basis."

Training African students is beneficial for Russia because later they will become the people Moscow can rely on in Africa: "I want to say that we in Russia need and benefit from such work [of educating African students]. Why? Because the people who worked with us, studied, received knowledge, acquired this knowledge, later use it in their countries. And, of course, they are guided by their partners in the Russian Federation. These are, relatively speaking, the people we rely on in these countries."

Russia will continue to supply grain to Africa, "we will do this as before, both contractually and free of charge."

Russia will easily adapt agricultural technologies to other climatic conditions, and then Africa will be able not only to produce but also to export its products: "Everything can be transformed, and Africa will not only be a food producer, it will not only fully cover its needs, but will also be able to export to foreign markets."

Russia and African countries intend to deepen their health protection partnership. In particular, joint exercises are planned to combat outbreaks of infections: "We will increase the supply of effective medicines, transfer mobile laboratories, and conduct joint exercises to respond to outbreaks of infections.

"I will mention, in particular, the decision taken to extend the Russian program of assistance to African countries to combat infections until 2029."

Back in the early 2000s, Russia was only a buyer of grain, "and now we top the world as a grain exporter."

The experience of Moscow medicine should not only be implemented in other regions of Russia but also offered for export: "We still have many healthcare problems in Russia. This is clear, and we must spread what is happening in Moscow to other regions of the Russian Federation. But why not offer the same to our friends in other countries, including Africa?"

Russia is one of the few countries in the world that can boast full digital sovereignty. Its IT companies successfully compete in foreign markets: "Russia, along with the United States, the People's Republic of China, is one of the few countries that has full sovereignty in digital technologies. Our platform solutions are very interesting and good. All this is developing not only on the territory of the Russian Federation. Our companies successfully compete in foreign markets.

"Our partners have great interest in small nuclear power plants. Our international competitors, almost everyone, are talking about their readiness to build low-power nuclear power plants, but so far no one has done anything. Only Russia has created such small nuclear power plants, which are working, even in the north they are working. Now we are actually completing two more nuclear power plants."

Of the 32 nuclear power units that are being built by third countries abroad, 28 are being built by the Russian company Rosatom: "The investments are significant. We are investing almost $25 billion in just one power plant in Egypt. There are other such examples. And we are ready to continue working in Africa."

Russia remembers all the tragic pages of history well, including the lessons of both world wars, and for its part does its best to ensure that nothing like this happens to humanity again: "We in Russia remember all this well, including the lessons of WWI and WWII. And we are doing everything to ensure that nothing like this happens on a global scale."

The West has not achieved its goal of inflicting a strategic defeat on Russia: "Many people are talking about preparing for war by 2030. Is this right or wrong? First of all, we are ready, but we don't want any war with Europe."

If it comes to a war between Russia and Europe, "there will be no winners. No one should forget about this."

Russia supports any steps to restore full-fledged relations with European countries: "Either with all of them together in the form of the well-known EU organization, or bilaterally. OK, we don't care, whatever they want."

"Regarding the telephone conversation with the representatives, or rather, with the leaders of the EU countries, with Finland and with Norway. They immediately stressed at the beginning of the conversation that they do not represent the EU as an organization, they are calling me in a national capacity, as they say."

The telephone conversation with Finnish President Alexander Stubb and Norwegian Prime Minister Jonas Gahr Store was initiated by them: "The conversation took place on their initiative, indeed.

"I know that there was criticism of them later, but it's not our business to assess whether it was correct or incorrect. I think it's right [that Stubb and Store called]. After all, there are the people in Europe who do not want to inflict a strategic defeat on Russia, as some do, but rather want to establish some kind of constructive dialogue."

Russia does not want a situation similar to the Strait of Hormuz and the Persian Gulf one to develop in the Baltic Sea. The issue of security in the Baltic was raised during the telephone conversation with Stubb and Store, who hope that all participants in international communication will comply with international maritime law: "We support this approach. Can it be different? Otherwise, there will be a situation similar to the situation in the Strait of Hormuz. Does anyone want the same situation to develop in the Baltic Sea as in the Persian Gulf or the Strait of Hormuz? I don't know. We don't want that."

Moscow is ready to respond to any challenge in the Baltic Sea but does not want to create tension there: "We do not want this. But we will respond to any challenge."

#103. Alaska governor orders probe into Democratic campaign's alleged links to same-name candidate

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Republican Governor Mike Dunleavy said he was acting on claims in a news report

The Republican governor of Alaska has ordered an investigation into an alleged "scheme" which led to two men named Dan Sullivan standing in the state's US Senate race.

Governor Mike Dunleavy said he was responding to a New York Times (NYT) story, external which said the team of Democratic candidate Mary Peltola worked with people who tried to recruit spoiler candidates in the Senate race.

The governor's post on X said he would investigate "whether this scheme violated Alaska's laws". He did not say which laws he had in mind.

Dan S Sullivan, the incumbent Republican senator running for re-election, and allies have accused the Democrats of trying to confuse voters. Peltola's team deny any wrongdoing.

"The Alaskans for Mary campaign has no involvement with either Sullivan campaign," the Peltola campaign said in a statement.

They say they did not encourage the second man, Dan J Sullivan, a retired teacher who lives in a remote fishing village, to stand. He has denied any links with the Peltola campaign.

The Alaska race is considered by analysts to be one of the tightest of the upcoming US midterm elections, which will decide which party controls Congress. Americans head to the polls across the country on Tuesday.

Dunleavy's statement, which did not name the Peltola campaign, said the NYT reporting had supported findings by the state Division of Elections earlier this year.

"I am therefore directing the Department of Public Safety to investigate whether this scheme violated Alaska's laws and whether criminal charges or civil action are warranted," he wrote. "The evidence will determine what happens next."

The NYT report alleged that people connected to Peltola's campaign, as well as senior aides at the Democratic Senatorial Campaign Committee (DSCC), worked to try to recruit Sullivan the schoolteacher to stand in the Senate race.

This was part of a broader effort to encourage other would-be "spoiler" candidates to enter the race and dilute the support of the sitting Republican senator, the newspaper reported.

The paper said it had seen text messages and interviewed 12 people who participated in or were told of the plan, or were would-be candidates who the team tried to recruit. The BBC has not independently verified the NYT's reporting.

"The DSCC is not involved with Dan J. Sullivan," the non-incumbent candidate, said Monica Robinson, a committee spokesperson, in a statement.

The ballot paper is set to list the two men as Dan S Sullivan, who will be described as the incumbent Republican senator, and Daniel J Sullivan Jr.

Senator Dan Sullivan told Fox News on Wednesday night that Peltola should "come clean" and described it as a scandal of the highest order.

His fellow Republican senator in Alaska, Lisa Murkowksi, accused the Democratic campaign of engaging in a "pattern of lies". President Donald Trump has called for Peltola to be barred.

Previous efforts by the Republicans to remove the non-incumbent Sullivan from the ballot have already been rejected by the Alaska Supreme Court which found no state laws had been broken by him running in the race.

Though officials at the state and federal level have called for investigations in the wake of the New York Times reporting, election law experts said it was unlikely to dislodge the second Dan Sullivan from the ballot and it was unclear at this point if any state laws had been violated.

"It turns out that convincing somebody to run for office is not a crime, even if their name happens to be the same as an incumbent," said Justin Levitt, an election law expert at Loyola Law School and a former justice department official.

The non-incumbent Sullivan had a constitutional right to run and have his name on the ballot, added Ruth Greenwood, an election expert at Harvard Law School.

Trump administration sues to block certification of upcoming Alaska election

Alaska's Democratic Senate candidate pauses midterms campaigning after car crash

The Trump administration filed a federal lawsuit last week aimed at preventing the certification of the result if the Trump-backed Sullivan loses, arguing that the presence of both of them on the ballot could confuse voters.

On Wednesday, the justice department amended its lawsuit to include the Times reporting, citing significant sections of the story as well as Dunleavy's announcement.

Some legal experts expressed scepticism of the merits of the lawsuit, and their chances of success given Alaska's courts had already allowed the second Sullivan to remain on the ballot.

The actual legal arguments the justice department made were a "frivolous theory," Rick Pildes, an election law expert at New York University, told the BBC.

He noted the lawsuit did not cite a specific provision that the government wanted to enforce, merely a general preamble taken from a sweeping US voting law.

Alaska election officials asked a federal court to dismiss the lawsuit "because the complaint has no legal basis".

The justice department has pledged continued action in the wake of the New York Times' reporting.

"They are cheating, let's call it what it is," Attorney General Todd Blanche said on Fox News on Wednesday.

In a series of Truth Social posts, Trump too pointed to the report and made a variety of allegations against Peltola, who he argued should be removed from the ballot. He repeated his endorsement for Dan S Sullivan, the incumbent Republican senator, writing: "Dan is a GREAT man, has ALWAYS delivered for Alaska."

Alaska's ranked-choice voting system means that voters could select both Sullivans if they are unsure at the ballot box.

The battle threatens to delay the full picture of nationwide results following the midterm elections. The ultimate question of which party controls Congress will have a significant bearing on the remaining two years of Trump's presidency.

A statement from an Alaskans for Mary spokesperson, written prior to Dunleavy's call for an investigation, said: "Mary got in this race because Alaskans deserve a champion who is fighting to lower their costs and put Alaska first, and no tweet or threat from any DC politician will keep her away from that fight.

"The Alaskans for Mary campaign has no involvement with either Sullivan campaign."

In a statement given to US media, the DSCC likewise denied that it had been involved in any such scheme.

Peltola earlier this week paused campaigning activities in order to recover after she and her family sustained "minor injuries" in a car crash.

#734. Live: Federal government launches probe into future of Australia Post

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The inquiry will examine if the postal service is meeting the needs of Australians.  (ABC News: Christopher Gillette)

Communications Minister Anika Wells says a parliamentary inquiry will examine whether Australia Post is keeping pace with Australians' needs for the future.

Choose what information you see below by using filters

A very quick parliamentary inquiry has been launched into new legislation handing Defence new powers to manage drones flying over its bases.

Drones are a rapidly growing problem for Defence, which yesterday told Estimates it is seeing hundreds of incidents each year where drones are flown into, or over, sensitive bases.

A series of incidents at one of the Air Force's most sensitive bases caused alarm earlier this year, when drones were seen flying over Williamtown, near Newcastle, which houses much of Australia's fleet of f-35 fighter jets.

Reports that staff on the base were instructed to close the blinds prompted questions over whether the ADF was capable of, or even allowed to, take down drones flying over its bases.

Legislation introduced to parliament this week aims to remedy that, giving the ADF the legal authority to "conduct counter-measures"—that is, take down—drones if it needs to.

Hearings are already underway this morning, with Defence officials up first, and companies like DroneShield up later on.

Australia's peak trucking and road freight bodies are urging the government to reinstate fuel tax relief.

This week's September inflation figures came in hotter than expected, with soaring fuel prices contributing to the rise.

The treasurer has so far ruled out repeating the temporary cuts to the fuel excise and heavy vehicle road user charge, which were implemented earlier this year.

But NatRoad CEO Warren Clark says the government should bring the road user charge down to zero until the end of January.

"What it does is give small businesses the cash flow injection, the cash flow help that it needs to actually remain in business through that really difficult and challenging time," Clark tells AM.

The Australian Trucking Association's Mathew Munro agrees, saying that with diesel prices once again on the rise, targeted relief must be back on the table for the industry.

"While the government can't do anything about the war in Iran, they certainly can do something about the taxation measures that they apply," the CEO says.

"We think that given the circumstances now are comparable to what they were early on in this crisis, we think that relief is now warranted again."

A Cairns man has been charged with threatening a federal parliamentarian on social media, marking the latest crackdown by federal authorities on digital abuse aimed at public figures.

The arrest comes just over a week after an alleged attack on Independent MP Helen Haines shocked the nation and highlighted the volatile environment facing Australia's elected representatives.

An Australian Federal Police statement said one of their National Security Investigations (NSI) teams executed a search warrant at the 53-year-old's Earlville home.

He was served a Notice to Appear in court for using a carriage service to menace, harass, or cause offence under section 474.17(1) of the Commonwealth Criminal Code.

The charge carries a maximum penalty of five years’ imprisonment.

The man is scheduled to appear in the Cairns Magistrates Court on December 3.

Labor MP Susan Templeman, chair of the communications, arts and sport committee, says the probe into Australia Post announced today will have a broad remit to look at the role the service plays in communities across the country.

Templeman said the committee would examine its ongoing sustainability and accessibility in both metropolitan and regional Australia.

"It's an absolutely vital part of all our communities … vital for business, and vital for our economy," she said.

The committee will consider growing competition from other parcel carriers and what that means for Australia Post's long-term future, while also considering how the service can diversify and what additional offerings it could provide.

It will also look at the reliability of postal services, particularly for small businesses that rely on the network to reach customers.

One Nation Senator Sean Bell has told News 24 the prime minister shouldn't be in charge of staffing levels for other parties.

One Nation isn't happy with Anthony Albanese after he apparently offered MP David Farley a $200,000 staffing allocation.

One Nation Leader Pauline Hanson, as well as a number of other parliamentarians, including former Labor senator Fatima Payman, have been pleading with the PM to bump up their staffing levels, which he has so far refused to do.

Bell says Albanese is playing "dirty games".

"This is all occurring because the prime minister is desperate," he says.

"They've got a budget that's a disaster, a treasurer under pressure, and the prime minister himself is under an incredible amount of pressure because he's been caught abusing the grants program".

The CSIRO has told a Senate estimates hearing that job losses are a part of the organisation's efforts to stay "contemporary and modern".

The science agency has slashed hundreds of jobs in recent years and says it is consulting on changes that could affect another 86 full-time jobs in its biosecurity and technology research units.

CEO Doug Hilton said the units could potentially be merged or transferred to make room for reinvestment in other research areas.

"There are opportunities to invest in areas that we think could generate more impact for the community," he said.

The reinvestment process could create 150 new roles, but Mr Hilton said it was not always possible to transfer staff between units.

He said that, for the past 20 years, the CSIRO had usually conducted an annual review cycle across its research portfolio, a process that could result in redundancies.

Deputy Opposition Leader Jane Hume says there is broad Coalition support for free trade, despite internal debate over the EU Free Trade Agreement.

The Nationals are arguing Australia should hold out for a better deal, but some Liberals say support for free trade is a core economic principle of their party.

Hume says the EU deal is simply "not fair" in its current form.

"It exposes some of our most important in industries to some unfair competition... and weakens our sovereignty by making international agreements legally binding," she told Radio National Breakfast.

Trade Minister Don Farrell told the National Farmers Federation last night that it was essentially this deal or no deal.

Hume says while the Coalition wants a "foot in the door" in Europe, it cannot come "at any price".

Labor has denied Prime Minister Anthony Albanese is trying to woo One Nation MP David Farley by offering him an extra staff member.

One Nation leader Pauline Hanson yesterday criticised the offer as an attempt by the government to "exert undue political influence" and split her party.

Farley received a letter from Albanese this week authorising him to hire an additional staffer on a $200,000 salary package, an "unsolicited" offer the Farrer MP has rejected.

Appearing on Channel 7, Employment Minister Amanda Rishworth denied the offer was an attempt to lure Farley over to Labor.

"Quite frankly, I think Pauline is jumping at shadows. If she's already having trouble keeping her party together, well that says something about her," she says.

"Just to be clear, lower house members wouldn't be doing their job if they were not meeting with ministers and meeting with the prime minister."

Trade Minister Don Farrell delivered some blunt hard truths to the National Farmers Federation last night, arguing an Australia-EU free trade agreement is vital to the nation's economic prosperity and could generate $10 billion in additional trade in its first year.

The National Farmers Federation have been some of the most vocal opponents to the FTA.

"In a world of increasing geopolitical uncertainty, when protectionism and tariffs are barriers to growth, we cannot afford to close ourselves to the world," Farrell said.

He told farmers that despite calls from the Coalition, the deal was as good as it was going to get and was not open to renegotiation.

"Take a look at the TV right now. Protests in France, far right governments in Germany, protestors occupying the Colosseum in Italy," he said.

"There is no way [Nationals leader] Matt Canavan can get a better deal".

Farrell acknowledged that beef and lamb industries wanted more out of the deal but said he had to represent all Australian industries, 95% of which say they support the agreement.

"I cannot in good conscience say that just because one sector didn't get everything they wanted, the entire economy gets nothing," he said.

The Albanese government is launching a parliamentary inquiry in a bid to shore up the long-term viability of Australia Post.

The 217-year-old institution has faced challenges in recent times, with falling foot traffic at some post offices and a drop in letters.

Last financial year, there were about 1.42 billion letters sent through Australia post — but that was down 14.7 per cent from the year before.

Communications Minister Anika Wells says changing consumer behaviour and commercial conditions posed significant challenges to the sustainability of Australia Post.

"That's why I've asked the House committee to explore how we can better support a sustainable and accessible postal service that delivers for all Australians," she said.

An Australia Post spokesperson said it looked forward to engaging constructively in the inquiry.

"For several years, we have highlighted the growing challenges facing Australia Post, which are shared by postal organisations around the world," they said.

"We welcome the government's action to address these challenges and support our long-term sustainability."

The inquiry is due to report back by the middle of next year.

I'm Holly Tregenza, joining you live from Parliament House in Canberra, here and ready to bring you all of today's federal politics news.

#745. Live: ASX set to edge up despite $US500b Alphabet share slump overnight

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Australian share market is likely to rise modestly on opening this morning, with ASX 200 futures up 15 points overnight.

Tech giant Google and its owner, Alphabet, lost more than half a trillion US dollars amid worries AI would subsume its advertising business, the largest one-day fall in the company's history.

See how the trading day unfolds on our blog.

Disclaimer: this blog is not intended as investment advice.

Choose what information you see below by using filters

I noticed in Googles result that they are claiming 1 billion (?) users of Gemini. Is there any further detail on how they came to that number? Are they counting Gmail/google related accounts? Because while I have a google account via Gmail, I don't use Gemini unless google forces through search results.

You'll be pleased to know Allan, you're not counted. According to Google's official announcement and tech reports, the 1 billion monthly active user (MAU) milestone tracks individuals who deliberately open the dedicated Gemini app or go to google.com to type a prompt, use voice features, or launch Gemini Live.

Morningstar's Lochlan Halloway writes that Anthropic's IPO could "be the biggest float ever, surpassing the record SpaceX set in June."

"Most of us know Anthropic through its Claude model, or perhaps through the software bloodbath it set off in February this year. Claude proved it could write plausible-looking software in minutes, at a fraction of the cost and with a fraction of the expertise, and the “SaaSpocalypse” ensued. The Australian tech index is still about a third below its August 2025 peak, and the US software applications index about a fifth below its own. It was such a fundamental challenge to the software business model that we rethought moats across our technology coverage, and of the 132 companies we reviewed, we downgraded the moat rating on 40," he said.

Halloway says at its proposed valuation it "would be worth almost as much as everything listed on the ASX put together." Not bad when you consider it was only founded in 2021 with Claude launching in 2023.

"But look at what Anthropic has done in that time. It went from US$1 billion of annual revenue to US$10 billion in about a year. Across nearly two decades of SEC filings, the only companies I could find that made the same leap as quickly were the Covid vaccine makers. By comparison, Google took three years to go from US$1 billion to US$10 billion, and Facebook four."

Staggering figures but dare I mention the b-word? (*insert bubble emoji*).  And can this kind of profit and growth continue?

According to Halloway, NYU professor Aswath Damodaran "puts the ceiling at the world’s total wage bill, about US$26 trillion a year" while Anthropic is even more generous with itself, estimating $40 trillion a year ("the work done by knowledge workers")...  One wonders if they've used AI...

But even if there is plenty of room to grow, will Claude be the model that wins? OpenAI, Google, Microsoft, Meta and SpaceX are all chasing the same customers, and we don’t know how this market shakes out.

The next question, Holloway asks, is who makes the money, is it Anthropic or its suppliers, like cloud computing companies (e.g., Amazon or Google) or chipmakers (e.g., Nvidia and TSMC).

Airlines show how that can play out. They are the ones selling to us, but what they sell is close to a commodity, and they earn the lowest returns in the aviation supply chain. The suppliers with few rivals, such as the engine and parts makers and the booking systems, earn far more. As Richard Branson once quipped, the fastest way to become a millionaire is to start out as a billionaire, and go into the airline industry.

The question is — can you lock customers in so a new agent isn't just a browser tab away?  I'm thinking Apple here, which prioritises keeping everything in its ecosystem and makes it hard to switch between.  As someone who keeps it casual with my AI agents, it's evident this will be a big challenge for Anthropic. Not least because you can ask ChatGPT, for example, to create an exportable file of everything it knows about you and then give it to another search engine.  I typically do this monthly and then share the data around with my various agents.

Halloway has some ideas for how Anthropic, or OpenAI, could create a 'moat' that would protect against this and encourage loyalty/give the model a differential:

"The first is a kind of network effect, in which the product gets better the more it is used. The more people use Claude to write code, the more Anthropic learns about what works. That makes its models and tools better, which brings in more users, who teach it more again.

H&M has paid $138,600 in penalties for allegedly selling more than 4,000 items of children’s clothing that didn't adequately label fire warnings, according to the ACCC.

The commission issued the clothing retailer with seven infringement notices for the alleged breaches of the Australian Consumer Law.

The standard requires children’s nightwear to carry an appropriate fire hazard label - either "high fire danger" or "low fire danger" depending on the composition of the garment and its flammability test results, according to the ACCC's statement.

The images of the recalled clothing items are available here.

HSBC's Paul Bloxham says the RBA has shifted from prioritising employment to tackling inflation. It has had a 2–3% inflation target since 1993 (I remember learning about it in high school in the early 2000s) but in recent years Bloxham says it prioritised keeping the economy in full employment over that target.

"Recent public commentary from senior RBA officials supports the idea that the RBA is more concerned about high inflation, is worried about its persistence, and seems to have a stronger proclivity to take policy action to deal with it," Bloxham says.

All signs point to a hike again come Tuesday — economists were pricing it in at 90% after inflation came in at 4.3% year-on-year earlier this week, walloping the target. If it's 25 basis points again, that would take the cash rate to 4.85%, figures we haven't seen since the GFC.

"We expect the RBA to pause after this, as the economy goes into a downswing: we see a recession as a 50:50 chance", Bloxham says.

"Offshore events, including the ongoing Middle East and Russia/Ukraine conflicts, have seen a further rise in input costs, such as refined oil products, like diesel, petroleum and plastics. Weak local productivity growth has meant that even only weak economic growth has left local inflation that is too high …

"The longer inflation is above target, the more likely it is that inflation expectations become unanchored," Bloxham says.

The RBA must weigh competing forces, including softening jobs data and falling house prices. Bloxham says inflation will trump these concerns for now.

"We expect the hikes to see growth near stall-speed in 4Q26 and 1Q27, and to force the unemployment rate higher, to 5.1% by early 2026. We expect the RBA to pause at 4.85% after its November hike, although there is still some risk of a follow-up hike in February. Our central case sees an extended hold at 4.85%, with cuts not arriving until 3Q27."

It's not often you see a 12% drop on shares like Alphabet, but tank they did today.

It comes after the Google parent-company reported disappointing results in search advertising and a massive acceleration in cash burn as it poured resources into building out AI.

"In a market worried about AI cannibalising the franchise, investors need to see search accelerating, not decelerating," said Dave Wagner, portfolio manager at Aptus Capital Advisors.

An area of success was Google Cloud — revenues more than doubled.

Its cash flow was car crash territory, more than $9b in the red — analysts had expected it to break even. It was spending big to boost cloud capacity and develop its AI Gemini, almost $6b last quarter.  Reuters reports this is the first time it went into the negative in 22 years as a public company.

Markets are now pricing an 85.6 per cent chance the Reserve Bank of Australia will raise rates by 25 basis points when it meets next Tuesday.

Communication Workers Union (CWU) National Secretary Shane Murphy said ensuring Australia Post is set up to deliver an accessible, affordable and reliable postal service for decades to come was crucial.

"There's no question that when it comes to postal services, demand has changed. Australia Post needs to change with it, but that change needs to strengthen the service, not diminish it," he said.

"Uniform pricing and service obligations underpin the integrated network that ensures Australians can participate equally in the national economy, regardless of where they live or operate a business from."

It's pointing to a modestly higher open of trade.

Gavin Friend Senior Markets Strategist NAB says a"fresh wave of anxiety over AI" has followed disappointing third-quarter earnings from Google parent company Alphabet.

Q3 earning news from Alphabet revealed disappointing search and advertising sales,

The results sent tech shares down with the tech-heave Nasdaq down 1.5 per cent and helping fuel a further push into the safety of bonds.

The Albanese government is launching a parliamentary inquiry in a bid to sure up the long-term viability of Australia Post.

The 217-year-old institution has faced challenges in recent times, with falling foot traffic at some post offices and a drop in letters.

Last financial year, there were about 1.42 billion letters sent through Australia Post — but that was down 14.7 per cent from the year before.

Communications Minister Anika Wells says changing consumer behaviour and commercial conditions pose significant challenges to the sustainability of Australia Post.

"That's why I've asked the house committee to explore how we can better support a sustainable and accessible postal service that delivers for all Australians," she said.

"For several years, we have highlighted the growing challenges facing Australia Post, which are shared by postal organisations around the world," the spokesperson said.

We've had a flurry of central bank meetings this week, with the Bank of Canada lifting its benchmark rate for the first time in three years to 2.5%, while the Reserve Bank of New Zealand surprised markets by staying on hold at 2.75% and the US Fed surprised no-one by doing the same to keep rates at 3.75-4%.

There's an interesting dichotomy between Google parent company Alphabet's third quarter results and their disastrous reception — a 12.6%, roughly $US500 billion share value wipe-out — and rival Microsoft.

#445. New Baha'i memoir recounts life inside Iran's prisons

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Prison air is “fetid” and “suffocating,” Mahvash Sabet writes in her new memoir, Open Wide the Doors, offering a rare account by a Baha’i prisoner of life inside Iran’s prisons and the persecution of the country’s religious minority.

The book, launched in London on October 21, was written inside prison “on scraps of newspaper and tissue paper and whatever else she could find,” according to Payam Akhavan, the international human rights lawyer who hosted the event.

The fragments were later smuggled out of Iran and assembled by Azita Mottahedeh and Bahiyyih Nakhjavani, the book’s translators.

Sabet trained as a teacher and was a member of the “Yaran,” or “Friends” of Iran, a group tasked, with the government's knowledge, with caring for the Baha’i community’s basic needs.

More than 200 Baha’is were executed after the 1979 Islamic Revolution, and the community continues to face widespread discrimination and persecution.

After Sabet’s arrest in 2008, the other six members of the Yaran were also detained. All seven were jailed for a decade on charges including conspiring against national security. Their former lawyer, Nobel Peace Prize laureate Shirin Ebadi, has said no evidence was presented to substantiate the charges.

Released in 2017, Sabet was arrested again in 2022 and sentenced to another decade in prison alongside fellow former Yaran member Fariba Kamalabadi. She is currently on medical furlough but could be recalled to prison at any time.

The book offers an unflinching account of prison conditions. In solitary confinement, the stench is so “noxious” that Sabet tries to breathe through a slit in her door. Other prisoners’ faeces lie in piles in her cell, and cockroaches crawl across the floor.

Remembering hours of blindfolded questioning early in her detention, Sabet writes that “the hair rose on the back of my neck” each time her interrogator drew close.

But alongside the degradation and violence, the memoir describes Sabet’s efforts to preserve compassion for those around her, including fellow prisoners and even her jailers.

At the London launch, two former cellmates and another former prisoner spoke about Sabet’s influence on their lives.

Roxana Saberi, a journalist imprisoned in Evin in 2009, recalled being “dumbfounded” when Sabet and Kamalabadi told her they were “trying to turn their experiences [in prison] into an opportunity.”

As Sabet showed “kindness and compassion to everyone, from her fellow cellmates to her captors,” Saberi came to believe that such an approach could help lay the foundations for “democracy, freedom and understanding” in Iran.

One episode in the memoir captures that compassion. When an “emaciated” crack addict, with “a head like a prop for an anatomy class … eyes set back in deep rings of darkness,” reaches toward Sabet from a lower bunk, she climbs down to hold the woman’s hand.

Although Sabet has only just secured a bed in an overcrowded ward, she also holds the woman’s wounded, “festering” feet, describing how her heart breaks and fills with love.

Sabet does not conceal her own suffering. After a brutal interrogation in which she endures insults and blows from six “sweating, stinking” men, she recalls how “desperate” she had been for “fresh air.”

Iqan Shahidi, an Iranian Baha’i imprisoned between 2012 and 2017, described Sabet as “like a light in the darkness for me.”

Now an academic in the United Kingdom, Shahidi said he had studied at the informal Baha’i university where Sabet taught before her imprisonment.

The institution illustrated how Baha’is responded to persecution, he said. Rather than resorting to political confrontation or violence, “instead they built something,” providing education to young Baha’is barred from public universities.

“Mahvash kept everybody so hopeful through very simple things in life,” said Nazanin Zaghari-Ratcliffe, the Iranian-British former prisoner who spent six years in detention.

Every year in prison, Zaghari-Ratcliffe recalled, Sabet invited the entire ward to a Nowruz feast. Tables were pushed together, and Sabet supervised the careful slicing of potatoes for traditional Persian dishes.

She continued paying for the annual celebration even after her release, sending money to jailed activist and future Nobel Peace Prize laureate Narges Mohammadi.

For Zaghari-Ratcliffe, the gatherings represented something larger than a shared meal.

Iran’s political prisoners, drawn from different ethnic and religious communities and political backgrounds, were forced to live together for years despite their differences.

Sabet, she said, would “make sure that we [were] all part of the same community.”

“We are against this oppression that has put us in this place … regardless of our differences, we are all one.”

That commitment extended even to prison staff.

Sabet recounts returning to a jail where she had previously been held and receiving a warm welcome from guards. One officer, who tells her that “all my life I have been a good Muslim,” confesses that he has come to hate religion and works for the government only to support his family.

Sabet tries to reassure him, expressing her belief that Iran has a better future and that its people will one day rebuild the country together.

The officer says he wishes he could believe her.

“So allow yourself to,” Sabet replies, “instead of wasting energy on your doubts!”

In the book’s final pages, Sabet recalls an unexpected encounter in prison with Jamaloddin Khanjani, the eldest member of the Yaran.

“The sheer wonder of what had happened felt like a recompense for all the beatings, a reward after all that solitude; it was like breathing fresh air in the suffocating corridors of Evin.”

An Iranian cleric and former Revolutionary Guards member arrested during January's nationwide protests has been sentenced to death twice in separate cases, including for apostasy, US-based rights group HRANA reported Thursday.

The cleric, identified only as Mehdi, was sentenced to death alongside two other protesters, Amirreza Rezaei and Vahid Ghahremani, by Branch 23 of Tehran's Revolutionary Court, according to HRANA.

The charges against the three included allegedly carrying out operations on behalf of Israel, HRANA said. They were informed of their sentences in prison on Monday.

Mehdi, whose surname remains unknown, received a second death sentence from Iran's Special Clerical Court on a charge of apostasy. HRANA provided no further details about the circumstances of that case or his previous service in the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC).

All three were arrested separately by security forces during the nationwide protests in January. They were transferred from Greater Tehran Prison to Ghezel Hesar Prison in Karaj on Wednesday.

The sentences come amid warnings from the United Nations that Iranian authorities have intensified executions and prosecutions linked to the protests during the war with the United States and Israel.

UN Special Rapporteur Mai Sato told the General Assembly on Wednesday that at least 34 people had been executed in connection with the nationwide protests.

Sato cited one case in which 34 defendants were tried in just five days, with 12 sentenced to death and seven already executed. She raised concerns about mass trials, forced confessions and the lack of due process in capital cases.

"The war has provided cover for the authorities to intensify their crackdown and tighten the repression," Sato said, adding that protesters had been portrayed as agents of foreign powers and peaceful dissent prosecuted as a national security threat.

The protests began in late December with strikes by merchants in Tehran before spreading across the country. At least 36,500 people were killed during the subsequent crackdown by security forces, according to Iran International's reporting, while tens of thousands were arrested or summoned.

Some Iranian families are paying thousands of dollars to put their children in foreign online schools, buying an international education from home and stepping outside the Islamic Republic’s school system, despite the education ministry’s warnings.

The government-affiliated newspaper Iran reported on Thursday that institutions offering foreign curricula and diplomas from countries including Canada and Britain were attracting Iranian students.

A mother whose 15-year-old daughter attends an online high school based in Toronto told the newspaper her family pays $7,000 annually to prepare her daughter for admission to Canadian universities without leaving Iran.

Another father cited shortcomings in Iranian schools’ approach to practical skills and creative thinking as reasons for enrolling his son in a foreign online college alongside his education in Tehran.

Mohammad Salimi, head of the Education Ministry’s Center for International Affairs and Overseas Schools, warned that the foreign online institutions in question lack authorization to operate in Iran and that their diplomas are not recognized by the domestic education system.

Students attending only these institutions are not registered in Iran’s official education databases and could face difficulties continuing their studies domestically, Salimi told Iran daily.

For families planning to send their children to universities abroad, however, recognition by institutions in the destination country may matter more than approval from Iranian authorities.

A diploma’s lack of recognition in Iran does not necessarily make it invalid internationally. Its acceptance depends on the school’s accreditation and the admission requirements of individual universities.

The expansion of international online schools allows students to follow American and British curricula without relocating.

International Virtual Learning Academy (IVLA), a private American school, provides online education from elementary through high school, allowing students to select courses and progress at a pace suited to their circumstances.

Annual high school tuition starts at $2,679 for its basic program, rising to $3,799 or $5,279 for packages offering additional educational support.

Britain’s King's InterHigh offers live and recorded online classes, with subjects including computer science, economics, psychology and film studies.

For the 2026–2027 academic year, annual tuition is £7,250 for students aged 14 to 16 and £7,585 for the pre-university program covering three principal subjects.

Crimson Global Academy also provides American and British curricula online. Annual full-time tuition for its American high school program ranges from $22,375 to $25,500.

The differences show that foreign online schooling does not carry a uniform price. Costs vary according to grade level, course selection and the educational services provided.

Ideological education and alternative choices

Some families are turning to foreign education in a country whose school system was restructured around the Islamic Republic’s religious and political objectives after the 1979 revolution.

UNESCO has documented how Iranian textbooks were revised after the revolution to incorporate Islamic teachings, with religious and political content extending beyond religious education into subjects including history, social studies and Persian literature.

Iran’s Fundamental Reform Document of Education also identifies the cultivation of Islamic-Iranian identity and religious values among the education system’s central objectives.

In a study published in July 2025, education researcher Golnar Mehran examined Iranian social studies textbooks and the role of Islamization and politicization in their content.

By contrast, the international online schools examined offer greater flexibility in course selection, different academic pathways and preparation for universities abroad.

The YouTube generation and English-language learning

The spread of the internet and social media has also exposed Iranian students to English-language material and international educational resources from an early age.

YouTube, online gaming and English-language social media content have expanded opportunities to learn beyond formal classrooms, despite extensive internet filtering in Iran.

A 2024 survey by the Iranian Students Polling Agency, ISPA, found that roughly half of the university students surveyed used YouTube.

Another official survey published in 2026 found that around 23 percent of children and adolescents aged six to 15 used tools to circumvent internet restrictions.

A 2026 study involving 120 English-language learners in Tehran and Isfahan found that structured educational use of YouTube, Instagram, Telegram and WhatsApp over 12 weeks significantly improved English proficiency among participants in the experimental group.

Such resources can make international online education more accessible to students who develop sufficient English-language skills.

IVLA, for example, expects international applicants to have adequate English proficiency but does not require a language-test certificate at enrollment.

Dollar-priced education and a future beyond Iran’s job market

The cost of foreign online schooling means it is largely accessible to families with greater financial resources, some of whom may be considering educational and professional futures beyond Iran.

English-language instruction, exposure to international curricula and experience with remote learning could help prepare students for university education abroad and potential participation in the global labor market.

The growth of remote employment has also created opportunities for people to work for foreign companies or clients without emigrating. Language skills, digital proficiency and familiarity with international educational and professional environments can support access to such work.

However, Iran’s internet restrictions, difficulties receiving foreign payments because of limited access to international banking networks, and overseas employers’ hiring policies can still obstruct those opportunities.

More than 16.5 million students were enrolled in Iran’s education system at the beginning of the 2026–2027 academic year, but no comprehensive figures have been published on the number attending foreign online schools or whether enrollment is increasing.

It is also unclear how many intend to pursue education abroad and how many regard foreign courses as a supplement to their domestic schooling.

Although the newspaper Iran focused on tuition costs and the risks associated with diploma recognition, the parents it interviewed described different priorities, including alternative curricula and preparation for education overseas.

For families already investing substantial sums in international schooling, recognition by Iranian authorities may carry less weight than access to foreign universities, migration opportunities or employment beyond the domestic market.

The Education Ministry’s warning about unrecognized diplomas therefore addresses only part of a broader issue: how far Iran’s education system can meet the expectations of students increasingly using the internet, English-language resources and online education to explore academic and professional opportunities beyond the country’s borders.

Young Iranians are turning from opium, their parents’ drug, to cannabis, methamphetamine and a widening range of synthetic substances, treatment specialists and officials say, as unemployment, hardship and uncertainty about the future reshape the country’s drug use.

Opium is still common among older users, Tejarat News reported. The younger generation’s drugs go by other names: cannabis, known locally as gol (bud), and methamphetamine, known as shisheh (glass).

In July, the head of prevention at Iran's Drug Control Headquarters, Zahra Abedini, said the highest prevalence of drug use was among people aged 24 to 35.

She estimated the prevalence of drug use among university students at between 2.5 and 2.7 percent, although the figures were based on existing studies. A new nationwide survey is underway to provide updated data on addiction in Iran.

Kamran Fateh, an addiction specialist, told Tejarat News that drug consumption should not be viewed simply as a matter of personal choice.

Behind many cases of drug use lies a combination of social, economic and psychological factors, he said.

"A young person facing anxiety, unemployment, a sense of having no future, emotional setbacks, economic pressure or family tensions may look for a quick way to temporarily escape those pressures," Fateh added.

Cannabis use and the changing drug market

The Drug Control Headquarters' director of treatment has identified cannabis and hashish as among the country's most commonly consumed substances, warning about the rapid emergence of synthetic drugs and new psychoactive compounds.

One concern highlighted in the report is the growing normalization of cannabis use among young people, some of whom regard it as less dangerous than traditional narcotics or believe occasional consumption carries little risk of dependence.

Fateh warned that misconceptions about the safety of certain substances were among the biggest obstacles to prevention.

"When a substance is described as 'recreational,' 'herbal' or 'less harmful,' people's awareness of the risk of dependence decreases," he said.

Addiction treatment specialists in several provinces have reported cases of young people beginning with cannabis before experimenting with other substances, although such progression is not inevitable.

According to prices reported by addiction treatment specialists, a cannabis cigarette costs around 1.5 million rials ($0.55), while methamphetamine sells for approximately 2.5 million to 7 million rials ($0.92 to $2.59) per gram.

Heroin prices range from 2 million to 7 million rials ($0.74 to $2.59) per gram, compared with 1.21 million to 3 million rials ($0.45 to $1.11) for opium. The figures are estimates rather than official market rates.

Experts have also warned that newly emerging substances are increasingly marketed with claims of reducing stress, boosting energy, improving concentration or promoting weight loss.

Economic hardship and uncertainty fuel vulnerability

Fateh said the expanding illegal drug market was making prevention increasingly difficult as suppliers introduced new substances and combinations to meet changing demand.

He also said that families sometimes discover drug use only after occasional consumption has developed into a more serious problem, adding that punishment and intimidation are not necessarily effective responses.

"If society focuses only on 'saying no to drugs' but offers no solutions to problems such as unemployment, social isolation, depression, identity crises, economic pressure and a lack of healthy recreational activities, many of the underlying causes of drug use will remain," he said.

Fateh highlighted the relationship between economic hardship and addiction, saying that rising drug prices could drive dependent users toward cheaper, unfamiliar or more dangerous substances.

"If a dependent person does not have access to effective, affordable and accessible treatment, higher prices may even push them toward more dangerous alternatives," he said.

He argued that enforcement measures against drug suppliers alone would not resolve Iran's addiction problem without addressing the broader economic and social pressures facing younger generations.

As more Iranians build new lives abroad, many parents back home face a less visible consequence of migration: years without seeing their children, family relationships maintained through phone screens, and growing uncertainty about who will be there for them in old age.

For Maryam, a 68-year-old retiree in Tehran, that distance has become part of everyday life. Her son lives in Canada and her daughter in Germany, and she has not seen either of them in years.

“I know they're building their futures, and I'm happy for them,” she told Iran International. “But sometimes I just wish they were here, even for an ordinary dinner.”

Maryam says regular calls and photographs help her stay connected with her family, particularly her grandchildren. But watching them grow up from thousands of miles away is difficult.

“They know me through the phone,” she says. “I wish I could be more than just a face on a screen in their lives.”

Last winter, Maryam was hospitalized with kidney problems. Her children arranged help through relatives, and a family member accompanied her to medical appointments.

“My daughter was very worried, and I kept telling her everything was fine,” she recalls. “Sometimes, especially when I'm ill, I just wish we weren't so far apart.”

Caring for a spouse without the children nearby

In Karaj, 72-year-old Hamid has spent the past two years caring for his wife after a stroke left her with limited mobility.

Their two sons live in Sweden and Australia. Although they call regularly and provide financial support, most daily responsibilities fall on Hamid.

He describes the exhaustion of managing medication, medical appointments and household chores.

“Some people think you're fortunate when your children live abroad,” he says. “But when your wife needs help getting out of bed, what matters is having someone nearby.”

The family has hired a part-time caregiver, but Hamid still handles much of his wife's care.

“Sometimes I want to tell my sons how exhausted I am,” he says. “Then I think about how far away they are and wonder what they could possibly do.”

Hamid does not blame his children for emigrating, but says family life has changed.

“We're still very close, and we talk all the time. But it's different when you can't sit together in the same room.”

For Nahid, a 64-year-old widow in Tehran, the question is whether she should leave Iran herself.

Her only daughter has lived in London for eight years and has encouraged her mother to move closer.

“My daughter tells me to come and live with her, but I've spent my whole life here,” she says. “My sisters, my friends, all my memories are here. At the same time, every year that passes, I wish I could be closer to her.”

Nahid worries about losing her independence in a country where she does not speak the language fluently or have an established social circle.

But staying in Tehran also means facing old age far from her daughter.

“I don't want to become a burden to my daughter,” she says. “But I don't want to leave behind everything I've built here either.”

Phone and video calls have helped families separated by migration remain part of one another's lives despite years of living thousands of kilometres apart. They share news, celebrate birthdays and check on each other's health, sometimes communicating more frequently than families living in the same city.

But as parents grow older, that distance takes on a different meaning. A sudden illness, a medical emergency or the growing need for help with everyday tasks can expose the limits of even the closest long-distance relationships.

For children who have emigrated, Iran remains the place where their parents are growing old. For the parents who stayed behind, each passing year brings a difficult reality into sharper focus: the people they may need most are the ones living furthest away.

As growing numbers of Iranian women appear in public without headscarves, authorities are pursuing organizers of cultural and sporting events over alleged violations of mandatory hijab rules.

Tehran’s prosecutor’s office announced this week that it had opened a case against the organizers of Tehran Art Fair, a privately run event held at the Iran Mall commercial complex from September 20 to 23, citing “failure to observe legal and religious requirements.”

The organizers have been summoned to court, while the fair’s ticketing website and social media account have been blocked on the prosecutor’s orders.

The announcement did not specify which activities at the event, which featured visual arts, music, theater and performance art, were considered violations.

Images and videos showed numerous female visitors without headscarves, as well as women performing without hijab.

In one performance, two women sat on the floor as red yarn was wrapped around them in a spiderweb-like pattern. Their bodies were then decorated with flowers before two other women cut the threads with scissors, freeing them.

Another video circulating on social media showed a woman in a long black dress that covered her body but left her head uncovered as she moved to music.

The action against the art fair came amid similar proceedings over Fitex Tehran, a sports equipment exhibition where women performed fitness routines and acrobatics to music without headscarves.

The judiciary announced Tuesday that an unnamed fitness blogger had been arrested on charges of “offending public sentiment” and “propaganda against the system.”

Videos of the performances, in which women wore long-sleeved tops and leggings but no headscarves, circulated widely on social media.

The prosecutor’s office ordered the event’s social media account closed for publishing “criminal content” and said an arrest warrant had been issued for one of the exhibition’s organizers.

The event drew criticism from conservative media. Tasnim, affiliated with the Revolutionary Guards (IRGC), condemned what it called widespread “deviations” at Fitex in an October 25 report and urged sports authorities and other institutions to intervene.

State broadcaster IRIB also described the event as an example of “deviance” and called for judicial action.

Other cultural events have faced scrutiny over the past year, including music and theater performances and Tehran Design Week, held at the University of Tehran’s College of Fine Arts.

In some cases, judicial intervention has concerned interactions between women and men rather than hijab violations.

Last month, those involved in the play Tehran, Paris, Tehran/Bridge faced prosecution after a scene showed a female actor briefly resting her head on the chest of a male actor playing her brother.

The prosecutor’s office described the actors’ behavior as “contrary to tradition and propriety.”

Authorities have also taken action against venues hosting events attended by women without hijab.

Earlier this month, the IRGC-linked Fars News Agency reported that a banquet hall in the religious city of Qom had been sealed on the prosecutor’s orders after the wife of prominent soccer player Ali Daei attended a private company conference there without a headscarf.

A judicial case was also opened against the conference organizers, and the prosecutor warned that everyone involved would face action.

Since the 2022 Woman, Life, Freedom movement, which followed the death in custody of Mahsa Amini, street-level enforcement of compulsory hijab has declined.

Increasing numbers of women now appear in public without headscarves, wearing clothing other than the long coats and chadors traditionally expected by authorities. Women also exercise in public parks without covering their hair.

But mandatory hijab rules remain in force, including in schools, government institutions and universities, while organizers of public events continue to face legal pressure.

Women’s rights activists and opponents of mandatory hijab argue that the growing visibility of women without headscarves does not amount to freedom of choice as long as legal restrictions remain.

The recent prosecutions show how authorities continue to enforce those restrictions through legal action against organizers, performers and venues, even as uncovered hair becomes increasingly commonplace in public.

#247. College basketball season is here. The eligibility chaos playing out in courthouses comes with it

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Todd Golden had already retained enough talent for his Florida team to be a contender for its second national championship in three years. Then came another win, this one in a courthouse allowing guard Denzel Aberdeen to join the top-ranked Gators for his fifth college season.

“He has a great legal team that put an incredible argument forward, so we’re planning on having him and we feel good about it,” Golden said during Southeastern Conference basketball media days.

These days, leading a major college athletics program requires a touch of legal proficiency alongside the Xs and Os. Lawsuits tied to player eligibility are crawling through courts across the country. Some target the NCAA's new age-based eligibility model, others the question of whether even minimal involvement with a professional American team equates to losing the now-lucrative chance to play in college.

It’s not stopping with the start of basketball season, either. The only thing certain now is volatility, with rosters changing by the week, even the day, at the bang of a judge’s gavel. Consider it an added test for frustrated coaches who were already scrambling to fill rosters in the era of free player movement through the transfer portal.

Even Aberdeen’s case, after he received a favorable ruling in September, is under appeal from the NCAA. For now, he’s good to go for the Gators opener Monday night against No. 14 Miami.

“They’re not going to say, ‘Oh, we have to end it by November 2nd so there’s clarity for the season,’” second-ranked Duke coach Jon Scheyer said when broadly discussing the eligibility chaos. “I think these cases are going to go well into the season. I think no question you’re going to have cases where certain players get an injunction, they’re eligible for a certain period, and then they’re not. And so how is that viewed from an NCAA Tournament standpoint?

“It’s going to be (unresolved) going into the season, which is the worst thing for the sport. Your roster should be set now.”

Court fights continue to create a volatile landscape

The lawsuits have largely centered around the NCAA’s adoption of a five-year eligibility model that is due to be fully implemented next fall. It will allow players to have five years of eligibility with the clock starting when an athlete turns 19 or graduates from high school, whichever is earliest.

That quickly led to lawsuits from players in the high school class of 2022, who began their careers in the 2022-23 season and completed four seasons of eligibility but are seeking that fifth year.

Then there was the added wrinkle tied to a once-unthinkable notion: players leaving college, signing with pro teams and then returning to college.

Those storylines enveloped the start of football season in September. The most notable example was LSU’s showdown with the Southeastern Conference in a bid to add at least two players who signed with NFL teams as undrafted free agents. That ultimately led the Power Four conferences — the Atlantic Coast Conference, Big Ten and Big 12 being the others — to toughen up rules banning professional athletes from returning to college.

Now men’s and women’s basketball face the same urgent questions.

As of Thursday morning, the NCAA said there have been 200 lawsuits filed since the June approval of the age-based eligibility model with approximately 850 plaintiffs across all sports seeking eligibility for the 2026-27 season. More than half of those lawsuits (about 115) are tied specifically to the new eligibility rules, the rest to questions over involvement with professional teams along with all other case-by-case complaints.

There are 362 men’s and 62 women’s basketball players in the total plaintiff count. Of that group, nearly half (171 men, 30 women) have received some type of relief from the courts allowing them to play while the rest are currently ineligible.

Coaches face uncertainty as to what's next

Yet things can change rapidly, with players moving from one category to the other. Some are tied to lawsuits in more than one state. Some athletes who lost in federal court turned to state judges with their arguments. There is even a College Sports Litigation Tracker website.

Louisville women's coach Jeff Walz added forward Amiya Joyner as a fifth-year player for the seventh-ranked Cardinals after a favorable ruling in a Louisiana case. He figures just let her practice and play until told otherwise.

“She's good according to, I've got my ChatGPT, my algorithm,” Walz quipped. “And I just say: when anything new comes up, tell me. And it told me ... that her court case was OK, I guess.”

Ohio Gov. Mike DeWine issuing an executive order allowing a fifth season of eligibility for college players in his state from the 2022 class. That decision impacted some cases even beyond Ohio’s borders and prompted the NCAA to sue DeWine.

All of this is set amid the backdrop of efforts to pass the NCAA-backed Protect College Sports Act, a bill that has passed the U.S. Senate but is uncertain to pass the House. It includes a five-year eligibility provision along with some protections for the NCAA against lawsuits.

“You don’t know if guys who will be playing in November will be playing in December,” said Minnesota men’s coach Niko Medved, who has had leading scorer Cade Tyson practicing with the team in hopes of returning for a court-granted fifth year. “That’s where it gets really tricky.”

Pending cases has some players facing an uncertain future

The cases keep churning with openers looming.

Just Wednesday, a Louisiana judge granted a request by lawyers for St. John’s transfer RJ Luis Jr. to temporarily block the SEC from punishing Luis if he’s added to LSU’s roster. Luis was briefly under contract with two NBA teams but never played in a game.

Meanwhile, forward Babacar Sane of No. 13 St. John’s faces a strange deadline. He played in the G League and signed a non-guaranteed training camp contract with the Utah Jazz in 2024 but never played. He has a temporary restraining order from a New York court allowing him to compete through a Nov. 9 hearing, guaranteeing him only two games.

Similar uncertainty follows N.C. State big man Ven-Allen Lubin and Texas Tech forward Darrion Williams; they’re plaintiffs in a Texas case seeking a fifth year of eligibility for this season. They’re clear for now, though the case is scheduled for trial Dec. 14, six weeks into the season.

“Once this was granted, I was just really grateful and I want to take advantage of this opportunity,” Lubin said, adding when asked about the cases nationally: “There’s just a lot so I can’t really keep up.”

Some cases check multiple boxes like No. 17 Kansas adding former Purdue star Trey Kaufman-Renn, who played four college seasons before being a second-round NBA draft pick in June and competing in the NBA Summer League.

The 2021 high school graduate redshirted his first season and didn’t sign an NBA player contract, according to his complaint that ultimately led to a preliminary injunction in his favor.

For now, he's set to play. That's about all anyone can promise.

“I find it very, very hard to consider how some people could be eligible and some people not eligible with the same type of lawsuit, but the eligibility is determined strictly where they filed the case,” Kansas Hall of Fame coach Bill Self said. “I don’t see how the way somebody was represented in a judge’s view in Colorado has anything to do with Ohio and why there could be more slack cut in another state or another county in which it’s filed and that kind of stuff.

“I do believe universal is the way that it should be, and if anybody’s eligible, I think they should all be eligible if they’re fighting for the same reasons.”

AP Basketball Writer Dave Skretta and AP freelance writer Luke DeCock contributed to this report.
