#5. Israeli opposition accuses Netanyahu of trying to 'delegitimise' election

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Activists with a placard saying "Love your neighbour" urge opposition figure Gadi Eisenkot to try to form government that includes Arab-led parties

The main Israeli opposition leader Gadi Eisenkot and Arab-led party leaders have denounced Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu for alleging foreign interference following the election which saw his coalition lose its majority.

Netanyahu's Likud accused Israel's Arab-led parties of "stealing" the election. Home security agency Shin Bet is investigating whether they received illegal funding from Qatar and Turkey.

Qatar's foreign ministry denied the "ridiculous and dangerous" allegations and accused Israeli politicians of conducting a disinformation campaign against Doha.

Final results confirmed that neither Netanyahu's bloc nor the Jewish opposition had won a majority in parliament.

The two Arab party lists - The Joint List and the Ra'am - defied expectations to win a record 19 seats in the 120-seat Knesset following a surge in turnout among Arab voters.

That positions them as potential kingmakers, with Netanyahu's bloc winning 53 seats and his rival Gadi Eisenkot's bloc securing 48.

On Thursday Likud alleged that voters had been "bribed" to vote for Arab parties, after the head of the Central Elections Committee (CEC) asked the Shin Bet security service to investigate "serious suspicions" of foreign interference.

Both Arab parties strongly rejected the claim, with the Arab-majority Joint List alliance saying it was baseless.

The row is being seen by Netanyahu's critics as an attempt to delegitimise the Arab parties, thereby making it politically impossible for Eisenkot to join forces with them - which is currently his bloc's only route to power.

Arab-led parties including the Joint List headed by Yousef Jabareen (C) won a record 19 seats and could play a kingmaker role

Israeli media reported that the CEC had been presented with a claim that Qatar had provided funding aimed at increasing voter turnout in Arab communities. Qatar's Foreign Ministry "categorically" denied the claims.

Netanyahu and his Likud Party framed the allegations as a "political earthquake" and an "an attack on democracy", aimed at removing their right-wing government.

But the Shin Bet later clarified that their investigation did not implicate an entire community who had voted legally, while acting CEC Director General Dean Livne said there was "no basis for allegations of systematic violations of election integrity."

Eisenkot, who was briefed on the investigation, said "it is too early to determine what happened, or if anything happened at all," and accused Netanyahu of running "an orchestrated and organized campaign to delegitimize the election results".

Balad chairman Sami Abu Shehadeh, who is part of the Joint List, dismissed the allegation, saying: "The losing side is constructing a racist blood libel and calling on the security agencies to use force in order to overturn the election results."

Netanyahu's attacks on Arab voters and parties have been ongoing for days. He spent much of election day posting on social media that high Arab turnout is an "emergency" and a "matter of life or death" for the Israeli right. He also shared AI-generated images of Arab voters at polling stations, in an attempt to encourage right-wingers to get out and vote.

Netanyahu shares AI image on day of Israeli election

Arab Israelis, many of whom refer to themselves as Palestinian citizens of Israel, make up about one fifth of the country.

For years, many boycotted Israeli elections, saying voting would legitimise a system that they say treats them as second-class citizens. But their turnout on Tuesday was inspired by a desire to oust the most right-wing governing coalition in Israel's history, led by Netanyahu.

The final results offer only three plausible options for a government to be formed: a national unity government involving Netanyahu and Eisenkot, a right-wing coalition involving Netanyahu and defectors from Eisenkot's bloc, or an Eisenkot-led government supported by the Arab parties.

Eisenkot and every member of his bloc bar one has previously refused to enter government with Arab parties, but speculation has swirled in recent days that an agreement could yet be reached.

A snap poll conducted by Hebrew University showed that the most popular option for voters from every party in the opposition bloc was for Eisenkot to form a minority government with Ra'am, and supported from outside by the Joint List.

Israel's election deadlock is a sign of the country's deep divisions

#287. Dartmouth provost resigns following accusations he used artificial intelligence for writing

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

A top administrator at Dartmouth College has resigned after a faculty committee found he used artificial intelligence to shape his writing, including in a guest column in The Washington Post on AI cheating.

The announcement Wednesday of Provost Santiago Schnell’s departure from the Ivy League university comes as colleges across the country grapple with how to transform teaching as more assignments can be completed with AI tools.

Dartmouth touts itself as the birthplace of AI, saying the field got its start at the school in Hanover, New Hampshire, in 1956 when a small group of scientists gathered for a summer research project on the topic. A conference commemorating the 70th anniversary — titled “What Must Remain Human?” — began Thursday.

University President Sian Leah Beilock said in a written statement that keeping Dartmouth at the forefront of AI means “leading through an environment that is fluid and fast-changing.”

“That is why, as leaders, it is so critical that we model the transparency and human judgment that we expect from our students and scholars,” the president wrote, adding that Schnell’s departure was "his decision” and that the school wishes him well.

Schnell referred questions to his attorneys, who didn’t immediately respond to an email from The Associated Press seeking comment.

Issues arose after the news outlet Semafor ran guest columns in major newspapers through an AI filter. Schnell’s August column in the Post, titled “Universities are fighting AI cheating. But there’s a deeper problem," was found to be entirely written by AI.

Schnell defended himself, saying at the time he used ChatGPT to "refine a few arguments” but that the column was based on his “own thinking.”

The college paper, The Dartmouth, then ran other publications from Schnell through an AI detector. Writing that predated ChatGPT’s 2022 public release was found to be human-written, but high rates of AI use were detected in his recent writing.

Schnell told the paper in an email last month that he uses AI for “language refinement, copyediting, improving clarity and organization and making writing processes more efficient.” The paper ran the statement through an AI-detector and found it was AI-written.

In a follow-up statement, Schnell told the paper that he “developed the substance and wrote a draft” of the email, then “used an AI tool to assist with language and clarity.”

An investigation ensued, and the faculty committee found that Schnell used AI to “meaningfully alter his writing” and “was not forthcoming about his AI use.”

Schnell, a Venezuelan-born mathematical biologist, previously taught and held leadership roles at the University of Notre Dame, the University of Michigan and Indiana University, according to his LinkedIn profile.

#27. 'The city is alive': A brief peace in Kherson, but residents fear what comes next

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The temporary ceasefire in Kherson has provided its residents with the first glimpse of peace in nearly five years

The Ukrainian city of Kherson has known its first two days of quiet after many years, as a US-brokered temporary ceasefire has allowed residents rare moments of respite from the constant Russian aerial attacks that plague the front line region.

Videos posted on social media showed people strolling down streets covered in anti-drone nets and gathering in the city's squares - ordinary activities that usually mean keeping one eye on the sky and one ear out for incoming drones.

"Ever since the ceasefire was announced, there's been total silence," said Olga, 42, speaking by phone from Kherson. "We are all, of course, overjoyed."

"I don't even know what to call it," she added, her smile coming through in her voice. "It's as if everything has finally come to an end."

Since Tuesday, Kherson has "literally come back to life," Ukraine's human rights ombudsman Dmytro Lubinets said.

"People are calmly going out, walking, enjoying the autumn sun. What for some is ordinary everyday life, for residents of the Kherson region today is the greatest desire."

The temporary pause in hostilities - due to end on Sunday - is the result of efforts by US First Lady Melania Trump to broker a safe passage out of the Russian-occupied Ukrainian city of Oleshky, which lies on the other side of the Dnipro river from Kherson.

For several days, Russian forces have been working to clear the road of mines, ahead of a planned evacuation of Oleshky's civilians on Friday.

But there are fears that, alongside carrying out the well-publicised "humanitarian operation" â which some American journalists will be invited to witness â the Russians are using the truce to bolster their forces in the area ahead of a renewed onslaught of Kherson from next week.

"They are... using this lull to strengthen their positions, bring in more military supplies, weapons, ammunition, and drones, and carry out some redeployment of their forces," an anonymous source working in local government told the BBC.

Petro Andriushchenko, Director of Ukraine's Center for Studying the Occupation, says Kherson locals have told him that they witnessed a military build-up around Radensk, some 20km (12 miles) from Oleshky.

Its residents will be saved but "we are simultaneously giving the enemy an opportunity to exploit the ceasefire for its own military purposes," he said. Once the ceasefire ends, the situation may be "worse than we saw before, because Russia's units will be stronger. They have enough time to prepare to prolong this action."

Though Kherson residents are clear-eyed about what lies ahead, for a brief period this week the city caught its breath.

No violations have been reported on either side since Tuesday. There have been other ceasefires in the past, but these were generally not respected, Kherson resident Dima said.

"This is the first time the shelling has stopped, people don't quite believe it," he told the BBC. "They are going to the shops, drinking coffee, walking around... The city is alive."

On social media, a woman named Yulia wrote about her unusually uneventful trip to the supermarket with an edge of disbelief. For once, she said, she returned home from the shops "whole and calm, not as usual - scared, sweating from the horror and with a lump in my throat."

"We've managed to adapt to this way of life," Olga said. "We've been living under these conditions for nearly five years now."

This week, dozens of people were also able to access their belongings and homes in the so-called "red zone" of Kherson â an area so exposed to drones and artillery attacks that it has become unlivable.

"Some are venturing into places where nobody has dared to go for years, filming areas where simply being present has meant - and will once again mean - almost certain death," said Denisa Bohackova, a Czech volunteer who lives in Kherson.

Dima described scenes of "girls, boys, parents, grandmothers" walking down the streets of the red zone carrying suitcases and bags, returning to their flats to see what they could retrieve.

Some of those who returned to the red zone found buildings reduced to charred, empty shells; in other cases, homes had been looted. The scale of destruction in some of these places, Bohackova said, is "breathtaking".

Others were rushing back for reasons beyond material concerns. Angela Dobrovolska's husband Serhiy was killed two years ago by a grenade dropped by a piloted Russian drone - an instance of a "human safari" attack of the kind that Kherson has become sadly infamous for.

On Thursday, Angela headed back from Kyiv to Kherson to visit his grave for the first time in months. "They say that all the people have rushed to Kherson," she said. "And I'll be going to the cemetery to my husband."

The population of Kherson has dwindled from over 250,000 to about 65,000 since the start of Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine, which will enter its sixth year in February.

Though civilian life in the city never stopped, Bohackova said, it also carried on under the conditions of an active battlefield, with schools, theatres, kindergartens and hospitals moved underground.

"An ordinary civilian from Kherson has experienced more of this war than many soldiers," Bohackova said.

Olga, who is raising two children, described regular blackouts, mines dropped by drones and concealed by fallen leaves, drone fragments ricocheting, and a city that typically shuts by the afternoon.

But since silence fell on the city, Olga has been able to live a normal life. She drove to a different town and took the bus to another district to visit her grandmother with her son. Around her, the shops were full, as were the cafes. After years of quiet streets, "there was even a small traffic jam," she smiled.

The evacuation of nearby Oleshky is due to be completed by Sunday, after which the ceasefire will be over. "The concern is that, starting Monday, things could turn truly chaotic," Olga said.

"We know the strikes will resume, and that they will increase," said Dima, as he and his wife prepared to visit their flat in the red zone for the first time in several months.

"These few days showed us how it could be if it all finally ended."

#458. Cleric arrested in Iran protests sentenced to death twice

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An Iranian cleric and former Revolutionary Guards member arrested during January's nationwide protests has been sentenced to death twice in separate cases, including for apostasy, US-based rights group HRANA reported Thursday.

The cleric, identified only as Mehdi, was sentenced to death alongside two other protesters, Amirreza Rezaei and Vahid Ghahremani, by Branch 23 of Tehran's Revolutionary Court, according to HRANA.

The charges against the three included allegedly carrying out operations on behalf of Israel, HRANA said. They were informed of their sentences in prison on Monday.

Mehdi, whose surname remains unknown, received a second death sentence from Iran's Special Clerical Court on a charge of apostasy. HRANA provided no further details about the circumstances of that case or his previous service in the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC).

All three were arrested separately by security forces during the nationwide protests in January. They were transferred from Greater Tehran Prison to Ghezel Hesar Prison in Karaj on Wednesday.

The sentences come amid warnings from the United Nations that Iranian authorities have intensified executions and prosecutions linked to the protests during the war with the United States and Israel.

UN Special Rapporteur Mai Sato told the General Assembly on Wednesday that at least 34 people had been executed in connection with the nationwide protests.

Sato cited one case in which 34 defendants were tried in just five days, with 12 sentenced to death and seven already executed. She raised concerns about mass trials, forced confessions and the lack of due process in capital cases.

"The war has provided cover for the authorities to intensify their crackdown and tighten the repression," Sato said, adding that protesters had been portrayed as agents of foreign powers and peaceful dissent prosecuted as a national security threat.

The protests began in late December with strikes by merchants in Tehran before spreading across the country. At least 36,500 people were killed during the subsequent crackdown by security forces, according to Iran International's reporting, while tens of thousands were arrested or summoned.

Some Iranian families are paying thousands of dollars to put their children in foreign online schools, buying an international education from home and stepping outside the Islamic Republic’s school system, despite the education ministry’s warnings.

The government-affiliated newspaper Iran reported on Thursday that institutions offering foreign curricula and diplomas from countries including Canada and Britain were attracting Iranian students.

A mother whose 15-year-old daughter attends an online high school based in Toronto told the newspaper her family pays $7,000 annually to prepare her daughter for admission to Canadian universities without leaving Iran.

Another father cited shortcomings in Iranian schools’ approach to practical skills and creative thinking as reasons for enrolling his son in a foreign online college alongside his education in Tehran.

Mohammad Salimi, head of the Education Ministry’s Center for International Affairs and Overseas Schools, warned that the foreign online institutions in question lack authorization to operate in Iran and that their diplomas are not recognized by the domestic education system.

Students attending only these institutions are not registered in Iran’s official education databases and could face difficulties continuing their studies domestically, Salimi told Iran daily.

For families planning to send their children to universities abroad, however, recognition by institutions in the destination country may matter more than approval from Iranian authorities.

A diploma’s lack of recognition in Iran does not necessarily make it invalid internationally. Its acceptance depends on the school’s accreditation and the admission requirements of individual universities.

The expansion of international online schools allows students to follow American and British curricula without relocating.

International Virtual Learning Academy (IVLA), a private American school, provides online education from elementary through high school, allowing students to select courses and progress at a pace suited to their circumstances.

Annual high school tuition starts at $2,679 for its basic program, rising to $3,799 or $5,279 for packages offering additional educational support.

Britain’s King's InterHigh offers live and recorded online classes, with subjects including computer science, economics, psychology and film studies.

For the 2026–2027 academic year, annual tuition is £7,250 for students aged 14 to 16 and £7,585 for the pre-university program covering three principal subjects.

Crimson Global Academy also provides American and British curricula online. Annual full-time tuition for its American high school program ranges from $22,375 to $25,500.

The differences show that foreign online schooling does not carry a uniform price. Costs vary according to grade level, course selection and the educational services provided.

Ideological education and alternative choices

Some families are turning to foreign education in a country whose school system was restructured around the Islamic Republic’s religious and political objectives after the 1979 revolution.

UNESCO has documented how Iranian textbooks were revised after the revolution to incorporate Islamic teachings, with religious and political content extending beyond religious education into subjects including history, social studies and Persian literature.

Iran’s Fundamental Reform Document of Education also identifies the cultivation of Islamic-Iranian identity and religious values among the education system’s central objectives.

In a study published in July 2025, education researcher Golnar Mehran examined Iranian social studies textbooks and the role of Islamization and politicization in their content.

By contrast, the international online schools examined offer greater flexibility in course selection, different academic pathways and preparation for universities abroad.

The YouTube generation and English-language learning

The spread of the internet and social media has also exposed Iranian students to English-language material and international educational resources from an early age.

YouTube, online gaming and English-language social media content have expanded opportunities to learn beyond formal classrooms, despite extensive internet filtering in Iran.

A 2024 survey by the Iranian Students Polling Agency, ISPA, found that roughly half of the university students surveyed used YouTube.

Another official survey published in 2026 found that around 23 percent of children and adolescents aged six to 15 used tools to circumvent internet restrictions.

A 2026 study involving 120 English-language learners in Tehran and Isfahan found that structured educational use of YouTube, Instagram, Telegram and WhatsApp over 12 weeks significantly improved English proficiency among participants in the experimental group.

Such resources can make international online education more accessible to students who develop sufficient English-language skills.

IVLA, for example, expects international applicants to have adequate English proficiency but does not require a language-test certificate at enrollment.

Dollar-priced education and a future beyond Iran’s job market

The cost of foreign online schooling means it is largely accessible to families with greater financial resources, some of whom may be considering educational and professional futures beyond Iran.

English-language instruction, exposure to international curricula and experience with remote learning could help prepare students for university education abroad and potential participation in the global labor market.

The growth of remote employment has also created opportunities for people to work for foreign companies or clients without emigrating. Language skills, digital proficiency and familiarity with international educational and professional environments can support access to such work.

However, Iran’s internet restrictions, difficulties receiving foreign payments because of limited access to international banking networks, and overseas employers’ hiring policies can still obstruct those opportunities.

More than 16.5 million students were enrolled in Iran’s education system at the beginning of the 2026–2027 academic year, but no comprehensive figures have been published on the number attending foreign online schools or whether enrollment is increasing.

It is also unclear how many intend to pursue education abroad and how many regard foreign courses as a supplement to their domestic schooling.

Although the newspaper Iran focused on tuition costs and the risks associated with diploma recognition, the parents it interviewed described different priorities, including alternative curricula and preparation for education overseas.

For families already investing substantial sums in international schooling, recognition by Iranian authorities may carry less weight than access to foreign universities, migration opportunities or employment beyond the domestic market.

The Education Ministry’s warning about unrecognized diplomas therefore addresses only part of a broader issue: how far Iran’s education system can meet the expectations of students increasingly using the internet, English-language resources and online education to explore academic and professional opportunities beyond the country’s borders.

Young Iranians are turning from opium, their parents’ drug, to cannabis, methamphetamine and a widening range of synthetic substances, treatment specialists and officials say, as unemployment, hardship and uncertainty about the future reshape the country’s drug use.

Opium is still common among older users, Tejarat News reported. The younger generation’s drugs go by other names: cannabis, known locally as gol (bud), and methamphetamine, known as shisheh (glass).

In July, the head of prevention at Iran's Drug Control Headquarters, Zahra Abedini, said the highest prevalence of drug use was among people aged 24 to 35.

She estimated the prevalence of drug use among university students at between 2.5 and 2.7 percent, although the figures were based on existing studies. A new nationwide survey is underway to provide updated data on addiction in Iran.

Kamran Fateh, an addiction specialist, told Tejarat News that drug consumption should not be viewed simply as a matter of personal choice.

Behind many cases of drug use lies a combination of social, economic and psychological factors, he said.

"A young person facing anxiety, unemployment, a sense of having no future, emotional setbacks, economic pressure or family tensions may look for a quick way to temporarily escape those pressures," Fateh added.

Cannabis use and the changing drug market

The Drug Control Headquarters' director of treatment has identified cannabis and hashish as among the country's most commonly consumed substances, warning about the rapid emergence of synthetic drugs and new psychoactive compounds.

One concern highlighted in the report is the growing normalization of cannabis use among young people, some of whom regard it as less dangerous than traditional narcotics or believe occasional consumption carries little risk of dependence.

Fateh warned that misconceptions about the safety of certain substances were among the biggest obstacles to prevention.

"When a substance is described as 'recreational,' 'herbal' or 'less harmful,' people's awareness of the risk of dependence decreases," he said.

Addiction treatment specialists in several provinces have reported cases of young people beginning with cannabis before experimenting with other substances, although such progression is not inevitable.

According to prices reported by addiction treatment specialists, a cannabis cigarette costs around 1.5 million rials ($0.55), while methamphetamine sells for approximately 2.5 million to 7 million rials ($0.92 to $2.59) per gram.

Heroin prices range from 2 million to 7 million rials ($0.74 to $2.59) per gram, compared with 1.21 million to 3 million rials ($0.45 to $1.11) for opium. The figures are estimates rather than official market rates.

Experts have also warned that newly emerging substances are increasingly marketed with claims of reducing stress, boosting energy, improving concentration or promoting weight loss.

Economic hardship and uncertainty fuel vulnerability

Fateh said the expanding illegal drug market was making prevention increasingly difficult as suppliers introduced new substances and combinations to meet changing demand.

He also said that families sometimes discover drug use only after occasional consumption has developed into a more serious problem, adding that punishment and intimidation are not necessarily effective responses.

"If society focuses only on 'saying no to drugs' but offers no solutions to problems such as unemployment, social isolation, depression, identity crises, economic pressure and a lack of healthy recreational activities, many of the underlying causes of drug use will remain," he said.

Fateh highlighted the relationship between economic hardship and addiction, saying that rising drug prices could drive dependent users toward cheaper, unfamiliar or more dangerous substances.

"If a dependent person does not have access to effective, affordable and accessible treatment, higher prices may even push them toward more dangerous alternatives," he said.

He argued that enforcement measures against drug suppliers alone would not resolve Iran's addiction problem without addressing the broader economic and social pressures facing younger generations.

Iran has used a secret network of more than 5,000 military agencies to import at least $2 billion worth of equipment, explosives and heavy-duty trucks since January 2025, bypassing Western sanctions and the US naval blockade, a Reuters investigation published on Thursday found.

One agency alone, identified as MOD 346428, imported $2.3 billion in military-related goods between January 2025 and June 2026, according to shipping records examined by Reuters.

The shipments included rocket fuel ingredients, explosives, aircraft parts, industrial machinery and hundreds of heavy-duty trucks that could be adapted for military use, with goods recorded as originating in China, Russia, Germany and other countries.

The findings provide details of how Iran has continued to obtain materials used in missile and drone production during the US-Israeli war, which began in February, despite attacks on its weapons production facilities.

Thousands of agencies linked to defense ministry

Reuters identified more than 5,000 agencies whose names begin with MOD, referring to Iran's Ministry of Defense, and which share the Tehran address of the Ministry of Defense and Armed Forces Logistics.

Although their registration records list February 11, 1979, as their founding date, most were established in 2013, when Iran was facing severe economic sanctions, the investigation found.

Reuters and Israeli-American risk consultancy 240 Analytics traced the agencies through Iranian banking records, corporate filings and shipping documents, including around 19,000 import records.

Some entities were linked to the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) and large Iranian foundations known as bonyads.

The agencies use intermediaries and overseas companies to purchase goods with proceeds from oil sales that bypass sanctions, according to the investigation.

In 97% of the shipments reviewed, transactions were recorded as settled "without currency transfer," allowing purchases to be made using funds held outside Iran.

Banking records also showed that 36 agencies held about $1.2 billion in foreign currency deposits at the end of 2024, while the wider network held around $1.8 billion in local currency, Reuters reported.

The news agency said it found no evidence that the numbered agencies identified in the documents had been individually sanctioned by Western governments.

Missile materials and hundreds of trucks imported

The investigation found that Iran continued importing materials that could be used in missiles and drones after the war began on February 28.

In the first six months of 2026, deliveries included detonators, explosives, aircraft engine components and rocket fuel ingredients.

In February, shortly before the war, Iran imported 93 metric tons of Chinese titanium and hundreds of tons of chemicals that could be used in rocket propellants and explosives, according to customs records.

Between January 2025 and June 2026, MOD 346428 also imported 435 Iveco trucks, some of which matched models used as disguised mobile missile launchers, Reuters reported.

The same agency imported thousands of Toyota Hilux and Land Cruiser vehicles, as well as Toyota Tundra trucks, models that Iranian forces have used for military purposes.

The investigation also traced imports of chemicals to the port of Bandar Abbas, where an explosion in April 2025 killed at least 70 people and injured more than 1,200.

Reuters said it could not determine whether chemicals in one of the shipments it identified were responsible for the explosion.

It also could not establish that all imported goods were intended for weapons production, noting that many materials and machines have both civilian and military uses.

Imports continued during war and blockade

The materials entered Iran through several routes, including cargo vessels crossing the Caspian Sea from Russia, trucks traveling from Central Asia and freight aircraft arriving from China, Reuters reported.

Monthly imports by the identified military agency fell by 47% to $54 million in March, shortly after the war began, but recovered to $77 million by May.

The investigation identified five shipments of Russian detonators worth $7.6 million since January 2025, including a $1.9 million delivery in June 2026.

Since the war began, $27 million worth of goods purchased by Iran's military were recorded as originating in the United Arab Emirates, mostly arriving by sea.

The Reuters data also listed hundreds of millions of dollars in trucks, aircraft components and electronics as coming from Germany and other European Union countries.

Reuters cautioned that some customs records identified shipments only through product codes and declared countries of origin, which it could not always independently verify.

Western companies and governments respond

China's Foreign Ministry told Reuters it was unaware of the reported shipments and rejected attempts to link Beijing to the regional conflict.

"We firmly oppose attempts to maliciously link regional hostilities to China or smear China's reputation," the ministry said.

Italian truck manufacturer Iveco said it had safeguards to prevent its vehicles from reaching prohibited users and destinations but could not control products resold by third parties.

Toyota said it did not conduct business in Iran and had no knowledge of the purchases described in the investigation.

The European Union said it was examining information about Iran's efforts to strengthen its missile and drone programs.

A US Treasury spokesperson said Washington had cut off Iran's economic lifelines and would continue enforcing sanctions.

"While the regime may continue its desperate attempts to evade our powerful sanctions, fund its military and weapons programs, and access foreign currency, we are always 10 steps ahead," the spokesperson said.

Iranian government bodies and companies contacted by Reuters, including the country's presidency and its UN delegation in Geneva, did not respond to requests for comment.

The departure of Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu could benefit Iran even if his successor maintained the same policies toward Tehran, Khorasan, a conservative Iranian newspaper close to Parliament Speaker Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf, said on Thursday.

The newspaper said a change in Israeli leadership would not necessarily reduce military threats against Iran but could weaken the political and diplomatic networks Netanyahu has built during his years in power.

"Ultimately, Netanyahu's departure is in Iran's interest, not because his successor would necessarily adopt a friendly approach, but because removing one of the main architects of the confrontation with Iran would reduce the accumulated political and diplomatic capacity behind it," Khorasan wrote.

The assessment came after Israel's parliamentary election on Tuesday produced no clear winner, leaving Netanyahu and his main rival, former military chief Gadi Eisenkot, seeking support to form a government.

With nearly 86% of votes counted on Wednesday, Israeli broadcasters projected Netanyahu's right-wing bloc would secure 53 seats in the 120-member Knesset, compared with 48 for Eisenkot's opposition bloc and 19 for Arab-led parties.

Neither main bloc reached the 61 seats needed for a majority, leaving the outcome dependent on coalition negotiations.

Netanyahu's experience seen as key factor

Khorasan said Israeli political groups shared many positions on security matters despite their differences and that Eisenkot, a former military chief, was not opposed to confrontation with Iran.

It argued, however, that Netanyahu had built political and diplomatic networks over many years that a successor could find difficult to replace.

"Netanyahu is not merely a prime minister," the newspaper wrote, adding that he had developed extensive political, regional and international connections and considerable experience influencing US decision-making.

The newspaper said Netanyahu knew how to make Iran a central issue in Washington's foreign policy and connect security developments with political opportunities.

"Removing such a figure from the top of power, even if the basic structure of Israeli policy remains unchanged, means losing part of this accumulated capacity," it said.

"Netanyahu and his allies have built ties over the years between the Israeli government and some opponents of the Islamic Republic, particularly the monarchist movement," the newspaper wrote.

"Netanyahu's departure could weaken part of this political image, although one should not expect all these relationships to disappear with a change in leadership," it added.

It said political divisions within a future Israeli coalition could make decision-making more difficult, although such disagreements would not automatically reduce the risk of military action.

Election leaves Netanyahu and Eisenkot seeking coalition partners

Netanyahu's Likud-led bloc emerged ahead of Eisenkot's opposition in the preliminary results but remained short of a parliamentary majority.

Parties representing Arab Israelis were projected to secure an unprecedented 19 seats following high voter turnout, giving them a potentially important role in determining the next government.

Eisenkot could seek support from Arab-led parties to secure a majority, although several parties within his opposition bloc have opposed such cooperation.

Khorasan said the high turnout among Arab voters was one of the election's most important developments but questioned whether Eisenkot could reach an agreement with Arab parties while maintaining support from his existing allies.

Reuters reported that Netanyahu's electoral performance had strengthened his position in coalition negotiations despite the loss of his previous parliamentary majority.

The prime minister will remain in office in a caretaker capacity while the process of forming a new government continues.

Iran remains central to Israeli political debate

The election took place during the US-Israeli war on Iran, which began in February, with the possibility of further military action remaining an issue in Israeli politics.

Netanyahu has repeatedly emphasized the Iranian threat during the campaign and has received public support from US President Donald Trump.

Eisenkot has criticized Netanyahu's handling of security matters and warned against the political use of military decisions.

Khorasan said Netanyahu's departure would not necessarily bring a change in Israel's approach toward Iran, noting that different political groups shared positions on key security issues.

"Internal disagreements do not necessarily lead to a reduction in military threats," the newspaper wrote, adding that political competition could sometimes result in more aggressive behavior.

It said the removal of Netanyahu would not end the confrontation between Israel and Iran but could reduce the ability of Israeli leaders to coordinate political and diplomatic efforts against Tehran.

"Sometimes removing one player does not, by itself, end the game, but it can make the game more difficult for the opposing side," Khorasan concluded.

As more Iranians build new lives abroad, many parents back home face a less visible consequence of migration: years without seeing their children, family relationships maintained through phone screens, and growing uncertainty about who will be there for them in old age.

For Maryam, a 68-year-old retiree in Tehran, that distance has become part of everyday life. Her son lives in Canada and her daughter in Germany, and she has not seen either of them in years.

“I know they're building their futures, and I'm happy for them,” she told Iran International. “But sometimes I just wish they were here, even for an ordinary dinner.”

Maryam says regular calls and photographs help her stay connected with her family, particularly her grandchildren. But watching them grow up from thousands of miles away is difficult.

“They know me through the phone,” she says. “I wish I could be more than just a face on a screen in their lives.”

Last winter, Maryam was hospitalized with kidney problems. Her children arranged help through relatives, and a family member accompanied her to medical appointments.

“My daughter was very worried, and I kept telling her everything was fine,” she recalls. “Sometimes, especially when I'm ill, I just wish we weren't so far apart.”

Caring for a spouse without the children nearby

In Karaj, 72-year-old Hamid has spent the past two years caring for his wife after a stroke left her with limited mobility.

Their two sons live in Sweden and Australia. Although they call regularly and provide financial support, most daily responsibilities fall on Hamid.

He describes the exhaustion of managing medication, medical appointments and household chores.

“Some people think you're fortunate when your children live abroad,” he says. “But when your wife needs help getting out of bed, what matters is having someone nearby.”

The family has hired a part-time caregiver, but Hamid still handles much of his wife's care.

“Sometimes I want to tell my sons how exhausted I am,” he says. “Then I think about how far away they are and wonder what they could possibly do.”

Hamid does not blame his children for emigrating, but says family life has changed.

“We're still very close, and we talk all the time. But it's different when you can't sit together in the same room.”

For Nahid, a 64-year-old widow in Tehran, the question is whether she should leave Iran herself.

Her only daughter has lived in London for eight years and has encouraged her mother to move closer.

“My daughter tells me to come and live with her, but I've spent my whole life here,” she says. “My sisters, my friends, all my memories are here. At the same time, every year that passes, I wish I could be closer to her.”

Nahid worries about losing her independence in a country where she does not speak the language fluently or have an established social circle.

But staying in Tehran also means facing old age far from her daughter.

“I don't want to become a burden to my daughter,” she says. “But I don't want to leave behind everything I've built here either.”

Phone and video calls have helped families separated by migration remain part of one another's lives despite years of living thousands of kilometres apart. They share news, celebrate birthdays and check on each other's health, sometimes communicating more frequently than families living in the same city.

But as parents grow older, that distance takes on a different meaning. A sudden illness, a medical emergency or the growing need for help with everyday tasks can expose the limits of even the closest long-distance relationships.

For children who have emigrated, Iran remains the place where their parents are growing old. For the parents who stayed behind, each passing year brings a difficult reality into sharper focus: the people they may need most are the ones living furthest away.

#856. IN BRIEF: FSB reports new cases of Kiev recruiting Russian teenagers for terrorism

MOSCOW, October 29. /TASS/. Russia’s FSB has released new details about Ukraine luring Russian teenagers into committing terrorist acts through the popular youth dating chatbot Divinchik.

- The FSB has reported new cases of teenagers being recruited by the special services of Ukraine to commit terrorist attacks through the popular youth dating chatbot Divinchik / Leo - Dating, Communication, New Friends.

- It said that in August-September 2026 alone, eight minors aged 14 to 17 and two adult Russian citizens (19 and 24 years old) were detained in Moscow, St. Petersburg and the Leningrad Region, Nizhny Novgorod, Omsk, Ryazan and Sverdlovsk regions.

- According to the FSB, the detainees planned or had already committed arson attacks on law enforcement vehicles, gas stations, energy and transport infrastructure facilities on the instructions of the Ukrainian special services.

- They also served as couriers for transferring funds received from defrauded citizens to crypto exchange offices.

- It said that since July 2025, 56 Russians aged 12 to 24, users of the Divinchik Telegram service, who committed crimes under the psychological influence of the special services of Ukraine, have been detained in various regions.

- The FSB showed a video recording of the detention of several teenagers involved in terrorist attacks through the popular youth dating chatbot Divinchik.

- In the footage, the young people said that they had met with young women in the chat, after which they were contacted by unknown people and forced to commit illegal acts through deception, blackmail and threats.

- As a result, several detainees set fire to gas stations, another photographed a relay cabinet, after which he was ordered to set it on fire to allegedly close his criminal case.

- The special services of Ukraine have involved two underage St. Petersburg schoolgirls in committing terrorist attacks through Telegram chatbot Divinchik /Leo, Svetlana Petrenko, an official representative of the Investigative Committee of Russia, told TASS.

- As a result, one of the schoolgirls set fire to a gas station in September 2026.

- Also in September, the second schoolgirl, while at a police station, poured flammable liquid over official cars, after which she set them on fire.

- After that, she went to another police department but was detained.

- In addition, a teenager has been brought to criminal responsibility in the Leningrad Region.

- A young man purchased flammable liquids and set fire to a gas station in St. Petersburg and the Leningrad Region, recording his actions on video.

- He acted on the instructions of a person who contacted him after meeting a young woman in the chatbot Divinchik and threatened negative consequences for him and his family members.

- They were all placed under house arrest.

- You should not divulge your personal data or the data of your loved ones when texting on a dating site, the FSB warned.

- It also said you should never give them your bank details, click on directed links, provide coordinates (geolocation) or photo and video images of buildings, structures, terrain, cars, equipment or people.

- People claiming to be from the Russian special services can later use this information for blackmail purposes, coercing the dupe into committing crimes under the threat of criminal prosecution.

- The Investigative Committee of Russia has urged parents to monitor their children's online activity so that they do not become involved in terrorist attacks and other illegal activities under the influence of the special services of Ukraine.

- Petrenko also reminded that no one should offer up their personal data when communicating on the Internet.

- The best thing to do if you receive a suspicious offer during online communication is immediately report it to the law enforcement agencies.

- In accordance with Article 31 of the Criminal Code, a person is not subject to criminal liability for a crime if he voluntarily and definitively refused to bring this crime to an end.

#29. 'Extremist right-wing ideologies' an increasing threat in France & 'across much of democratic world'

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

François Picard is pleased to welcome Dr Joseph Downing, Senior Lecturer in International Relations and Politics at Aston University. In modern France, the politics of insecurity increasingly extends beyond questions of crime and public order to encompass deeper struggles over national identity, disappearance of the political centre and erosion of democratic values. The emergence of far-right extremist violence, alongside the persistent stigmatization of ethnically diverse working-class suburbs, raises urgent questions about the relationship between political rhetoric, radicalization and the erosion of democratic moderation. Downing examines these interrelated challenges against the backdrop of a deadly shooting in the Paris suburbs, allegedly motivated by racist and anti-Muslim hostility. For Downing, the attack illustrates a broader shift in Europe's extremist landscape, in which the rise of far-right political violence demands greater public and institutional attention: "This is really one of the first and one of the most serious right-wing nationalist, nativist-inspired terror attacks that you see in France." Yet Downing's analysis reaches beyond the horrific attack. He challenges the political representation of France's working-class suburbs as centres of Islamist radicalism, describing such portrayals as a divisive rhetorical strategy that stigmatizes communities whose residents are overwhelmingly engaged in ordinary civic and economic life. His central political concern is the weakening of the democratic centre. Amid rising living costs, social insecurity and the circulation of polarizing identity narratives through digital media, he argues that political movements on the ideological fringes gain an advantage by offering apparently simplistic, and often divisive solutions, to deeply complex problems. "The centre of politics in France, as in many other democracies, is struggling to hold." Perhaps most revealingly, Downing identifies the deliberate or careless conflation of racial identity, migration status and religious extremism as a particularly damaging feature of contemporary political discourse. By treating French citizens of African or Arab heritage as perpetual outsiders, political commentators risk reinforcing the very divisions that undermine national cohesion. The interview therefore opens onto a wider question at the heart of contemporary democratic politics: what happens when narratives of national insecurity become more politically influential than the social realities they claim to describe? Downing's reflections illuminate not only the challenges facing France but also the wider vulnerabilities of democratic societies confronting political polarization, economic anxiety and the normalization of exclusionary rhetoric.

#387. Met refers itself to police watchdog over Meghan warning letter

Move comes as Philippines police investigate alleged child sexual abuse images reportedly found at Thomas Markle Sr’s former home

The Metropolitan police has referred itself to the police watchdog over potential errors in its handling of a 2019 letter warning of dangers to the security of the Duchess of Sussex posed by her father.

The letter made allegations about Thomas Markle, the father of Meghan, who is facing claims of child sexual abuse images offending linked to the Philippines, which he denies.

It was allegedly written by his former wife and the Met says the letter was passed seven years ago to the private office of the Duke and Duchess of Sussex, who were then both working royals.

The Guardian understands that the long letter made claims about Markle’s activities in the US, the Philippines and Thailand.

The Met said it did not investigate as the allegations related to potential unlawful activity overseas.

The key problem for the Met is its admission that it did not share the letter with law enforcement agencies overseas, and that issue has been referred to the police watchdog, the Independent Office for Police Conduct.

The Guardian understands the Met is now sending details of the letter to the FBI in the US and law enforcement in the Philippines, where Markle lived until May, as well as Thai counterparts.

Markle, now in the US, has admitted paying for sex but denies possessing material that would be illegal.

On Saturday Jonvic Remulla, the Philippine interior minister, told the Guardian that all signs point to Markle being a “possible sex offender of the grossest kind” and that a full forensic investigation will give a “definitive picture”.

Officials and investigators in the Philippines are examining digital images alleged to belong to the estranged father of the Duchess of Sussex, and alleged to have been found at his former home in the south-east Asian country.

Meghan’s half-brother, also called Thomas, reported to Philippine authorities last week the alleged discovery of child sexual abuse images.

The allegations about the letter and alleged Met failings surfaced publicly a week ago. The Met has been unable to find its own copy of the letter making the claims, alleged to have been written by Markle’s former wife.

Met Commander Adam Slonecki, who leads its Protection Command, said the letter had been sent in 2019 to the Fixated Threats Assessment Centre: “The author of the letter suggested they were writing to raise concerns relating to the security of the Duchess of Sussex. While the letter did not actually contain any information specifically related to security matters, it did contain a number of claims about alleged offending outside the United Kingdom.

“The letter was assessed and subsequently passed to the office of the Duke and Duchess of Sussex for their awareness. The details of the allegations in the letter were not shared with other law enforcement agencies.”

Slonecki added: “A voluntary referral has been made to the Independent Office for Police Conduct (IOPC) in respect of the decision not to inform other law enforcement agencies, and the decision to pass the letter to the office of the Duke and Duchess of Sussex.”

The Met cannot find a copy of the 2019 warning letter in its systems, and asked the Duchess of Sussex to send the copy she had to assist Britain’s biggest force to better understand its decision making seven years ago, which she did.

An IOPC spokesperson said: “We have received a voluntary conduct referral from the Met police, which relates to the handling of a letter it received in 2019 involving allegations of offending. We are now assessing the referral before deciding what further action may be required.”

Possible options include the police watchdog independently investigating or sending the matter back for the Met to investigate.

The scale of embarrassment for the Met will depend on whether investigators in the Philippines assess that the allegations relating to child sexual abuse images have any credence.

#565. Bolsonaro ally blames ChatGPT for now-deleted ‘president-elect of Brazil’ claim

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sign up now: Get ST's newsletters delivered to your inbox

Flavio Bolsonaro stunned Brazil’s political establishment on Oct 28 by alleging - without evidence - that the Oct 25 runoff election had been marred by fraud.

SAO PAULO - A close ally of Brazilian Senator Flavio Bolsonaro on Oct 29 said AI chatbot ChatGPT was responsible for language in a letter briefly posted on social media by the right-wing lawmaker describing himself as Brazil’s rightful “president-elect”, while insisting the mistake did not alter Bolsonaro’s view that the election was illegitimate.

Bolsonaro stunned Brazil’s political establishment on Oct 28 by making the claim and alleging without evidence that the Oct 25 runoff election, which he lost to leftist President Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva by more than four million votes, had been marred by fraud.

Roughly an hour after sharing the document, the senator deleted it from his accounts and published a revised version, replacing the term “president-elect” with “leader of Brazil”.

As Brazilian media and political observers speculated about the abrupt change, one of Bolsonaro’s closest allies suggested that the explanation for the mistake was not political, but technological: the use of OpenAI’s ChatGPT.

Right-wing influencer and podcast host Paulo Figueiredo, who serves as an unofficial spokesperson for the Bolsonaro family in the United States and first shared the letter with journalists, said the document had gone through several drafts and incorporated contributions from multiple people.

Figueiredo said that Bolsonaro’s younger brother, former Congressman Eduardo Bolsonaro, sought ChatGPT’s help to finalise the letter. According to Figueiredo, the chatbot inserted an outdated version of Brazil’s coat of arms and used a previous draft of the letter.

“When generating the document, AI ended up including an earlier version of the text, in which Flavio presented himself as the true ‘president-elect’,” Figueiredo wrote on X, adding the mistakes were noticed only after he distributed the letter to the press.

According to him, Bolsonaro ultimately opted for the term “leader” for legal and moral reasons. He added that those involved found the mistake “hilarious” and described tweaking the letter as “nothing major”.

OpenAI and Bolsonaro’s press officers did not immediately reply to requests for comment.

“In the end, the essential is expressed precisely by Flavio: These elections were illegitimate, he does not recognise the result, and he will continue fighting to restore democracy in Brazil,” Figueiredo said.

The senator arrived in Miami on Oct 28 after leaving Brazil, where he refused to comply with a Supreme Court order to turn in his passports as part of a probe into alleged money laundering, in which he denies wrongdoing.

Bolsonaro’s decision to declare himself the country’s rightful “leader” has failed to garner support from key figures in his political camp, leaving him increasingly isolated.

Lula’s victory has been recognised by Brazil’s federal electoral court, global leaders, international observers, and Bolsonaro’s own party. REUTERS

#337. Driverless big rigs have already hit US roads but three challenges are slowing expansion

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Given those challenges, companies are testing automated trucks on roads that lack complications

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Driverless trucks with artificial intelligence on board could dramatically change the landscape of freight shipping. A few companies have begun testing early models on some limited routes in Texas and California. Simulations of automated fleets show the potential benefits of the technology: The vehicles could improve safety, enhance fuel economy and lessen transit times.

Truck makers and operators still have to solve some major issues, however, including how automated trucks will operate in variable weather and road conditions, and whether drivers should still be inside big rigs.

I am an engineer who studies human factors and transportation safety, and in my view, it is likely that automated trucks and human drivers will be working together for quite some time.

Fully automated trucks, which can drive on an interstate highway in good weather without human intervention, use a combination of AI, machine learning, computer vision, pattern recognition and adaptive decision-making. Torc, one company developing these vehicles, uses AI to combine data from multiple sensors and generate models that predict truck performance.

Data taken from modern big rigs that are equipped with certain technology such as automatic emergency braking and lane-keeping assistance systems indicate that fully automated, driverless trucks could reduce the number and severity of crashes. They could also save fuel and therefore cost by optimizing acceleration and braking, and by running multiple vehicles in a close-knit convoy or platoon. Driverless trucks could reduce parking needs as well as emissions from idling because they could run almost 24 hours a day and would not need to idle when they do park, which is common for regular rigs at truck stops.

Finally, automated trucks could help ease the ongoing driver shortage. Ironically, automation could make driving less stressful and less physically demanding, improving job satisfaction among drivers.

The main reason automated trucks are not common today is that they have difficulty operating in variable weather, under changing roadway conditions and alongside unpredictable drivers.

Automated trucks require sensors, radars and cameras that gather information about the environment around them to make decisions. However, snow and rain remain a problem for all automated vehicles, including cars, because they prevent the technology from “seeing” the roadway. Driverless taxi company Waymo, for example, only operates driverless cars in areas that get no snow.

Furthermore, any stretch of road where there may not be a predictable, clear surface and prevalent lines is difficult to handle. Imagine a construction zone with orange cones, narrow lanes and missing lane lines. Driverless car companies still try to avoid these areas. And, oddly, some research shows that automobile drivers tend to crash into driverless cars at a higher rate than they do cars piloted by people.

Shannon Roberts is an Associate Professor of Industrial Engineering at UMass Amherst. This article is republished from The Conversation under a Creative Commons license. Read the original article .

Given those challenges, companies are testing automated trucks on roads that lack complications.

For example, autonomous truck maker Aurora is operating large freight trucks on I-45, a relatively flat and straight highway between Dallas and Houston. Rival Kodiak’s robotrucks are delivering sand to industrial sites in Texas over roads few people use. Few if any automated trucks are being tested in chaotic urban environments, given the pedestrians, bicyclists, traffic lights and elements of city traffic.

Some companies that were developing automated trucks have scaled back ambitions, changed direction or exited the market.

In 2023, Embark, TuSimple and Locomotion closed down. Waymo began to focus solely on cars. Other companies narrowed their focus to trucks that would operate only along a few dedicated routes between two large cities or manufacturing sites.

In my view, for years to come, automated trucks with AI-based controls will still require a driver.

The best technology will support a symbiosis between human and machine, where the person and the truck each do what they do best. A human would likely drive the truck across the “first mile” – the uncertain and crowded warehouse grounds and local streets to a highway. The driver would then stop using the steering wheel, gas pedal and brake and let automation take over. The human would take back control during the “last mile” to the truck’s final destination.

Research my colleagues and I conducted shows that human-machine interfaces that communicate between drivers and autonomous vehicles will be key. For example, a screen or heads-up display inside the cab would tell the driver how the technology is doing. Verbal messages or a vibrating seat could signal that the automation cannot handle a developing situation, and the driver would take over.

Developers will have to be aware of possible biases. So-called automation bias could lead to a driver who trusts and relies on the truck’s decision-making so much that they defer to it. Algorithmic bias could cause truck sensors to recognize certain groups of people on the road more than others, such as only detecting pedestrians of a certain size or shape.

The long-term vision is to have no driver, in part to lower costs. But driverless trucks do not seem to me to be practical on a large scale in the near future. It is more realistic to envision the driver and the automated truck as a team. Vehicle manufacturers, technology developers, regulators and drivers will need to work together to realize the benefits.

#253. JD Vance letter may offer last chance to avert fresh conflict between US and Iran

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

US vice-president writes to top Iranian security official in sign Washington has not abandoned diplomacy

Hopes of averting a major new conflagration between Iran and the US soon after Tuesday’s midterm elections could rest on Iran’s response to a personal letter from the US vice-president, JD Vance, offering to restart diplomacy.

The letter, written after predominantly Qatari mediation, was sent to the secretary of Iran’s supreme national security council, Mohsen Rezaei on Sunday. It requires Tehran to agree to make commitments on its nuclear programme at the start of a process that would lead to the opening of the strait of Hormuz within seven days in return for US sanctions relief.

A reply is imminent, but Iran is reluctant to give upfront and irreversible commitments on its nuclear programme, such as exporting its stockpile of highly enriched uranium to a third country, if the compensatory US steps, such as lifting sanctions on Iranian oil exports, are reversible.

Some form of third-party guarantee that both sides will adhere to their commitments has been discussed, with China often mentioned as the only country of sufficient status to underwrite it.

The return of Vance, a sceptic about the war, to the negotiations is significant and a sign the US has not abandoned diplomacy.

One Democratic senator briefed on the targets the US military had identified in Iran said the plan for a renewed attack was “insane” and would lead to “the likely destruction of our commitment to the rule of law”.

In a sign of Iran’s reluctance to meet US demands about sequencing, its chief negotiator, Mohammad Ghalibaf, wrote on X: “Iran’s conditions have been repeatedly and clearly stated to the mediators. The carrot-and-stick approach has no effect on Iran. Progress can only be achieved once Iran’s seven conditions have been agreed upon.”

Those conditions include lifting sanctions, ending asset freezes and ending the war on all fronts, a phrase that requires an Israeli ceasefire in Lebanon.

The US wants front-loaded, specific commitments about the removal of Iran’s stockpile of highly enriched uranium. Tehran is reluctant to give up its bargaining leverage, and is also resistant to the return of UN nuclear inspectors for fear they might provide the US with intelligence on the whereabouts and state of the stockpile.

It has proposed blending the stockpile down so it is no longer usable to make a nuclear weapon.

The Vance letter was sent to Rezaei rather than Ghalibaf or the foreign minister, Abbas Araghchi, because Washington judged that decision-making power rests with those closest to the Islamic Revolutionary Guards Corps. In practice, Iran tries to reach decisions through consensus at the highest level of government in a six strong committee.

Ghalibaf and Rezaei met the supreme leader, Mojtaba Khamenei, last week to discuss the risk of a new war. Khamenei has said he would like an end to the current state of neither war nor peace. Iran has well-laid plans ready to strike back against US interests in the region, by hitting desalination plants, internet cables and electricity grids across the Gulf.

For a third US attack in two years to have any rationale, or prospect of inducing Iran’s capitulation, the plan would require the US, probably in conjunction with a caretaker government in Israel, to mount attacks on previously off-limits targets. Europe would not join the attacks, but the views of the Gulf states, the chief targets of Iran reprisals, would be key.

The Connecticut senator Chris Murphy told a briefing at the Middle East Institute: “I did get a fairly detailed conversation about what the war plan would be … I will just say this their plan is insane. It will lead to devastating escalation and the likely destruction of our commitment to the rule of law.

“There has been open reporting as to the civilian infrastructure we may be targeting … I think the plan they have developed will lead, in my judgment, to a prolongment [sic] and escalation of this war that will be devastating to our security interest.”

Although Tehran trusts Vance marginally more than most White House politicians and envoys, the Iranian high command is still suspicious about his status after he negotiated a 14-point memorandum of understanding which then fell apart when other members of the US administration took charge of its implementation.

That led to unilateral US steps to reopen the strait of Hormuz that Iran said contravened the terms of the agreement. Trump also came to detest it because it delayed the start of nuclear talks for as long as 60 days.

In an offer made by Tehran on the margins of the UN general assembly recently, it proposed foreshortening the timeframe before nuclear talks started, saying they could start withinsix or seven days of the US restoring the sanctions waiver on its oil exports and the reopening of the strait of Hormuz.

#356. India ready to share battle-tested counter-terrorism expertise with ASEAN partners: Rajnath

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Account subscription benefits alongside Premium Stories, Editorials, Opinions and more. Unlock these with Subscription

The View From India Looking at World Affairs from the Indian perspective.

First Day First Show News and reviews from the world of cinema and streaming.

Science For All The weekly newsletter from science writers takes the jargon out of science and puts the fun in!

Data Point Decoding the headlines with facts, figures, and numbers

THEdge At the cutting edge of education and careers

Health Matters Ramya Kannan writes to you on getting to good health, and staying there

Gender Agenda Stories from beyond the binary.

The Hindu On Books Books of the week, reviews, excerpts, new titles and features.

Updated - October 29, 2026 07:27 pm IST - New Delhi

In this screengrab from a video received on October 29, 2026, Union Defence Minister Rajnath Singh addresses the 13th Association of Southeast Asian Nations Defence Ministers’ Meeting. | Photo Credit: PTI

India is committed to collective regional security and the eradication of terrorism in all its forms and is ready to share its battle-tested counter-terrorism expertise with ASEAN partners to strengthen joint capabilities, Defence Minister Rajnath Singh said on Thursday (October 29, 2026).

Addressing the 13th ASEAN Defence Ministers’ Meeting-Plus (ADMM-Plus) in Cebu, Philippines, Mr. Singh said India’s counter-terrorism experience was not theoretical knowledge but had been acquired through decades of confronting complex security challenges, including cross-border terrorism.

He said India’s strategies had been tested and refined through real-world operations, with Operation Sindoor demonstrating the country’s proactive counter-terrorism doctrine.

During the operation, India dismantled terrorist infrastructure and enforced a firm red line against state-sponsored terrorism, he said.

Emphasising that terrorism knows no borders, Mr. Singh said India was prepared to share its operational experience with ADMM-Plus partners through joint exercises, capacity-building programmes and intelligence sharing.

Counter-terrorism remains an important area of cooperation under the ADMM-Plus framework, with India and Malaysia co-chairing the Experts’ Working Group on Counter-Terrorism for the 2024-2027 cycle.

In a significant proposal to deepen regional engagement, Mr. Singh called for the establishment of a dedicated defence ‘Youth Bridge’ to strengthen institutional people-to-people connectivity between India and ASEAN countries. The ASEAN members appreciated the move of establishing ‘Youth Bridge’.

The initiative would promote digital innovation and capacity building, while providing greater opportunities for young people to contribute to regional peace and security. The proposal was appreciated by ASEAN members.

Highlighting the importance of maritime security, Mr. Singh said India had designated 2026 as the ASEAN-India Year of Maritime Cooperation and had conducted the second ASEAN-India Maritime Exercise. He reiterated India’s commitment to freedom of navigation, international maritime law and the security of global sea lanes.

Pointing to the Indian Navy’s uninterrupted operational presence in the Gulf of Aden since 2008, he said India had escorted thousands of merchant vessels and thwarted dozens of hijacking attempts, irrespective of the vessels’ flag or nationality.

He reaffirmed India’s commitment to ASEAN centrality and a free, open, inclusive and rules-based Indo-Pacific, adding that New Delhi would continue to serve as a reliable net security provider in the Indian Ocean Region and the wider Indo-Pacific.
