#376. Trump’s Cheesecake Factory fixation reveals his true weakness in the midterms homestretch

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Barack Obama got into a tit-for-tat campaign-trail battle where Trump managed to nuke his own administration’s response. Reports say the president is living in a precarious fantasy world. Holly Baxter writes about a chaotic time in MAGA, where it’s unclear whether anyone is talking to ordinary Americans

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

The president is drowning in his own AI slop — with a side of cheesecake.

A few days out from the midterms, and it seems things inside the White House are tense: a damning New York Times report describes him as cocooned in a media environment of his own making, spending time locked in a dining room where he consumes flattering news and gets fed AI videos for social posting from Bill Pulte. Pulte is supposed to be the director of the Federal Housing Finance Agency but has morphed into what one DC insider described as a “court jester,” popping up with memes and AI-generated imagery every so often to boost Trump’s spirits.

Meanwhile, out on the campaign trail, Trump keeps insisting that the midterms will go well if people “pretend he’s on the ballot.” He repeats often that all sitting presidents tend to do badly in the midterms, however, because even he’s aware the polling doesn’t look good. And he’s gotten into a verbal tussle with Barack Obama about the Cheesecake Factory, because of course he has.

In case you’re in need of a quick update: At an Ohio campaign rally a couple of days ago, Obama joked that Trump’s tacky renovations in the White House have made it look like “Saddam Hussein opened a Cheesecake Factory”. It was an offhand comment that got a few laughs, and it clearly got under Republicans’ skin.

The administration gamely tried to generate some outrage traction in response by claiming that Obama is a snob from the “Martha’s Vineyard set” who hates everyday American restaurants. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent — a true man of the working class, with an estimated net worth of $600 million and a past life as a hedge fund manager — was cajoled into tweeting that he “always” orders the French dip cheeseburger whenever he’s down there sampling the Cheesecake Factory wares, which, of course, is often.

Republican influencers went wild trying to push the idea that this is proof Obama doesn’t live like “regular folks” and therefore probably secretly hates them.

It’s a valiant effort, but it’s hard to do when you’re defending a president who recently yelled at one of his own rallies that “we’re making a freaking fortune” on oil because of the Iran war, meaning that shareholders in Big Oil companies are. He then followed up by adding that his own publicity team told him not to say that, because of course ordinary Americans are very much not making a fortune — they are, instead, struggling to afford their groceries or fill up their cars with gas — but hey, whatever, screw those guys, amirite?! The small crowd of hardliners present may have cheered, but those words reverberated beyond that venue.

Not long after that came his Cheesecake Factory response, at a campaign stop in Mississippi where he managed to destroy all the hard work done by his own administration. The White House “has to be pristine,” he said; all the pillars and the stones have to be properly maintained, and the buildings have to be kept beautiful. He added that he’d try and tell Obama that, but Obama doesn’t “understand cleanliness”.

So which is it: Obama’s an uncultured swine who can’t even comprehend basic hygiene and wouldn’t know to notice when a pillar is cracked (I don’t know about you, but I always notice when the pillars at my place are looking subpar!), or he’s an out-of-touch member of the elite who spends too much of his time among rich people with private chefs? Poor Scott Bessent. He really ate that cheeseburger, and all for naught.

Spending all his time inside an echo chamber constructed from bots probably isn’t helping Trump to stay on message. And therein lies the rub: The president’s fixation on fake images, petty feuds and culture war nonsense is colliding with the inconvenient fact that most Americans are worried about the real world. They also increasingly distrust the technology helping him escape it.

A Washington Post-ABC News-Ipsos poll published last week found that 57% of Americans thought AI was a bad thing for society. Two-thirds opposed the construction of new data centers, and 67% disapproved of Trump's handling of the technology. An AP-NORC poll found that 64% of Americans thought AI was developing too quickly, with broad agreement across party lines that the government should prioritise keeping it under human control and protecting workers from job losses.

These are not the numbers of a public waiting impatiently for Silicon Valley and First Buddy Elon Musk to accelerate into the future. They are the numbers of a public that sees a technology being pushed into every corner of life without much confidence that the people deploying it have their interests at heart. Trump’s cosy alliance with all the Big Tech billionaires, and his insistence that there should be no guardrails at all — because otherwise China will end the world and wouldn’t it be better if we ended the world instead? — clearly hasn’t convinced the wider populace. No amount of videos of himself dancing around with a tiger has managed it yet.

Another hugely inconvenient truth: The MAGA movement has spent years insisting that photographs, videos, journalism and official accounts of events are not to be trusted. If you’re suspicious of everything, you’re vulnerable to manipulation by the one guy who says he’s worked it all out. But now AI is making that suspicion literal.

A video can be fabricated, a photo can be generated, a person can appear to say something they never said, in a place they never visited, surrounded by people who never existed. The evidence that might once have settled an argument can itself become the subject of an argument. Everything is potentially fake, and therefore anything can be dismissed.

If you’re heading up a movement that feeds off blending fact and fiction, that’s a problem. Because all of a sudden, you’re not in charge of the unreality; the unreality is everywhere.

Trump's political operation once benefited from the gap between what people could see and what they were told to believe, and now that gap is being filled with an industrial quantity of synthetic nonsense, and the president himself has become obsessed with it. It’s a hubristic downfall of Shakespearean proportions: the poor king’s mind has been addled by the poison he wished to visit upon us all! The court jester still dances, but he’s weeping behind his hands, and the other right-hand man is at a Cheesecake Factory desperately trying to force down a burger and a side of cold fries!

Obama, by the way, followed up at a second rally by clarifying that when he was raising his two daughters, he “lived at the Cheesecake Factory” (as well as Chuck E. Cheese.) Truly, no parent escapes the horrors. “That was my jam for about a decade,” he said.

It was a small, deft piece of political comedy. Obama is warm, quick, entirely comfortable-seeming onstage and, of course, a relatively sprightly 65 years old.

Elsewhere, Trump was continuing the trail by deploying “the weave,” but in less funny, more angry, distinctly non-warm ways. His latest obsession — that James Talarico is a vegan (he isn’t) and that he throws up around meat — led to an unfathomably bizarre performance at the podium where he made odd hand and mouth gestures that were supposed to imitate Talarico trying meat and hating it.

Then, on Thursday morning, the president was asked outside Air Force One as he landed in South Carolina about the idea of Pete Hegseth being impeached. “All they do is talk about impeaching everybody,” he responded, about the Democrats. “And honestly, if we wanted to play that game, we could do it just as well.” It was a response that sounded like it might make sense on the surface, but was actually complete nonsense: For one thing, impeachment articles against Hegseth were introduced by a Republican, Thomas Massie, and for another, who is the standing administration going to impeach among the Democrats?

It’s hard not to view all this footage and think: Let’s get you to bed, Grandpa. Or at the very least: Let’s get you back to the AI slop room, when you’re safe from the cameras and Bill Pulte made you a nice new video where you look like Jesus Christ.

#614. EU considering Canada as ‘associate member’, Ursula von der Leyen says, despite internal opposition

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sign up now: Get ST's newsletters delivered to your inbox

European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen delivering a keynote speech at an EU-Canada Summit in Montreal, Canada, on Oct 29.

MONTREAL - European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen said on Oct 29 the EU and Canada continue to explore Canada becoming the union’s first “associate member,” a term so divisive among member countries that it does not appear in a joint declaration.

The European Union and Canada aim to deepen cooperation on AI, climate, energy and security, von der Leyen said, in a speech at a Canada-EU summit in Montreal.

The associate member term, which Germany and several other EU countries oppose, has no legal meaning.

“Our idea is simple: it is an association without accession,” von der Leyen said.

“We can build a partnership that comes closer to membership than anything we have done before.

“We can work on everything, from AI and climate to energy or security,” she said.

Canadian and EU leaders, however, dropped the idea of associate EU membership from their joint declaration, seen by Reuters, agreeing instead to an “ever closer association.”

The declaration, which officials plan to make public on Oct 30, said work to define the Canada-EU relationship would continue until another summit next year.

Deeper ties ​to Europe are part of Prime Minister Mark Carney’s strategy to reduce Canada’s economic and security reliance on the US since ⁠President Donald Trump imposed tariffs on a range of Canadian goods. Still, Canada’s efforts to draw closer to the EU risk angering Trump as trade talks between Canada and the US ​continue.

Von der Leyen first floated associate membership for Canada six weeks ago, prompting a rebuke from countries that argued they could not agree to a concept that had no legal status and had not been fleshed out, diplomats said.

Three EU diplomats, speaking on condition of anonymity, said they were surprised von der Leyen continued to use the “associate member” term, since the declaration deliberately excluded it.

Speaking later at the summit, European Council president Antonio Costa said the idea of associate membership for Canada was “audacious,” but that he shared von der Leyen’s ambition.

A previous draft statement ​had included at least two references to Canada as an associate member, including an acknowledgment that defining the term would take another year.

Carney said the term for Canada’s new relationship with the EU is insignificant.

“This new alliance is not a marketing exercise. The name doesn’t matter,” he told reporters.

“What matters is that the complementarities of our two jurisdictions strengthen our sovereignty, strengthens Europe’s sovereignty, various strategic autonomies, critical minerals, energy, other aspects, AI,” Carney said.

Canada and the EU were both trying to find an effective response to the geopolitical threats Trump has posed, said Mark Manger, a professor at the Munk School of Global Affairs at the University of Toronto.

“Canada and the EU both are now in this position where the most important ally they relied upon is suddenly not behaving like an ally anymore,” he said.

Manger said Canada and the EU have an enormous opportunity to expand their relationship, but that it would take time to address regulatory inconsistencies, build new undersea cables linking Canada to Europe and secure common supply chains, some of the objectives mentioned in the joint declaration.

The declaration also proposes conducting a joint feasibility study on Canadian liquefied natural gas exports to Europe ‌and enabling ⁠young Canadians and Europeans to work across the Atlantic.

Von der Leyen called for deeper cooperation between the EU and Canada on Arctic security.

“When we see Russia rebuilding its Arctic military footprint, pursuing hybrid strategies against its neighbours and reshaping the security of the Arctic, it impacts the European Union and Canada,” she said.

“We have to act and change our strategic posture and, together with like-minded partners like Canada, stand up for these values.”

Later on Oct 29, Carney will meet with European business leaders before hosting a dinner with EU leaders. REUTERS

#349. Texas Republican group seeks to block certification of state’s election

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Republicans in a Texas county have filed a lawsuit asking for ballots in the state’s midterm races to be hand-counted.

Days before the United States’ pivotal midterm elections, a Texas group affiliated with the Republican Party has asked a federal judge to stop certification of the state’s upcoming election results.

In a lawsuit filed on Tuesday, the Potter County Republican Party and its chairman, Daniel Rogers, claim potential election interference by artificial intelligence (AI) or “malicious actors” that could compromise the results of next Tuesday’s elections in Texas.

The plaintiffs, who are asking for a hand count of every ballot from every precinct in the state before election certification happens, allege voting machines have components inside made by China that would allow them to be manipulated.

“If a component is tampered with during manufacturing, it could serve as a point of entry for unlawful and malfeasance hackers, allowing for the possibility of synchronized attacks across multiple election machines and jurisdictions,” the lawsuit read.

The legal challenge comes as several polls show Democrat James Talarico narrowly leading his Republican challenger, Ken Paxton, in Texas’ all-important US Senate race that could ultimately decide which party controls the chamber after November 3.

The Talarico and Paxton campaigns and the Potter County Republican Party did not immediately return requests from Al Jazeera for comment.

The legal complaint does not specify if any voting machines in Potter County, or elsewhere in Texas, have been compromised. The plaintiffs acknowledged in their filing that no previous election in the county was “altered” by a third party, arguing that the possibility of vote manipulation by AI makes the state’s election safeguards easily compromised.

“The real likelihood of a clever and undetected hack by recently developed AI programs, which conceal their activity to avoid detection, reinforces the need for a full verification based on hand-counting of all paper records or ballots in all Texas precincts, prior to certification of the federal results,” they wrote.

State law in Texas already requires hand-count audits in elections where electronic tabulators are used. But the plaintiffs argue that does not go far enough.

“The paper record or ballot provides an independent verification mechanism only if election officials actually compare a complete hand count of all of the paper records or ballots,” the filing said.

The lawsuit also claims US “adversaries”, such as China, Iran and Russia, are capable of accessing election systems.

US President Donald Trump in July declassified documents that, he claimed, showed Chinese interference in the US 2020 presidential election, reviving his attacks on US elections despite no evidence that Beijing impacted the vote he lost that year.

Last week, the US Department of Justice also sought to block certification of an election, claiming, in Alaska, that an independent candidate with the same name as the state’s Republican Senator up for re-election is running in order to mislead voters.

Alicia Pierce, a spokeswoman for Texas’s Secretary of State, whose office administers elections in the state, said it “does not plan to comment on the lawsuit at this time”.

#369. Finland launches criminal probe of suspected environmental damage by Google

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The US tech giant is investing $15bn in a huge new data centre project in northern Finland.

Finnish police have launched a criminal investigation into suspected breaches of environmental law at the construction site of a planned Google data centre in northern Finland.

The project is part of a $15bn deal announced last month by Alphabet’s Google to invest in artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure in Finland, which the US company described as its largest investment in Europe.

Authorities have said Tuike Finland, a local subsidiary of Google, conducted forest clearing and drainage at a planned site near the town of Muhos, before the environmental impact assessments required under Finnish law had been completed.

“We are investigating whether the company’s operations have breached the Water Act and resulted in environmental damage,” Detective Superintendent Kari Pakanen told the Reuters news agency on Thursday, adding that a guilty finding could lead to a corporate fine.

Pakanen declined to say whether any individuals could become a target of the investigation, and a Google spokesperson said the company would cooperate with the police investigation and continue to focus on maintaining compliance at its sites.

Videos and photographs showing the scale of the forest clearance at the site of the data centre, and Google’s initial reluctance to address the issue in the days after the news broke, have prompted widespread anger in Finland, where there is a high level of respect for the natural environment among the public.

“All the outrage and dismay directed at Google, I can very much share that, especially when we are talking about the parent company valued as the third-largest in the world,” Finnish Green MP Oras Tynkkynen told Al Jazeera. “There is just no excuse. You have to know the legislation of the country you are operating in; you can afford to hire the best legal experts to help you navigate the local legal requirements.”

Finnish authorities ordered Google to stop work at the data centre site at the start of October, and the latest information from the police highlights shortcomings in the drainage and water management at the site.

Tynkkynen called Google’s actions “unforgivable”, but cautioned that while the anger being directed at the US tech giant was justified, it was also diverting scrutiny away from legacy Finnish forestry companies, which he says are “by far the biggest contributors to biodiversity loss in this country”.

Finland’s cool climate, low-carbon electricity and government endorsement of AI projects have contributed to making the country one of Europe’s main destinations for data centre investments in recent years.

Finland, similar in size to Germany – but with one-seventh of the population, less than six million people – has had less vocal opposition to data centre buildouts than other places, including in the United States.

AI data centres are among the main drivers of a projected rise of up to 40 percent in Finland’s electricity consumption in the coming years, if all agreed grid connection projects go ahead, grid operator Fingrid has said.

Google’s planned investments in Finland include data centres, electricity grid improvements and clean energy and battery projects to power services such as Gemini, Search, Maps and YouTube.

Supporting Google’s plan, Finland’s Prime Minister Petteri Orpo said in September that the deal would be a boost for the economy, playing down concerns that it would suck up energy and push up power prices.

#379. Al Jazeera marks 30th anniversary with network overhaul

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Marking three decades, Al Jazeera unveils a new structure: three channels, 33 programmes, and tech-driven innovation.

Al Jazeera is marking its 30-year anniversary with a transformation of the network, introducing a three-channel structure for its Arabic-language broadcasting operation and 33 programmes aimed at providing audiences with more in-depth coverage and analysis.

Since its launch on November 1, 1996, the Qatar-based network has helped reshape Arab journalism, challenging dominant political narratives and providing a platform for political debate rarely seen on television in the region at the time.

Reflecting on this enduring legacy during a special anniversary episode of the network’s Arabic-language Baad al-Bath podcast, veteran anchor Khadija Benguenna detailed the foundational shifts the network brought to the Arab news industry.

Benguenna said that Al Jazeera had popularised practices such as breaking news alerts, continuous live coverage, and exclusive reporting, which were subsequently adopted by other Arab broadcasters.

Former Al Jazeera news director Ahmad al-Sheikh echoed that sentiment during his appearance on the same broadcast. He described the Arab world’s media environment as previously devoid of independent voices, and credited Al Jazeera with providing an example for other Arab broadcasters to improve standards across the board.

The anniversary brings a significant restructuring of the Al Jazeera Media Network’s Arabic-language news services, separating rolling breaking news coverage, live event broadcasting, and more in-depth reporting across three channels.

Speaking to the Qatari newspaper Al Raya, Asef Hamidi, the managing director of Al Jazeera Arabic, described the changes as a “new renaissance” for Al Jazeera. “We are talking about numerous paths undergoing significant development that the audience will notice,” Hamidi said.

Hamidi, who was appointed to his current position in September 2025, said the restructuring was intended to address the growing volume and complexity of news and the need to offer audiences different ways of following events.

In an interview with Qatari newspaper Al Watan, Sheikh Nasser bin Faisal Al Thani, Al Jazeera Media Network director general, explained the philosophical necessity behind the expansion.

“From the very beginning, Al Jazeera was more than just another television channel,” Sheikh Nasser said. “It was a broader project to deepen public understanding.”

He said the strategy for the network’s fourth decade would be guided by three principles.

“Our fourth decade will be defined by three priorities: immediacy, understanding and the human dimension,” he said.

Under the new structure, each of Al Jazeera’s three Arabic-language news channels will have a distinct editorial focus.

Al Jazeera Mubasher, which began broadcasting in 2005, will continue its concentration on uninterrupted live coverage of events, without commentary from presenters or correspondents.

For breaking developments, the new Al Jazeera Now service will focus on developments as they unfold. But unlike a conventional news channel, it will operate without scheduled bulletins or a fixed programme grid.

Meanwhile, the flagship Al Jazeera Arabic channel will retain its news bulletins while pivoting more towards in-depth analysis and investigative journalism, as well as other programmes.

The restructuring is accompanied by plans for 33 programmes, promoted under the slogan “Your questions are our questions”.

Sheikh Nasser said the expansion was intended to provide more context on news events to Arab viewers.

“The real competitor today is not another channel, but noise – the flood of information that makes understanding harder, not easier,” he said, adding that Al Jazeera wanted to fill that gap in understanding.

Hamidi said the new programming would include investigative documentaries, discussions of political and social issues, and programmes examining the historical roots of current conflicts.

The network’s ambitions extend beyond television broadcasting to strengthening Al Jazeera’s local and regional reporting through dedicated digital platforms covering Palestine, Egypt, Sudan, the Gulf and other parts of the Arab world.

The Syria Now platform, which offers documentaries, investigations, and field reporting focused on Syrian affairs, has quickly emerged as one of the primary digital outlets for news on the country.

Looking ahead, the network is also planning a project called Al Jazeera Cultural, provisionally scheduled for launch in late 2027.

The initiative will serve as a space to foster sustained debate about the Arab world’s cultural and intellectual life.

The expansion also forms part of a wider technological transformation, including the development of The Core, an artificial intelligence initiative announced in December 2025 in collaboration with Google Cloud.

The initiative aims to integrate AI into newsroom operations, helping journalists manage information, coordinate coverage and make better use of the network’s reporting archives.

One component, Orbit, is being designed to coordinate newsroom workflows, including monitoring developments, commissioning stories, organising interviews, translating material and managing reporting assignments.

Sheikh Nasser said the first phase of The Core is scheduled to launch on November 1.

Despite the increased use of AI, Sheikh Nasser insisted that human oversight would remain primary.

“Artificial intelligence enables the human and does not replace him,” Sheikh Nasser said. “The main standard for us is conscience and editorial judgement, and editorial judgement is human.”

#664. Farmer-funded attack ads targeting Republicans air across rural America

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sign up now: Get ST's newsletters delivered to your inbox

US Representative Ashley Hinson - a Republican from Iowa and US Senate candidate - and US President Donald Trump (just seen) during a campaign event in Sioux City, Iowa, on Oct 22.

WASHINGTON – A lobbying group that says it represents farmers and meat companies is helping to fund US$3 million (S$3.8 million) in attack ads against Republicans in some of the most competitive US midterm election races, criticising the party’s candidates on cost-of-living and animal-welfare issues.

That sum is a small slice of the billions being poured into the Nov 3 midterms. But President Donald Trump’s Republican Party already faces headwinds among typically stalwart rural supporters due to concerns about tariffs and high fuel costs driven up by the war in Iran.

The ads in Wisconsin, Pennsylvania, Colorado and Iowa are paid for by the political action arm of the American Meat Producers Association (AMPA), an organisation founded in 2025 that represents farmers who support high animal welfare standards.

The group has just one listed employee on its website: its president Holly Bice. It does not disclose its donors or member lists.

But farmers are one source of cash for the midterm ads, according to Reuters interviews with farmers associated with the AMPA.

The ads criticise four incumbent Republicans for supporting a Bill in Congress, the Save Our Bacon Act, which would overturn California and Massachusetts laws that require higher farm animal welfare standards and which have for years divided the pork industry.

The Bill has been endorsed by nearly 400 state and national farm groups.

The Bill has been endorsed by nearly 400 state and national farm groups. AMPA says it represents farmers who oppose the bill.

But the ads also attack the candidates on other topics, like their support for Trump’s tariffs on Canadian goods.

One ad against US Representative Gabe Evans, a Colorado Republican, says he “voted for Trump’s tariffs and the war in Iran, sending prices skyrocketing.”

Evans did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

Many farm groups have remained allied with Republicans this midterm cycle.

For instance, the political action arm of the Iowa Farm Bureau, a branch of the national Farm Bureau lobby group, in September endorsed all Republican candidates for House, Senate and state secretary of agriculture.

Bice, AMPA’s president, said the organisation is running ads on issues beyond its focus of animal welfare because “farmers and ranchers don’t experience policy one Bill at a time”.

“These ads point out that certain lawmakers have voting records that have hurt farmers,” Bice said in response to e-mailed questions, declining to name the major donors to its political action committee running the ads.

Bice said AMPA and its PAC are supported by farmers, meat companies and “people who care about family farming”, but declined to provide more details.

Russ Kremer, a Missouri hog farmer who is on AMPA’s leadership council, said there are roughly 5,000 farmers involved with AMPA and that many had contributed to its PAC, but he did not provide details.

Farmers have been hard hit in 2026 by record diesel prices, higher fertiliser costs and reduced exports to China.

Trump plans to give farmers US$8 billion in diesel aid, bringing government payments to farmers to a record high of US$55.4 billion in 2026, though the funds will not arrive until after the election.

American farmers have been hit hard hit this year by record diesel prices, higher fertiliser costs and reduced exports to China.

AMPA has spent US$2.9 million to date on digital and radio ads, according to federal election filings. Of that, about US$2.5 million has funded ads opposing US Representative Ashley Hinson, an Iowa Republican, in her Senate race, the filings show.

The ads target Hinson’s sponsorship of the Save Our Bacon Act, which Hinson has said would support Iowa’s hog farmers.

“She don’t give a damn about Iowa farmers. She will never feel the pain her votes have inflicted on people like me,” says a man in the ad.

Billy Fuerst, Hinson’s campaign communications director, said: “Iowa farmers strongly support Ashley Hinson because she delivers for agriculture.

“She has even bucked her own party to get year-round E15 passed out of the House, a critical priority for corn farmers and ethanol producers.”

The Bill supported by Hinson would expand sales of E15, a higher-ethanol gasoline blend.

Dean Frazer, an Iowa hog farmer, told Reuters he supports Hinson and the Save Our Bacon Act in part because it is costly for farmers to convert their operations to comply with the higher animal welfare standards.

Hinson has been endorsed by the Iowa Corn Growers Association, as well as the state Farm Bureau chapter.

Trump won Iowa by double digits in the 2024 presidential election, but polls show Democrats tied with or leading Republicans in many of the state’s midterm races.

Rural approval of Trump hit a new low in October of 45%, according to Reuters/Ipsos polling.

AMPA’s spending is not a straightforward effort to elect Democrats. The group has also backed some Republicans who share its views on animal welfare standards.

AMPA has spent US$100,000 on ads supporting Republican incumbents Brian Fitzpatrick of Pennsylvania and Andrew Garbarino of New York, who have opposed the Save Our Bacon Act.

Both are favoured to win in the midterms, according to the latest polls. REUTERS

#60. Brazil’s victory for the planet and democracy: seven lessons from Lula’s win

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The trouncing of the far right provides a playbook for social and environmental movements in other countries facing elections, including the US, Spain and France

Thank you, Brazil, for demonstrating how the far right can be trounced while protection for the Amazon is strengthened. In a world where nature and democracy appeared to be bleeding out in tandem, voters and forest defenders in this global south powerhouse have shown it is possible to staunch the flow. This should provide much needed inspiration and, more practically, a playbook for social and environmental movements in other countries facing elections, including the US, Spain and France.

The astonishing comeback victory of Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva in Sunday’s presidential runoff against his extreme right challenger Flavío Bolsonaro has brought oceans of rapturous supporters on to the streets, while also thrilling conservationists, climate activists and social justice campaigners across the world.

Coming just weeks after the Lula administration announced a record fall in Amazon and Cerrado destruction, they hope Brazil can now go on to achieve its target of zero deforestation by 2030 and continue to support a global transition away from fossil fuels. That would provide a huge boost to flagging global efforts to stabilise the climate and protect degraded ecosystems. It would also set a remarkable example of leadership by the global south.

Big battles lie ahead. In the Amazon region itself, far-right members of Bolsonaro’s party have won the governorships of seven of the nine states and a large number of municipal mayorships. Brazil’s congress is now dominated by agribusiness and mining interests that have set the political agenda in recent years, and will push hard to allow more extractivist industries in indigenous lands, expand road-building, privatise rivers, weaken environmental protections and possibly attempt to impeach the president.

In the regional sphere, Lula, alone among the leaders of main South American nations in not aligning with Donald Trump’s so-called Shield of the Americas, will face pressure from Washington to channel critical minerals and other natural resources to the US, rather than selling to China or Europe, or leaving them in the ground. And, on the planetary level, Brazil faces the threat of accelerating global heating, which is pushing the Amazon ever closer to the point of no return.

So although Sunday’s result will hopefully provide a little breathing space for the Amazon and other crucially important biomes, the joy and hope that Brazil has gifted the world will quickly fade unless it inspires political change in other countries. In that sense, the biggest environmental impact of Lula’s victory may be the lessons it provides for campaigners elsewhere. Here are some initial takeaways:

1 Politics matter. There is no higher environmental priority than defeating the far right, which is a Trojan horse for extractivist – and often criminal – groups to capture states and erode protections for people, democracy and the environment. Just look at the 59.5% surge of Amazon deforestation under the 2019-2022 term of Jair Bolsonaro – the highest level of any four-year presidency in Brazil. Compare that with the 71.2% fall during the Lula administration. Over the past year, forest loss was 3,335 sq km, the lowest level since the start of satellite measurements in 1988. And Lula is not, by any means, an environmentalist. He has approved several destructive projects himself, but he is a people person who listens to the voice of social movements.

2 Don’t give up. Lula was largely written off by the markets and some polls after his first-round defeat. No first-round loser had ever previously come back to win the runoff. Lula was able to do so by securing an extra 10m votes over three weeks. The energy for this came from the streets and the forests. Social movements organised giant rallies in cities. In the Amazon, indigenous, riverine and quilombola groups travelled by hours – sometimes days – to reach polling stations by boats or buses.

3 Mobilise a campaign army. The first round of Lula’s campaign had been stodgy, old-fashioned and defensive – three traits that are also often applicable to environmental campaigns. This was transformed during the runoff, when federal deputy André Janones launched a “Lula’s Army” WhatsApp group that used humorous memes, live streams and algorithmic outreach to seize digital territory that had long been dominated by the right. By voting day, this army had 10m “soldiers”, about 8% of the electorate. Volunteers devoted hours posting stories and spreading messages across social media, speeding up the Lula campaign’s response time and emotional impact. Everyone was instructed not to share or respond to posts by the opposition camp.

4 Go on the offensive against US tech giants. Lula’s campaign accused Meta, which runs the Facebook, WhatsApp and Instagram social media platforms, of political favouritism towards Bolsonaro in its algorithms and AI tools. Meta denied bias but acknowledged technical failures. Brazil’s electoral court ordered corrective action. Among the most popular chants of Lula supporters was “Hey Meta, vai tomar no cu!” (Hey Meta, take it up the arse!). Many on the left have blamed US social media platforms for spreading rightwing disinformation and fearmongering, causing recent electoral defeats in Colombia, Chile and Peru. Lula made clear that would not be allowed in Brazil: “We had to overcome foreign interference through digital platforms, which we will have to regulate. We cannot have a monstrously large corporation earning millions and billions paying no taxes to this country and trying to steer our election results.”

5 Reclaim the flag. Brazil’s green, yellow and blue national colours had been associated with Bolsonaro’s movement in previous elections. This time, Lula seized it back by posing in a national football team strip while his “army” launched a campaign called “The green and yellow is ours”. Before voting day, millions of people across Brazil and the world – from Indigenous leaders in the Amazon to Hollywood celebrities and European football stars – flooded social networks with images of themselves in Brazil colours making an L (for Lula) sign with their thumb and little finger extended.

6 Increase social spending. Amazon protection may have galvanised conservationists, but the far greater motivations for many Lula voters were record low unemployment, robust wage growth, a 15% increase in “bolsa familia” poverty relief payments and a promise to raise the minimum wage. The importance of combining social justice with climate and nature action is widely recognised by Brazilian environmental groups. Without it, elections are lost and protests can erupt in the streets – as the world learned from France during the violent gilets jaunes (yellow vests) demonstrations of 2018.

7 Defend sovereignty, democracy and sustainability. Lula and his army called out the interlinked threats posed by the Bolsonaro family’s fealty to Donald Trump (now doubly confirmed by Flávio’s post-election flight to the US). They highlighted the interlinked threats posed by the obsequience to Trump, Bolsonaro’s desire to change the constitution and his promise to open up exploration of the Amazon and other biomes. This reflects a global phenomenon. Now that seven of the planetary boundaries essential for the flourishing of life have been breached, more and more people are affected by business activities that make the situation worse by destabilising the climate, damaging ecosystems and polluting the air, soil and rivers. This makes it harder for extractive companies to secure a social mandate so they become more inclined to either break the law or support extreme politics that override democracy.

The former environment minister Marina Silva pulled these threads together during the victory celebrations, saying Brazil had come together against these threats: “Long live the people who emerged from every corner of Brazil to sow hope for a future that is socially just, sovereign, democratic and sustainable.”

Brazil has given the world a remarkable example of hope through action. Now it needs others to follow.

#530. The rainfall England needs between now and April to escape drought, according to experts

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Despite rain brought by storms Austen and Boelo, swathes of the UK are still in drought

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Storm Austen and Storm Boelo swept across the UK in quick succession. Rain lashed windows, river levels rose and the clocks went back. It feels like winter has arrived all at once.

Yet most of England and Wales are still in drought. Parts of Cornwall, Devon, Dorset, Somerset and Sussex are in severe drought, the highest level the Environment Agency can declare.

South-west and south-east England have had their driest April to September in over 100 years.

But how can this be when it is cold, dark and pouring with rain? Drought tightens or loosens its grip over months, not just a few days.

It will be the rainy or dry nature of the months ahead that will decide whether the drought ends, or more worryingly, if it stays into next summer.

Summer droughts grab the headlines and public attention. When we have a dry period over the summer it’s everywhere, with brown lawns, hosepipe bans and cracked reservoir beds.

But winter is when the UK refills its water stores.

Between October and March, with shorter and colder days, less water evaporates and plants drink far less, so rain finally has the chance to refill our reservoirs.

Rain can soak deep into the ground to top up groundwater, the water held in chalk and sandstone rocks beneath our feet, like a giant sponge.

Groundwater supplies much of the drinking water in southern and eastern England, but it fills slowly, over months and years.

Think of it like a bank account. A hot summer is a big splurge with lots of withdrawals. But a dry winter means the deposits needed to repay that spending never arrive.

That’s why a dry winter can do more damage to our water supplies than a heatwave.

This year the account is badly overdrawn. Southern England has just had its driest April to September in more than a century, alongside record summer heat.

The Wimbleball reservoir on Exmoor, which supplies around 400,000 people, was full in March, after an extremely wet winter.

Then the weather shifted from wetter than normal to dryer than normal. By October, water levels at Wimbleball were down to about 13%.

Droughts that carry on into winter are rare in the UK. Usually the autumn rain arrives, soil gets wetter and water stores recover by Christmas. Every instinct suggests that a stormy autumn should fix things.

In the 2010 to 2012 drought, two dry winters in a row left groundwater in south and east England so low that hosepipe bans came in during spring 2012. Then the wettest April on record arrived, and parts of the country swung from drought to flood within weeks.

What stands out now is how quickly droughts are stacking up. This is the third drought in England in five years. The stores barely have time to recover before the next one begins.

England and Wales can be in drought and experience flooding simultaneously. After months of dry weather it takes a lot of rain to wet the ground all the way through. Storms such as Boelo and Austen bring bursts of heavy rain.

Drains are overwhelmed and rivers rise quickly, sometimes spilling out onto their floodplains. But the reservoirs and groundwater that supply our taps respond far more slowly, and need lots of water to fill up.

So the type of winter rains make a big difference.

Floods can hit fast. But the key parts of the water cycle are slow.

Hannah Cloke is the Regius Professor of Meteorology and Climate Science at the University of Reading.

This article is republished from The Conversation under a Creative Commons license. Read the original article.

Rain has to wet the soil all the way through before it can trickle down into the rock, and groundwater can take months to respond. The Environment Agency estimates England needs around 120% of normal rainfall between September and April for every area to come out of drought.

We would need half a year with consistently more rain than we would expect at this time of year to break this drought.

If rain falls short across the whole winter, then England could start next summer in drought, with even less room for manoeuvre.

But climate scientists don’t expect there will be a dry winter every year, either now or in decades ahead as fossil fuel burning continues to heat the planet.

Climate projections suggest UK winters will get wetter on average. But averages can hide the danger.

Summers heated by the burning of fossil fuels dry out the soil and drain reservoirs faster, leaving a bigger hole for winter to fill. Winter rain is increasingly arriving in heavy bursts that are harder to capture and more likely to flood. And natural swings in the weather will still deliver dry winters from time to time.

Put those together and the chance of a drought rolling into winter, or across two winters, goes up. This is a trend that will keep getting worse as long as we keep heating the planet.

Preparing starts with how we think about water. It is precious, not endless. Yet we have built a system that lets a wet winter rush away to the sea, then rations what is left through a dry summer.

As with other parts of the UK’s water system, we are paying the price now for years of chronic underinvestment.

Water companies need to move much faster on fixing leaks, which still waste billions of litres every day. New reservoirs are being planned in England, which will help deliver more storage. But they will take more than a decade to build, cost billions of pounds, and cannot be the whole solution.

There is also a low-tech local answer, that would need regulation and a programme of retrofitting to make a big difference. Rainwater harvesting and water reuse in new and existing homes, sustainable drainage in our towns, and encouraging wetlands and floodplains that hold water like a sponge.

Changes to our landscape and homes can help prevent both problems.

Each of us can help. Take shorter showers, ask your water company about a meter and and fit a water butt to catch rain for the garden. These habits add up.

Autumn storms have started to replenish water reserves, but it’s not the finish line. We also need to get ahead of what is likely to happen in years to come.

#540. European stocks rally after ECB and Fed holds

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Stocks in London closed higher on Thursday, as investors weighed earnings, rate calls in Europe and the US and a hefty share price fall for Google owner Alphabet.

In London, the FTSE 100 Index ended up 27.57 points, 0.3%, at 10,722.07.

The FTSE 250 ended 34.90 points higher, 0.1%, at 24,674.16, and the AIM all-share advanced 2.53 points, 0.3%, at 763.45.

In European equity markets on Thursday, the CAC 40 in Paris ended up 1.1%, while the DAX 40 in Frankfurt climbed 1.3% after the European Central Bank’s decision to leave interest rates unchanged.

The central bank maintained its deposit facility rate at 2.50%, the main refinancing operations rate at 2.65% and the marginal lending facility rate at 2.90%, as it warned that rising energy prices could push inflation higher while weighing on economic growth.

“Energy prices have continued to rise, posing upside risks to the inflation outlook and downside risks to the growth outlook,” the ECB said.

Thursday’s decision came hours after ECB president Christine Lagarde announced that she would step down on February 7 2027, eight months before her term was due to expire in October.

Ms Lagarde, who has led the central bank since November 2019, informed the Governing Council and European Council president Antonio Costa of her decision, setting the stage for a leadership contest at a critical time for eurozone monetary policy.

JPMorgan analyst Greg Fuzesi observed: “The decision was expected by financial markets with the expectation that an interest rate hike will follow in December.”

He noted that Ms Lagarde said, however, that there was a debate about hiking at Thursday’s meeting and that the ECB “could” have hiked given the data.

“She also said that barring surprises, ‘the direction of travel is clear to all of us’. A hike in December therefore remains very likely and we continue to expect at least one further hike after that, most likely in March,” Mr Fuzesi said.

Against the dollar, the euro firmed to 1.1162 dollars on Thursday from 1.1148 dollars on Wednesday. Against the yen, the dollar was trading at 157.85 yen, down from 158.23 yen.

The pound was quoted at 1.3369 dollars, up from 1.3310 dollars. Against the euro, sterling climbed to 1.1977 euros from 1.1941 euros.

The Dow Jones Industrial Average was down 0.3% at the time of the closing bell in London on Thursday.

The S&P 500 was also 0.3% lower and the Nasdaq Composite slid 1.3%.

The US central bank also left interest rates on hold on Wednesday, despite accelerating inflation.

At the press conference following the announcement, Federal Reserve chairman Kevin Warsh reaffirmed his determination to ensure price stability as he looked to justify leaving interest rates unchanged despite the backdrop of accelerating inflation.

Mr Warsh told reporters it was the “right policy decision for this moment” and that the US central bank “will deliver price stability, no ifs, no buts”.

Eric Winograd, chief US economist at AllianceBernstein, said he was “struck” again by the gap between Mr Warsh’s “very strong rhetoric” about fighting inflation and the committee’s decision to delay action.

“My perception is that Chair Warsh prefers not to raise rates if he can avoid it. If the data force his hand he will do so, but the bar to a hike seems higher for him than the bar to a hold,” he added.

Morgan Stanley expects two more 25 basis point hikes in December and March, bringing the terminal range to 4.25%-4.5%.

“The Fed’s measured approach, tightening financial conditions, and Chair Warsh’s emphasis on productivity-led growth suggest less need for aggressive tightening,” Morgan Stanley analysts said.

The pressure on the US central bank was reflected in figures on Thursday which showed the personal consumption expenditures (PCE) price index rose 3.6% in September, accelerating from 3.4% in August and meeting the FXStreet cited forecast.

The core reading, which excludes food and energy, is the Federal Reserve’s preferred inflationary measure.

The core PCE price index picked up 3.1% on-year in September, quickening from 3.0% in August and also in line with the market forecast.

On Wall Street, Google owner Alphabet slumped 12% after poorly received earnings.

David Morrison, at Trade Nation, said Alphabet’s falls came as “investors clocked a sharp drop in free cash flow, along with a slowdown in Google’s search engine growth”.

The latter raised concerns that alternative AI large language models were “increasingly eating” into Google’s search engine dominance.

“Both earnings per share and revenues came in below expectations, something that hasn’t happened in close to four years,” Mr Morrison noted.

The yield on the US 10-year Treasury was quoted at 5.00%, trimmed from 5.03%.

The yield on the US 30-year Treasury was quoted at 5.33%, unchanged from Wednesday.

Also supporting equity markets was an easing in oil prices amid a renewed diplomatic push to bring the US and Iran back to the negotiating table.

US vice president JD Vance has contacted Iran’s security chief in a bid to revive peace talks that could head off a renewed American assault on the Islamic Republic, the Wall Street Journal reported on Wednesday.

Brent was quoted at 113.75 dollars a barrel, down from 116.37 dollars at the same time on Wednesday.

In London, WPP rose 5.4% as it reiterated its annual guidance following a third-quarter performance in line with expectations, as the company continued implementing its Elevate28 strategic turnaround plan.

The London-based advertising and marketing services group said revenue for the three months to September 30 rose 0.5% to £3.27 billion from £3.26 billion a year earlier.

Revenue less pass-through costs fell 2.0% on a reported basis to £2.41 billion from £2.46 billion.

Shell gained 2.1%, despite the lower oil price, as it reported better-than-expected third-quarter results and increased the size of quarterly share buybacks.

Adjusted earnings rose to 10.81 billion dollars from 5.43 billion dollars a year ago, beating 10.33 billion dollars consensus compiled for Shell by Vara.

This was supported by higher refining margins, higher realised liquefied natural gas prices and higher integrated gas volumes, partly offset by lower crude and oil products trading and optimisation, higher well write-offs and lower realised liquids prices.

Analysts at RBC Capital Markets commented that Shell’s “underlying earnings and cash flow performance highlight a business that is extremely well suited to take advantage of the current disruptions in the energy complex, across oil, products and gas”.

Elsewhere, Haleon slumped 15% as it lowered full-year revenue guidance after a weaker-than-expected third quarter.

The Surrey-based consumer healthcare company now expects 2026 organic revenue growth to be around 2.5%, down from between 3% and 5% previously.

Haleon, which owns Aquafresh oral health brand, Centrum vitamins and Panadol painkillers, said sales rose 2.5% to £2.87 billion in the three months ended September 30.

Organic growth of 2.6% was below consensus which looked for an increase of 3.4%.

The FTSE 100 listing said performance in the quarter was held back by soft cold and flu consumption leading to lower orders in North America, and a raw material classification change for Centrum over-the-counter products in China which meant it had to “cease importation until further notice”.

Gold was quoted at 4,012.39 dollars an ounce, down from 4,039.18 dollars.

The biggest risers on the FTSE were WPP, up 20.7p at 404.9p, 3i Group, up 77p at 2,432p, London Stock Exchange Group, up 258p at 9,444p, ICG, up 47p at 1,851p and Melrose Industries, up 11.9p at 475p.

The biggest fallers on the FTSE 100 were Haleon, down 56.3p at 317.7p, Lloyds Banking Group, down 5.24p at 98.66p, St James’s Place, down 41p at 1,072.5p, Rio Tinto, down 187p at 6,206p and Diageo, down 36p at 1,701.5p.

Friday’s local corporate calendar has third-quarter results from lender NatWest, oil major BP and drugs firm AstraZeneca.

Friday’s global economic calendar has an interest rate decision in Japan overnight, inflation figures in France and Germany, GDP data in Canada and the Chicago PMI.

#548. Trump’s Cabinet members are violating federal law by joining him on campaign trail, experts allege

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

At least nine of Trump’s 15 cabinet secretaries have been accused of violating the Hatch Act over the past few months, according to a report

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Ethics experts have accused some of President Donald Trump’s Cabinet of violating federal law by joining him on the campaign trail to rally for Republican candidates ahead of the midterm elections.

At least nine of Trump’s 15 cabinet secretaries have been accused of violating the Hatch Act —a federal law prohibiting federal employees from engaging in certain partisan political activities in their official capacity — over the past few months, according to The New York Times.

The president and vice president are exempt from restrictions under the 87-year-old law, but it applies to all other civil employees of the executive branch.

Some cabinet members have been accused of using their official government titles while taking part in campaign rallies and activities, with nonprofit groups filing complaints with the Office of Special Counsel to investigate.

If the office deems the Hatch Act has been violated, penalties can range from a formal warning, suspension and demotion, to termination of employment and being blacklisted from future federal jobs.

Interior Secretary Doug Burgum, Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick and FCC Chairman Brendan Carr were named in a formal complaint brought by Democracy Defenders Fund after they joined Trump at a midterms campaign event in Alaska to rally for GOP Senate candidate Dan Sullivan.

Trump introduced Lutnick and Burgum to the stage by using their official government titles.

Their attendance at the Oct. 13 event raised “serious questions about whether they violated federal laws prohibiting federal employees from using their official authority to influence an election,” the nonprofit said in an Oct. 19 news release.

Carr said he was speaking in a personal capacity, but the nonprofit noted that his remarks “focused on FCC business related to Alaska, including committing to work with Senator Sullivan to get every person in Alaska connected to high-speed internet.”

The Office of Special Counsel declined to comment when reached by The Independent.

“It has long been established that senior administration officials are allowed to discuss current events, policies, and the candidates involved with those policies in order to advance the agenda of the administration,” said White House spokeswoman Lauren Bis.

Elsewhere, Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy endorsed Senate candidate Mike Rogers in Michigan and Attorney General Todd Blanche landed in hot water at a New York rally in August when he appeared to endorse the GOP candidate for governor.

The rally was headlined by Trump, with critics saying it was “beyond inappropriate” for the attorney general to attend a party political event. Blanche was also criticized for speaking at the midterms Republican National Convention event in September.

Other Trump cabinet officials have popped up at midterm campaign rallies as the Republicans look set for a difficult Election Day next week.

Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth was accused by the nonprofit Steady State of violating the Hatch Act while stumping for candidates in Kentucky and Iowa.

“According to the lawyers, I’m only here in a personal capacity today,” Hegseth told the crowd, the Iowa Capital Dispatch reported at the time.

The group has also filed a lawsuit and Hatch Act complaints against several government officials over Trump’s controversial taxpayer-funded ads.

“Officials should not be using their titles or taxpayer resources to be involved in political campaigns, yet the Trump administration continues to increase activity that goes against the letter and the spirit of this principle,” Kedric Payne, the head of ethics for the nonpartisan Campaign Legal Center, told the Times.

#569. FACT FOCUS: Misinformation about voting spreads less than a week before Election Day

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

False and misleading claims about the voting process are being spread online as voters cast their ballots in the 2026 midterms, with Election Day less than a week away.

The posts often focus on alleged election rigging or voter fraud. However, closer scrutiny of the claim typically reveals a logical explanation.

Holes in California ballots are not nefarious

CLAIM: A punched hole in a Ventura County, California, ballot return envelope is proof of election rigging.

THE FACTS: This is false. The ballot return envelopes have two holes, according to election officials. They help election workers confirm that the ballot has been removed and act as an accessibility feature.

A video spreading online shows the front of a return envelope from Ventura County that has a punched hole on one side. A man explains that if ballots are inserted with a certain side facing the front of the envelope, the bubble for Democratic gubernatorial candidate Xavier Becerra is visible through the hole.

The man warns: “Be very careful how you place your ballot in your California envelope.” A popular X post claimed that “California is rigging elections,” using the video as alleged proof.

Simone Seydoux, spokesperson for the Office of the Ventura County Clerk-Recorder & Registrar of Voters, explained that the envelopes follow a design standard recommended by the California secretary of state. Both holes can be seen from the back of the envelope. They appear on each side of the spot voters must sign, acting as a guide for those who are vision impaired. One of these holes goes through the entire envelope so that election workers can ensure it is empty after the ballot has been removed. Workers also secure stacks of emptied envelopes for storage by inserting zip ties through this hole.

“The holes are NOT positioned in order to see the vote on a particular contest,” Seydoux wrote in an email. “The envelopes are ordered ahead of time and used from one election to another if possible.”

Ventura County's 2026 general election ballot consists of three double-sided ballot cards, according to Seydoux. She added that, “voters are welcome to fold the ballot and place it in the envelope in any order.”

The California secretary of state's office confirmed earlier this month that there is nothing untoward about such holes.

“The small holes on some county ballot return envelopes are an accessibility feature to allow sight-impaired voters to orient themselves to where they are required to sign the envelope,” the office wrote in an Instagram post. “If a voter is concerned their ballot choices might be visible through these holes, they can insert their ballots into the return envelope in various ways where all choices are hidden.”

New York City ballots are not missing candidates, may be more than one page

CLAIM: A New York City polling site gave a voter two ballots, one of which was missing the conservative candidates.

THE FACTS: This claim was originally made in a TikTok video by a user who says in the comments that she voted at the Borough of Manhattan Community College in New York City. A sample ballot for that location shows that it consists of two doubled-sided pages. One side of the second page includes judicial races, in which no conservative candidates are running.

“They’re betting on your stupidity and they’re betting on your ignorance that you are not going to see that they’re giving you a ballot that is missing half the candidates,” the woman in the video says. “That’s actually some real foolery and real trickery right there. So honestly, wake up, wake up, wake up, wake up.”

There are four judicial races on the ballot. Democratic candidates are running in all of them and Working Families Party candidates are running in one. There are no Republican or Conservative Party candidates listed since there are none in the race.

“The only reason there would be no candidate on a party line in a contest on the ballot is because no individual was nominated by that party for that particular office,” Kathleen McGrath, a spokesperson for the New York State Board of Elections, wrote in an email. “Of course, all contests offer voters a space for write-in candidates, so voters can write in another person if they do not like the choices printed on the ballot.”

She added that New York City has had multi-page ballots for many years.

The woman in the video also highlights the fact that she was told she didn’t need to show her ID before voting. In New York, voters provide their Department of Motor Vehicles number or the last four digits of their social security number when registering to vote. They may also use another form of valid photo ID or a government document that shows their name and address, such as a utility bill or a bank statement. Voters will be asked for ID at the polls if their identify cannot be verified before Election Day, according to the state’s registration form.

McGrath explained that voters must only verify their identity at the polls via signature if additional identification, such as a photo ID, has already been verified. Their signature is reviewed and approved by two bipartisan poll workers at the site to ensure it matches what is in their voter record.

“Unfortunately, much mis- and disinformation about elections and voting spreads quickly online,” she wrote. “Worse yet, many social media posts are made solely as efforts to troll or get clicks (with no concern for truth or impact). Sometimes, it is simply the individual not understanding the process — which is run securely, transparently, and always by bipartisan officials in New York State. Boards of elections remain the trusted sources for accurate information.”

Alleged vote flipping is attributed to user error

CLAIM: Touchscreen voting machines in multiple states are flipping votes from Democrats to Republicans.

THE FACTS: Election officials say the touchscreen challenges are almost always the result of user error or an easily fixed screen miscalibration, and say the number of complaints this year isn’t higher than it has been in years past, despite outsized attention on social media. They emphasize that voters can select a candidate as many times as needed, consult a poll worker for help and review selections before submitting their ballot.

“Almost all of the claims of vote flipping are understandable human error,” said David Becker, executive director of the Center for Election Innovation and Research, who noted disinformation purveyors often seize on well-meaning concerns to sow doubt in the voting process. “Is there one of us who hasn’t had a butt dial or mistyped something on our phones? It is not nefarious.”

Voters across the country cast ballots in various ways, including with ballot marking devices that print a paper record or with paper ballots they fill out by hand.

The counties where most of the recent vote-flipping social media reports are concentrated use ExpressVote ballot marking devices from a company called Election Systems & Software. The machines let voters make their selections on a touchscreen. Then, a paper record is typically printed for review before the votes are tabulated.

Katina Granger, senior manager of public relations for the Nebraska-based ES&S, said the company works closely with election officials and provides technical support when needed but has not been made aware of any widespread issue with voting machines this cycle.

She said when a voter is having difficulty, they should ask a poll worker for assistance. If they’ve already printed a ballot that doesn’t reflect their intended selections, “they should notify a poll worker rather than cast it so the jurisdiction can follow its procedures for replacing the ballot.”

Marking ballots is normal procedure in many jurisdictions

CLAIM: Ballots marked by poll workers will be disqualified in the 2026 midterm elections.

THE FACTS: Voting procedures vary by jurisdiction, but it is not unheard of for poll workers to mark ballots as part of the process. There are multiple reasons a poll worker might write on a ballot, according to election officials in multiple states. Experts advise voters to check what the rules are in their district and to avoid making stray marks of their own.

In many North Carolina counties, for example, election workers write “P” for provisional or “A” for absentee in a box in the upper left corner of a ballot so that they can be identified if they are put into a tabulator instead of sealed in an envelope as they are supposed to be. Ballots in Illinois are initialed before they are issued to voters.

“I always like to say, get your facts straight from the source, and the source is your local election official,” said Pamela Smith, president and CEO of Verified Voting, a nonpartisan group focused on election technology. “Not your friends, cousin, uncle, on Facebook.”

Smith encouraged voters to seek out information on the websites or social media accounts of those officials. She also suggested asking a poll worker who marks a ballot why they’ve done so if a voter has concerns.

Find AP Fact Checks here: https://apnews.com/APFactCheck.

#607. Former Adidas boss in court after being told to part-demolish £1.3m country home '2 metres too big'

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

The local council imposed an enforcement notice demanding that the Nixons either demolish their new home or carry out comprehensive alterations

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

A former Adidas boss who bought a £1.3m dream home in the country only to be ordered to partly demolish it because it is two metres "too big" is suing the developer who sold him it for £900,000.

Former managing director of Adidas in Korea, Edward Nixon and wife Sally paid a total of £1.3 million for former farm outbuilding West Barn, in Holdenby Road, East Haddon, Northamptonshire, commissioning developer, Barry Howard, and his company to convert it into a sprawling rural home fit for the 21st century.

Mr Howard - head of Northampton-based house building and development company, Barry Howard Homes - sold the couple the £600,000 parcel of land where West Barn stands and had already built a “shell of a house” on the site.

He and the Nixons then struck a £700,000 deal for the developer to “finalise the construction of the house in accordance with drawings provided to the Nixons”.

The couple say they were told by Mr Howard during a video call in July 2020 that “the house is built and the planners are happy with it”.

But they were horrified when in June 2021 the local council imposed an enforcement notice, demanding that the Nixons either demolish their new home or carry out comprehensive alterations, telling them the 36m former barn was bigger than the approved plans and two metres too long.

The case has now spilled over into court, with the Nixons suing Mr Howard for £900,000 for "fraudulent misrepresentation" on the basis he must have known the house was bigger than it should have been when he filled in an enquiry form.

But the housebuilder is disputing the couple's claim, insisting he had no idea that the renovated barn’s dimensions had gone over the limit when he sold it.

Mr Nixon, 56, is an ex MD of Adidas and founder of Brand First Clearance Solutions, a company specialising in advising on clearing excess stock.

Barry Howard, 57, has enjoyed a long career as a successful developer and housebuilder in the Northampton area.

Daria Gleyze, the couple’s barrister, claimed at Central London County Court that Mr Howard made a series of misleading statements which induced his clients to go ahead with buying into the barn conversion project, including a video call in July 2020 in which he declared: “the house is built and the planners are happy with it”.

And in September 2020, when filling out the standard enquiries form questionnaire accompanying the sale contract, Mr Howard answered “no” to the question, “are you aware of any breach of planning law in relation to the construction, use or occupation of the property," the barrister said.

But in truth the finished house project exceeded its permitted length by two metres, she told Judge Nigel Gerald, with West Northamptonshire Council demanding drastic alterations due to “its sheer bulk and mass deviating from the character and form of the original approved building.”

The court heard that the house ultimately produced by Mr Howard’s company, Barry Howard Homes, exceeded its permitted length by around two metres and its “ridge height” by just under one metre.

The local authority decided that the changes had “detracted from the character and appearance” of the original barn, and in December 2020 the Nixons discovered the planning rules had been broken - three months after completing the barn’s purchase.

In June 2021, the local council imposed an enforcement notice demanding that the Nixons either demolish their new home or carry out comprehensive alterations.

The following year council officers finally approved “remedial works” which will result in a “significant decrease in size and a different layout of the house”, explained Ms Gleyze.

“Their new house will have to be much smaller,” she told Judge Gerald.

The Nixons’ legal team say the couple ended up buying the project on the strength of the “false representation” made in the enquiries form, and are now claiming £900,000 compensation from Mr Howard - partly to cover the costs of “making their house smaller”.

They are also suing for “fraudulent misrepresentation” on grounds that Mr Howard must have known the outsize building had breached planning rules when he filled in the enquiry form.

But developer Mr Howard is fighting the Nixons’ claim, insisting he had no idea that the renovated barn’s dimensions had gone over the limit when he signed it away to the couple.

Mr Howard, representing himself in court, insisted he “had no knowledge that this building was being built in breach”, also claiming that Mr Nixon had been fully aware of all the planning developments in relation to the barn, being “involved in every detail of the building’s design”.

But the couple’s barrister branded his defence “demonstrably untrue”, arguing: “It is inconceivable, and wholly fanciful to assert, that Mr Howard, who prides himself on being a professional builder with ample experience of building houses, would not have realised that he had deviated from the plans approved when he constructed the house, especially given the very significant discrepancies between the house as-built and as approved.

“It is clear that the claimants suffered loss as a result of these misrepresentations.

“They were forced to incur significant costs in attempting to remedy the breach of planning and they will end up with a property significantly smaller and less valuable than what they had purchased.

“A valuation dated 13 October 2025 estimated a difference of £350,000 between the value of the house as built and as it would be after remedial planning measures are implemented,“ added Ms Gleyze.

During a court hearing last week, the Nixons’ lawyers asked Judge Gerald to order “summary judgment” against Mr Howard on grounds that he has “no realistic prospect” of defeating the claim against him.

But after a brief hearing, the judge agreed to Mr Howard’s request to have the case adjourned in order for him to gather more evidence and instruct solicitors.

“It seems fair to give Mr Howard a relatively brief opportunity to find new solicitors and put in a witness statement,” he decided.

“The essence of his witness statement would be to explain why he didn’t know at the time that the representation which was made was wrong,” the judge added, although also noting evidence produced by the Nixons from the project architect “on one reading to the effect that prior to September 22 Mr Howard had been told that the planning regulations had been breached”.

The judge adjourned the case until late November to permit Mr Howard time to prepare his case.
