#64. EU chief lays out vision for new Canada partnership at Montreal summit

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ursula von der Leyen reiterated on Thursday her wish for Canada to become an "associate member" of the EU.

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen has laid out her vision for what an "associate membership" status for Canada to the EU would look like as officials gather in Montreal to discuss closer ties.

It is "a partnership that comes close to membership than anything we have done before," she said on Thursday's remarks at the two-day Canada-EU summit.

She added it is a type of relationship that would be open "only to our closest partners outside of Europe who are ready to open a new frontier together."

It includes close alignment on governance and policies, and partnering in research and innovation.

For decades, Canada-EU summits took place without much fanfare - but that's not the case for this year's meeting in the Canadian city.

Thursday and Friday's summit is being closely watched for details on how Canada and the European bloc deepen their ties.

Von der Leyen first proposed "opening the door" to Canada last month for what she called an "associate membership" - a status that does not currently exist.

The overture came after Prime Minister Carney addressed the European Parliament in Strasbourg, telling EU lawmakers that Canada and Europe were "stronger together" in the face of "geopolitical rupture".

Carney and many EU member states welcome closer ties, but not all are on board with what to call this new relationship.

Some, like Germany, are questioning the "associate member" term, fearing that Canada is getting preferential treatment ahead of other nations who also want in.

According to reports by multiple news outlets, Germany and other nations successfully lobbied to remove mention of the title in a draft statement ahead of the summit, opting instead for softer language that references an "ever closer association".

Germany's ambassador to Canada, Matthias LÃ¼ttenberg, told CTV News on Tuesday that some member states were "a little surprised" by von der Leyen's announcement last month.

While there is broad support for closer ties, the concept of "associate membership" is something that is not clearly understood as it pertains to Canada, he said.

"Germany has been using the term of associate membership for something completely different, which is the relationship of the EU to Ukraine and potentially Moldova", he said, both of which have been trying to join the bloc for many years.

Despite some opposition, von der Leyen has continued to use the term has not addressed the disagreement over the term.

Watch: 'A better opportunity' - Canadians on a closer alliance with Europe

Later on Thursday, Carney and von der Leyen and business leaders from both Europe and Canada will gather for a business forum.

Canadian officials said they also expect agreements to be hammered out on defence, critical minerals, research and trade over the two days.

Speaking to reporters earlier this week, European officials downplayed the possibility of a major announcement on Canada's new relationship with EU, saying much will be sorted in the coming months.

But both Canadian and EU officials have provided some clues on what the relationship could look like, including closer business ties and allowing more students to study on either side of the Atlantic.

Canada also does not plan on paying a membership fee similar to other EU nations, Canadian officials have said. Instead, it is looking at participating in different EU programmes that would benefit both Canada and member states.

And despite some disagreement about the wording, both have signalled they are fully on board with deepening ties.

"The wording is emblematic of the ambition," said one senior Canadian official who was granted anonymity to speak to reporters at a briefing ahead of the summit.

Polls show the majority of Canadians support forging closer ties to Europe, external, though the Conservative opposition in Canada has criticised the effort as a distraction from other issues, like the high cost of living.

"No one's mortgage or grocery bill will come down," said Conservative leader Pierre Poilievre in a statement, adding that Canada already has a free trade pact with the EU.

What could EU associate member status mean for Canada?

Canada welcomes EU proposal to become 'associate member'

Many Canadians are boycotting the US, so this is where they're travelling instead

Since taking office last year, Prime Minister Carney has pushed Canada closer to Europe as his country continues to deal with an ongoing trade war with the US, its neighbour and largest trading customer.

US President Donald Trump has called the idea of Canada becoming an associate member of the EU "laughable", and has threatened "very serious tariffs" on Europe if he considered it a "hostile act".

Canadian officials stressed on Wednesday that their pivot to Europe is not meant to offend anybody, but rather to build on work that was already underway.

Canada recently signed an agreement that gives its defence companies procurement access to the EU's Security and Defence Partnership programme.

It also signed a landmark energy deal with Germany in May that would see the first-ever long-term shipments of liquified natural gas from Canada to Europe in the coming years.

On Thursday, Canada released its new National Security Strategy, the first in two decades, which underlines supporting security and stability in Europe as a priority.

As for what this new relationship status will be called, Carney has said the "nomenclature" does not matter.

"What matters is the difference it will make to the lives of Canadians," he said.

Real Madrid’s Kylian Mbappe should be allowed to move on after apology to China, says French football federation chief.

Mbappe’s club ⁠Real Madrid and La Liga have also apologised following the remarks, while the league’s president Javier Tebas set up a crisis unit and met Chinese ambassador to Spain, Yao Jing, ⁠on Wednesday as Spanish football sought to contain the fallout.

The backlash highlights the risks for athletes and brands in the lucrative Chinese market, where satire about Mao, modern China’s founding leader who governed the country from 1949 until his death in 1976, remains ‌a social taboo.

China’s state-owned broadcaster Migu is not showing Real Madrid’s game at Racing Santander on Sunday, and Chinese brands including dairy companies Mengniu and Shanghai Milkground Food Tech, as well as Eastroc Beverage, have ceased all cooperation with Mbappe.

Diallo expressed hope the issue would soon be ⁠resolved.

“I believe we should now leave it at that so that he can continue his ⁠season in the best possible conditions.”

The controversy stems from ⁠a long-running joke surrounding 27-year-old Mbappe’s influence at club level.

In a video on a French podcast that has since been taken down, Mbappe is heard discussing a meme in which his face had been ‌photoshopped onto that of “some guy”, adding: “I think he’s a Chinese dictator.”

Fans have previously created memes superimposing Mbappe’s face on images of military and political leaders to suggest ‌he ‌has the power to influence decisions at the teams he plays for. Mbappe has been nicknamed “the dictator” in the European press.

A spokesman for China’s Ministry of Foreign Affairs said the government is taking Mbappe’s apology “seriously”.

#494. Iranian media debate new war amid Hormuz attacks, military buildup

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The prospect of another US-Iran war has fuelled debate over whether Washington is preparing for a prolonged campaign of military and economic pressure or whether diplomacy can still avert a wider conflict.

The debate comes amid continuing attacks on shipping in the Strait of Hormuz, warnings from Tehran that it is better prepared for another confrontation, and fresh revelations about Iran's efforts to circumvent sanctions and the US naval blockade to import military equipment.

Iran's Revolutionary Guards said Thursday that three tankers had struck naval mines in the strait over the previous 24 hours. Vice President Mohammadreza Aref said Tehran was managing the strategic waterway and that its defensive readiness had improved since the 12-day and 40-day wars.

Analyses published in Iranian newspapers in recent days offer differing assessments of the likelihood of another confrontation and the prospects for a negotiated settlement.

The economic daily Donya-ye Eghtesad said war was not inevitable, pointing to continuing backchannel contacts despite deep disagreements between the two sides.

Political analyst Rahman Ghahramanpour told the paper that another conflict would be unlikely to end after only a few days, warning that even limited US military action could become part of a sustained campaign combining strikes and sanctions.

Ghahramanpour described a possible eight-to-twelve-month strategy aimed at weakening Iran's ability to resist US demands.

Reuters investigation published Thursday revealed how Iran has used a network of more than 5,000 military-linked agencies to import at least $2 billion worth of equipment, explosives and heavy-duty trucks since January 2025, circumventing Western sanctions and the US naval blockade.

The findings shed light on Tehran's efforts to sustain its military capabilities despite the pressure campaign.

Former diplomat Kourosh Ahmadi challenged predictions that another war was imminent, arguing that the remaining disagreements between Tehran and Washington might not be substantial enough to justify the costs of a new conflict.

Writing in the reformist daily Shargh, Ahmadi said President Donald Trump's statements should not be treated as evidence that he had decided to attack Iran, while warning that another war could have devastating consequences.

He identified three broad options available to Washington: pursue a diplomatic settlement, continue economic and military pressure to weaken Iran, or undertake another military operation.

Diplomacy remained possible, Ahmadi argued, because the dispute concerned the sequencing of concessions more than fundamentally incompatible objectives.

The moderate news website Fararu examined the damage already being inflicted by the prolonged stalemate.

Former diplomat Fereydoun Majlesi argued that Iran's state of “neither war nor peace” was itself becoming a major economic burden, with uncertainty prompting businesses to hold on to cash and avoid investment.

Such decisions weaken production, employment and economic growth over time, he said, even without another military confrontation.

Both sides, Majlesi said, continued to define an acceptable agreement primarily in terms of their own interests while resisting the concessions necessary to bridge the gap.

He warned that prolonged uncertainty could make an eventual settlement more difficult as economic pressure and mutual distrust intensified.

For Ghahramanpour, the question was not simply whether Washington might strike again, but whether military pressure could secure political concessions without triggering a wider war.

The departure of Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu could benefit Iran even if his successor maintained the same policies toward Tehran, Khorasan, a conservative Iranian newspaper close to Parliament Speaker Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf, said on Thursday.

The newspaper said a change in Israeli leadership would not necessarily reduce military threats against Iran but could weaken the political and diplomatic networks Netanyahu has built during his years in power.

"Ultimately, Netanyahu's departure is in Iran's interest, not because his successor would necessarily adopt a friendly approach, but because removing one of the main architects of the confrontation with Iran would reduce the accumulated political and diplomatic capacity behind it," Khorasan wrote.

The assessment came after Israel's parliamentary election on Tuesday produced no clear winner, leaving Netanyahu and his main rival, former military chief Gadi Eisenkot, seeking support to form a government.

With nearly 86% of votes counted on Wednesday, Israeli broadcasters projected Netanyahu's right-wing bloc would secure 53 seats in the 120-member Knesset, compared with 48 for Eisenkot's opposition bloc and 19 for Arab-led parties.

Neither main bloc reached the 61 seats needed for a majority, leaving the outcome dependent on coalition negotiations.

Netanyahu's experience seen as key factor

Khorasan said Israeli political groups shared many positions on security matters despite their differences and that Eisenkot, a former military chief, was not opposed to confrontation with Iran.

It argued, however, that Netanyahu had built political and diplomatic networks over many years that a successor could find difficult to replace.

"Netanyahu is not merely a prime minister," the newspaper wrote, adding that he had developed extensive political, regional and international connections and considerable experience influencing US decision-making.

The newspaper said Netanyahu knew how to make Iran a central issue in Washington's foreign policy and connect security developments with political opportunities.

"Removing such a figure from the top of power, even if the basic structure of Israeli policy remains unchanged, means losing part of this accumulated capacity," it said.

"Netanyahu and his allies have built ties over the years between the Israeli government and some opponents of the Islamic Republic, particularly the monarchist movement," the newspaper wrote.

"Netanyahu's departure could weaken part of this political image, although one should not expect all these relationships to disappear with a change in leadership," it added.

It said political divisions within a future Israeli coalition could make decision-making more difficult, although such disagreements would not automatically reduce the risk of military action.

Election leaves Netanyahu and Eisenkot seeking coalition partners

Netanyahu's Likud-led bloc emerged ahead of Eisenkot's opposition in the preliminary results but remained short of a parliamentary majority.

Parties representing Arab Israelis were projected to secure an unprecedented 19 seats following high voter turnout, giving them a potentially important role in determining the next government.

Eisenkot could seek support from Arab-led parties to secure a majority, although several parties within his opposition bloc have opposed such cooperation.

Khorasan said the high turnout among Arab voters was one of the election's most important developments but questioned whether Eisenkot could reach an agreement with Arab parties while maintaining support from his existing allies.

Reuters reported that Netanyahu's electoral performance had strengthened his position in coalition negotiations despite the loss of his previous parliamentary majority.

The prime minister will remain in office in a caretaker capacity while the process of forming a new government continues.

Iran remains central to Israeli political debate

The election took place during the US-Israeli war on Iran, which began in February, with the possibility of further military action remaining an issue in Israeli politics.

Netanyahu has repeatedly emphasized the Iranian threat during the campaign and has received public support from US President Donald Trump.

Eisenkot has criticized Netanyahu's handling of security matters and warned against the political use of military decisions.

Khorasan said Netanyahu's departure would not necessarily bring a change in Israel's approach toward Iran, noting that different political groups shared positions on key security issues.

"Internal disagreements do not necessarily lead to a reduction in military threats," the newspaper wrote, adding that political competition could sometimes result in more aggressive behavior.

It said the removal of Netanyahu would not end the confrontation between Israel and Iran but could reduce the ability of Israeli leaders to coordinate political and diplomatic efforts against Tehran.

"Sometimes removing one player does not, by itself, end the game, but it can make the game more difficult for the opposing side," Khorasan concluded.

US President Donald Trump confirmed he approved Vice President JD Vance's outreach to Iran, saying it focused on medical supplies, as Washington weighs a possible week-long military campaign against Tehran after next week's midterm elections.

Trump said on Wednesday that Vance had sought his approval to contact Iranian authorities but denied directing him to restart peace negotiations.

"They need help with medical supplies and various things. He asked me what I thought. I said, 'if you want to do that, that's OK,'" Trump told reporters.

"Basically, it's getting supplies in to help people that are not well," he added.

The Wall Street Journal reported that Vance had sent a letter to Mohsen Rezaei, secretary of Iran's Supreme National Security Council, seeking to restart negotiations between Washington and Tehran.

The diplomatic contacts come as regional mediators led by Qatar seek an agreement to prevent further military action, while US and Israeli officials prepare for possible strikes after the November 3 elections.

Vance seeks discussions on Iran's nuclear program

Vance's letter to Rezaei stressed that any negotiations aimed at preventing further US military strikes would require discussions on Iran's nuclear program, Axios reported, citing a US official.

The official said Vance had proposed contacting Rezaei, who has direct access to Iran's Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei, and that Trump had supported the initiative.

"The President was enthusiastic about the letter," the official told Axios.

The Wall Street Journal reported that the outreach focused on exploring a possible framework for renewed negotiations rather than presenting a detailed peace proposal.

According to the newspaper, Vance had previously expressed reservations in private about prolonging the war, which began in February, while publicly supporting the US military campaign.

The vice president previously led US negotiations with Iranian representatives, but those efforts did not produce a lasting agreement.

A central issue remains Washington's demand for nuclear concessions before economic relief, while Tehran wants the US to lift its naval blockade, release frozen Iranian funds and allow oil exports before negotiations move forward.

Qatar leads efforts to restart negotiations

Regional mediators led by Qatar, with support from Pakistan, Saudi Arabia, Egypt and Turkey, are working to restart negotiations between Washington and Tehran, according to Axios.

Qatari negotiator Ali al-Thawadi visited Tehran earlier this week to discuss possible terms for an agreement with the Trump administration.

One proposal under discussion would allow inspectors from the International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA) to visit Iranian nuclear facilities damaged during US and Israeli attacks in exchange for limited sanctions relief.

The arrangement could include waivers allowing Iran to export oil and the release of some frozen funds to purchase food and other humanitarian supplies, Axios reported, citing a regional source familiar with the discussions.

The proposals seek to address differences between Washington's demand that Iran give up its enriched uranium stockpile and Tehran's insistence that the US first ease economic restrictions.

"There is some movement. We are waiting for the Iranian response," the regional source told Axios.

The source said the proposed arrangement could allow Iran to retain its enriched uranium initially while Washington secured steps toward renewed nuclear oversight.

Trump confirmed to Axios on Wednesday that negotiations were taking place through mediators but said their outcome remained uncertain.

"We need to finish the job — either through a deal or another way — we will find out if negotiations can succeed," Trump said.

Qatari mediators also presented Iran with ideas for a seven-day process to address concerns on both sides, Reuters reported on Wednesday, citing an official briefed on the negotiations.

According to the official, the draft calls for daily confidence-building measures from each side, with the process potentially beginning early next week.

Several countries would serve as guarantors of the agreement, the official said, adding that Iran was expected to respond by the end of the week.

However, a senior Iranian official told Reuters that Qatar had not presented a formal peace proposal but had instead put forward ideas Tehran considered unacceptable.

The official said Iran would show no flexibility on the nuclear issue.

Another option under discussion involves reviving a September 2025 agreement between Iran and the IAEA in Cairo that provided a framework for inspections of Iranian nuclear facilities.

The agreement was suspended after the UN Security Council reimposed sanctions on Iran the following month.

IAEA Director General Rafael Grossi told the UN Security Council on Tuesday that reviving the Cairo agreement could provide a basis for restoring inspections at facilities damaged during the war.

US and Israel prepare for possible week-long military operation

US and Israeli officials are preparing for possible renewed military action against Iran after the November 3 midterm elections, according to reports by Axios and the Wall Street Journal.

Axios reported, citing US and Israeli officials, that a potential military operation could involve a week of extensive strikes against Iranian leadership, energy infrastructure, nuclear facilities and other targets.

Israeli military chief Eyal Zamir spoke with US Central Command chief Admiral Brad Cooper on Monday to coordinate preparations for possible attacks, the report said.

The Wall Street Journal separately reported that Washington had warned several Persian Gulf countries to prepare for a possible short, intense military campaign following the elections.

The US military has been strengthening regional missile defenses, relocating personnel from vulnerable bases and replenishing munitions, according to the newspaper.

Additional naval and air forces are also being prepared for possible operations.

Trump has not made a final decision on further military action, the reports said.

Saudi officials have urged Washington to allow more time for negotiations before deciding on further strikes, Axios reported, citing a US official.

Saudi, Turkish and Pakistani security officials also discussed the situation during recent meetings in Tehran, according to the report.

A reported order by Iran’s supreme leader to end the state of “neither war nor peace” may signal a turn towards diplomacy. But a closer reading suggests Tehran is offering no concessions, only restating its longstanding demands for ending the confrontation with Washington.

On Tuesday, Iranian state media announced that Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei had received Maj. Gen. Mohsen Rezaei, secretary of the Supreme National Security Council, and ordered an end to “the war and the ‘neither war, nor peace’ conditions.”

Abroad, that was read as Tehran wanting out. It wants nothing of the sort. Read the whole sentence, and the “order” is the same demand the Islamic Republic has made since the shooting stopped in April: the stalemate ends when Washington capitulates.

The news cycle started with a readout released by Mojtaba’s official website, which was reproduced word for word across the domestic press. The item is a summary, not a transcript. Every line attributed to Mojtaba is in reported speech, without a single direct quotation.

He is said to have called for “exerting control over the Strait of Hormuz,” declared the enemy “desperate,” bound Iran’s “interests” to those of its regional allies in the “Axis of Resistance,” and called for an end to the war “through realization of the conditions set out by the country’s officials.”

That last clause is the whole message, and the conditions it refers to are no secret. Rezaei himself set them out in September: an end to the war on all fronts, Lebanon included; the release of Iran’s frozen funds; an end to the US naval blockade of Iranian ports; and guarantees against renewed strikes.

Earlier versions added compensation for war damages, the lifting of sanctions and recognition of Tehran’s control over the strait. In short: Tehran keeps its grip on Hormuz, its regional allies stay in place, and Washington concedes. Then, and only then, the stalemate ends. Not one condition softened, not one position moved.

Now consider what the readout is not. It is not independent evidence that a meeting took place. It carries no date and no place. Under Ali Khamenei, the leader’s office opened such items with “this morning” or “this afternoon” and named the venue, usually the Imam Khomeini Hussainiya in Tehran. That convention has quietly disappeared.

The readout carries no image, either. No independently verified new photograph, audio or video of Mojtaba Khamenei has surfaced since the February strike that killed his father. The same was true of the four earlier meetings state media has reported for him, twice with the president, once with the parliament speaker and once with the chief justice.

Eight months in, the authorities have offered only written accounts of these meetings, without independently verifiable visual or audio evidence. When and where they took place, and whether the official accounts are accurate, remain unverified.

Some have nonetheless read the announcement as significant, on the grounds that Mojtaba called for an end to the war. The record says otherwise: the conditional clause simply restates Tehran’s standing terms.

A call to end the stalemate with no change in terms is the oldest tactic in the Islamic Republic’s repertoire: stall, buy time, rebuild; when the pressure rises, flirt with peace; and when an opening appears, strike.

The readout did not specify when or where the meeting took place. Although it was published Tuesday, a day after Saudi, Turkish and Pakistani envoys visited Tehran, the text does not establish that the meeting occurred that day or that it was connected to the diplomatic visit.

What “neither war, nor peace” means in the mouth of a Khamenei, the family’s own usage helps explain.

In August 2018, Ali Khamenei told a public audience that there would be no war and no negotiation with the Trump administration, because talks would end to Tehran’s detriment. The government daily Iran bannered it the next morning: “there will be no war, and we will not negotiate.” In May 2019, he called negotiation “poison” and declared the nation’s choice to be “resistance.” The limbo was Tehran’s own posture, and a boast.

After the twelve-day war of June 2025, he flipped the phrase. Addressing the cabinet on September 7, he said hope and productivity had to prevail over the “neither war, nor peace state that the enemy wants to impose on us” as part of its “psychological war.” His remedy was self-reliance: “don’t wait for external developments.” The limbo was now the enemy’s doing; the answer was still not to talk.

President Masoud Pezeshkian has leaned on that speech ever since, most recently on October 24, when he said, “Many are struggling for their evening bread” because of the war and the stalemate, adding, “One cannot plan in limbo... The martyred Leader ordered us to go and finish the job.”

The IRGC’s press corps answered within a day. Javan headlined that exiting the stalemate “will not come through negotiation” but through “changing the enemy’s calculations.” Fars News presented the phrase as the doctrine of the “martyred Imam,” with negotiation a “strategic trap.”

Tuesday’s readout appears to settle that argument in the IRGC’s favor. The son inherits the father’s phrase and makes it a condition for ending the confrontation: the stalemate is over when the other side accepts Tehran’s terms.

Three uses of one phrase, one underlying position. Refuse talks in 2018. Blame the enemy for the stalemate in 2025. Demand an end to the stalemate on Tehran’s terms in 2026. At no point has “ending neither war nor peace” meant Tehran moving an inch.

Meanwhile, the authorities continue to announce meetings involving a leader who has not appeared publicly in independently verified images or recordings. This is not an order to end the stalemate. It is the stalemate, restated as a demand.

The IRGC-linked Tasnim news agency has set out the case for Iran to withdraw from the Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty (NPT), adding to a renewed debate over Tehran's nuclear strategy as the region remains caught between diplomacy and war.

In a commentary published Wednesday, Tasnim argued that the treaty had failed to protect Iran's interests and questioned the benefits of remaining a member after successive wars with Israel and the United States.

The intervention comes as Qatar promotes a diplomatic framework reportedly linking the reopening of the Strait of Hormuz, sanctions relief and efforts to reduce tensions involving Yemen's Houthis.

It also follows signals from Tehran that its leadership wants to end the current diplomatic and military stalemate.

Supreme National Security Council (SNSC) Secretary Mohsen Rezaei said Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei had told him during a meeting on Tuesday that it was time to end the state of “no war, no peace.”

President Masoud Pezeshkian said Wednesday that his government would do everything it could to fulfil the leader's wishes.

Tasnim's commentary, titled “Why Should Iran Leave the NPT?”, highlights divisions over how Tehran should respond to international pressure on its nuclear program.

Hardline politicians have repeatedly raised the prospect of leaving the NPT during periods of nuclear negotiations, arguing that the treaty has failed to protect Iran's interests.

Tasnim identified three central objectives of the treaty: preventing the spread of nuclear weapons, encouraging nuclear-armed states to disarm and protecting members' right to use nuclear technology for peaceful purposes.

The agency argued that the treaty had failed on all three counts.

It accused the International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA) of abandoning its role as an impartial technical body and becoming politically aligned with the United States and Israel.

The agency had failed to defend Iran's rights when its nuclear sites were attacked, so withdrawal would be a legitimate option for Tehran, Tasnim argued.

Tasnim maintained that withdrawal would not necessarily mean developing nuclear weapons. Some lawmakers, however, have explicitly linked the two.

On Tuesday, MP Abolfazl Aboutorabi called for Iran to withdraw from the NPT and begin building a nuclear bomb.

Other hardline figures, including members of the ultraconservative Paydari Front, have advocated withdrawal, arguing that continued membership has failed to prevent attacks on Iranian nuclear facilities despite their being subject to international safeguards.

The calls have met resistance from other establishment-aligned media, reflecting disagreements over the potential costs of withdrawal.

Khabar Online, a pragmatic-conservative outlet, sharply criticized the campaign in an October 25 report, singling out the Paydari Front and questioning the political judgment of those advocating departure from the treaty.

Its headline described the proposal as a radical demand dating back two decades, with “neither head nor tail.”

The reformist Arman Melli newspaper has also taken a position against withdrawal, broadly reflecting the Pezeshkian administration's preference for maintaining Iran's existing treaty commitments.

Nour News, which is close to the SNSC, adopted a more cautious approach in its own October 25 analysis.

It argued that neither withdrawal nor continued membership could guarantee Iran's security and that dissatisfaction with the IAEA did not, by itself, justify abandoning the treaty.

The outlet also warned that withdrawal from the NPT would create further political and diplomatic complications, while remaining in the treaty would still allow Tehran to restrict aspects of its cooperation with the IAEA.

Withdrawal, it concluded, should not be considered without a clear plan for what would follow.

Leaving the NPT would have implications beyond Iran's relations with the IAEA.

Under Article X of the treaty, a state party may exercise its right to withdraw if it determines that extraordinary events have jeopardized its supreme interests. It must give three months' notice to other treaty parties and the UN Security Council, explaining the events behind its decision.

Withdrawal could further complicate Tehran's relations with Western governments and raise questions about the future of international monitoring of its nuclear activities.

Despite the calls from hardline lawmakers and media outlets, Iran has announced no decision to leave the treaty.

The government continues to maintain that its nuclear program is peaceful, and neither the SNSC nor Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei has publicly endorsed withdrawal.

The competing arguments underscore a debate within Iran's political establishment: whether abandoning the treaty would strengthen Tehran's position after repeated military attacks or leave it more isolated and vulnerable as regional powers seek a diplomatic settlement.

Iranian lawmakers are reviewing a new internet regulation bill that could expand authorities' powers to restrict online access, raising concerns over further controls after months of nationwide internet shutdowns.

The proposed Cyberspace Regulation Bill is being examined by a parliamentary committee, but its official text remains unpublished, Iranian news outlet Jamaran reported.

Parliament received the proposal on August 3, but lawmakers reviewing it have declined to disclose its provisions.

"A decision has been made not to publicize the details of the Cyberspace Regulation Bill for the time being," Rouhollah Abbaspour, spokesman for the parliamentary committee, said.

An unofficial 12-article draft circulating online resembles the controversial Internet Protection Bill, known as the Sianat plan, which stalled in the previous parliament. However, a committee member told local media that the version under review differs from the circulating text.

The proposal has drawn criticism within parliament, with some lawmakers questioning the need for additional controls when international internet access is already heavily restricted.

Mostafa Pourdehghan, a member of parliament's Industries and Mines Committee, criticized repeated attempts to restrict international connectivity and expand domestic alternatives, citing existing filtering, poor internet quality and rising costs.

Pourdehghan warned that further restrictions during wartime could increase public anxiety, particularly among young people, and predicted the bill would meet the same fate as the earlier so-called Protection Plan.

Lawyer Kambiz Norouzi warned that the proposed legislation could give the Supreme Council of Cyberspace extensive authority while weakening government oversight.

In an editorial published by Shargh newspaper, Norouzi criticized Article 1 of the circulating draft, which designates the council as the highest authority for internet policymaking.

Article 6 would also allow internet access to be suspended or restricted on orders from judicial authorities or other competent bodies, without clearly identifying those additional authorities.

"This provision amounts to creating unlimited and unregulated power to violate fundamental freedoms and is certainly unconstitutional and illegitimate," Norouzi wrote.

Norouzi also argued that the bill would concentrate decision-making in an institution whose proceedings are largely closed to public scrutiny.

The official parliamentary text has not been published, leaving uncertainty over whether these provisions appear in the version under review.

Months of shutdowns and previous restrictions

The proposal follows months of internet disruption affecting millions of Iranians.

Authorities imposed a nationwide blackout on January 8, 2026, during anti-government protests. Connectivity was only partially restored before another near-total shutdown began on February 28 following the outbreak of war involving Iran, Israel and the United States.

The second blackout lasted 88 days, with international connectivity beginning to return on May 26.

Restrictions disrupted businesses, education and communications. Iran's Chamber of Commerce estimated direct economic losses at $30 million to $40 million daily, with indirect losses potentially doubling that figure.

The legislation follows years of attempts to expand state control over online communications.

The earlier so-called Protection Plan sought greater oversight of foreign platforms but stalled following public opposition.

In July 2025, President Masoud Pezeshkian's administration also withdrew a separate cyber censorship bill following criticism over its potential impact on free expression.

Authorities have continued developing the National Information Network, which allows selected domestic services to operate independently of international connectivity.

The Supreme Council of Cyberspace, established by former Supreme Leader Ali Khamenei in 2012, already plays a central role in internet policymaking.

For now, lawmakers remain divided over the proposal's prospects, while the refusal to release its official text has left its scope, legal implications and potential impact on internet access unclear.

#671. HMRC targets 200 billionaires in crackdown on tax avoidance

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

The tax authority says it is aiming to help taxpayers ‘understand and meet their obligations’

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

HMRC has assigned dedicated tax compliance managers to every billionaire linked to the UK in a bid to maximise revenue and crackdown on avoidance.

Over 200 billionaires have been identified by the tax authority, and will now be paired with a ‘Customer Compliance Manager’ who will offer pre-filing discussions to help manage their affairs.

The initiative has been launched in a bid to close what HMRC terms the “wealthy tax gap,” which was estimated at £3.6bn in 2024/2025. This represented six per cent of the entire tax gap – the difference between the amount of tax that should have been paid to the government and what was collected.

Labour MP James Murray said: “Wealthy taxpayers make a significant contribution to the Exchequer and wider economy; the 1.3 million individuals in the population accounted for £160 billion in tax receipts in 2025 to 2026.

“Their tax affairs can involve multiple taxes, businesses, investments, trusts and jurisdictions, making effective tax administration increasingly dependent on HMRC having a joined-up understanding of their circumstances,” the financial secretary to the Treasury and paymaster general added.

“Our aim is to make it easier for taxpayers to understand and meet their obligations, using better information to reduce unnecessary contact and identify potential problems earlier, whilst concentrating specialist compliance activity on the areas of greatest risk.”

These 200 billionaires have been identified as those who have a UK tax footprint. This means they are required to submit a UK self-assessment return, but may not be a resident. They join a collection of around 2,400 wealthy individuals who already have a compliance manager in the UK.

The move comes alongside a plan to use new technology to monitor the tax affairs of these wealthy individuals. This will include “advanced analytics, automated triggers and artificial intelligence (AI) to identify emerging risks earlier and target compliance activity where it will have greatest impact”.

In a new report, HMRC writes: “Wealthy customers’ tax affairs can involve several UK and international income sources, financial accounts, assets, entities and advisers.

“Better information and modern technology can make tax administration simpler and more effective. They can help customers and their advisers understand their tax position and get their tax right, while giving HMRC earlier insight into potential risks and better tools to manage complex tax affairs.”

It adds that, alongside hiring 400 new tax specialists, the changes will raise at least £500m as part of a larger commitment to claw back £10bn in additional tax revenues by 2030.

Last year, HMRC was criticised by the Public Accounts Committee for failing to track how many billionaires pay tax in the UK. There were “significant opportunities to collect more revenue” being missed through the lack of oversight, the government’s spending watchdog found at the time.

#690. What was the best and worst news for small firms in the Budget?

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Chancellor John Healey gave with one hand and took with the other

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

The Independent SME Channel is brought to you by Hostinger.

The 2026 Budget is in the books and it will not go down as one of the most notable ever seen.

Chancellor John Healey was clearly keen to tread lightly and steer away from anything that would rock the boat for an already struggling economy.

There was still some substance there from the perspective of small businesses, some of which was positive – but some that was bad news.

Raising small business rates relief thresholds to £18,000 and £21,000 is a decent helping hand for some businesses.

Phil Vernon, head of specialist business rates projects at PwC UK, described it as a welcome boost for the high street.

“It will take around 70,000 more shops, cafés and pubs out of business rates entirely, and a two-year real-terms freeze gives the rest some certainty,” he said.

“But small business rates relief is generally limited to businesses with a single property, so a local baker or café owner with a second shop will often get none of it, though they will still benefit from the freeze. That also sharpens the cliff edge: opening a second site could now cost a growing business more in lost relief than ever before.”

Another helpful move is the trade allowance being tripled. While it has only moved from £1,000 to £3,000, it will take some people with small side hustles out of paying tax.

Lee Murphy, managing director of the Accountancy Partnership, said: “Raising the trading allowance is probably the most useful measure announced in the Budget for anyone with a side hustle. From selling on Vinted to tutoring in the evenings, anyone with a side income can earn up to £3,000 per year and have nothing to file.

Everything you need to build, launch and grow online

Everything you need to build, launch and grow online

“In a practical sense, this takes a lot of people out of self-assessment altogether. My warning to those who still need to submit a return is that the allowance can still be used to reduce your tax bill, but only instead of claiming expenses, and does not sit on top of them.

“If your costs are more than £3,000, claiming them could be better. You’d also still need to keep records to prove your income.”

Other measures that provide a small boost included changes to mileage cost rules and paternity leave entitlement.

Josh Toovey, head of policy and research at IPSE, said: “Some of these tweaks are genuinely helpful. Reversing the cash ISA cut, better mileage rates, parental pay and an increased trading allowance will all make a difference, and business rates reform is a welcome boost for our smallest high street businesses.

“But taken together, they don’t go anywhere near far enough.”

The minimum wage has been raised by 3.9 per cent, which will add to the cost of running many businesses.

For workers aged 21 and over, it will rise from £12.71 an hour to £13.21.

The rate for workers aged 18-20 will go from £10.85 an hour to £11.27.

Large corporations may not feel the squeeze too much and have the market power to pass the cost through to their customers, but many SME businesses could feel a real hit to their books.

Mr Healey also made some ominous comments about changes to filing requirements for self-assessment taxpayers.

He said HMRC will “reform” self-assessment in 2029. He wants to bring these taxpayers “into line with the majority” by replacing large annual or semi-annual bills with smaller, more regular payments.

This is already in motion to some degree, with self-assessment payers above staggered thresholds having to start filing quarterly returns. The government has badged it up as Making Tax Digital. The first threshold was £50,000 and came in this past April.

The aim from the government’s point of view is not to make life easier or more profitable for businesses, but to get more money into HMRC, more quickly.

This is an attack on small businesses, as it creates a much bigger admin burden. Filing a tax return quarterly is essentially four times the work as filing annually, however you dress it up.

The other aspect is that it will cost people money in a couple of ways. If you are unable to do your own returns, it means you will need to pay an accountant. It is highly unlikely accountants will take care of four returns for a similar price to one.

Another way it will cost people money is that a pay as you earn system will move money from a small business bank account to the government’s pocket earlier.

Small businesses will therefore lose all the interest that would have been earned from that money being in their accounts during the year.

#801. Pentagon suspects hackers caused mysterious freezer malfunctions at military bases

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Military investigators are in touch with their French counterparts about a cyberattack that crippled supermarkets there

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

The Pentagon believes a spate of freezer malfunctions that spoiled food at military bases across the United States in August may have been the work of hackers, according to a new report.

The malfunctions affected at least six commissaries in late August, according to a Reuters review. Commissaries are stores on military bases where service members buy their food.

In at least one of the cases, the cooling units' temperatures were cranked up overnight, leading to mounds of wilted or melted food in the morning.

“Trust us when we say it was 'spoiled,'" Fort Huachuca in Arizona said in a Facebook post on Aug. 26 after freezers "went into defrost mode where it heated up all the food.”

Military investigators were in touch with their French counterparts about a similar cyberattack on commercial freezers that disrupted supermarkets there earlier this month.

The US Department of the Army Criminal Investigation Division said its Cyber Field Office was involved in the investigation and that it was working with French law enforcement to better understand "the refrigeration outages in Europe."

When asked about the investigation by Reuters, the Pentagon said that a unit of Cyber Command – the military's cyber force – was "working in close coordination with interagency and law enforcement partners."

The Pentagon seemingly has not reached a conclusion about what happened or who might be responsible. The spate of rogue freezer behavior and the possibility of cybersabotage were first reported on August 28 by a blog called Signal and Silence.

If the military base malfunctions are confirmed to be a cyberattack, it would be yet another illustration of how hackers can target vulnerable critical infrastructure, including cold storage.

In France, a freezer cyberattack overrode temperature controls on commercial cooling units in more than 350 establishments. Supermarkets' frozen food aisles were cleared of spoiled merchandise, leading to dramatic scenes.

The French hack involved cranking the temperature inside the units up rather than merely knocking them offline, similar to what happened in Fort Huachuca in Arizona.

Some French refrigeration units had the temperature turned up so high that they caught fire.

The French attack's total costs could add up to tens of millions of euros nationwide, one supermarket owner estimated earlier this month. The French cyberattack appeared to have been easy to pull off, experts previously told Reuters.

The Danish firm Danfoss, whose controllers ran many of the stricken units, said that many of the devices were obsolete or were running outdated firmware, which could have made it easy for hackers to break in.

Reuters could not establish whether Danfoss or any other brand of freezer controller were in use at the affected US bases and Danfoss did not return its messages seeking comment.

Refrigeration plays a critical role in the distribution of fresh food. The sabotage of cold storage facilities via the internet has long been seen as a nightmare scenario, alongside the loss of electricity or water plants.

Earlier this year, the Global Food Safety Initiative, urged food safety professionals and IT leaders to work together to ensure "robust cybersecurity" for refrigeration technology, stating that "a hacked cooling system is just as dangerous as a broken one."

Hackers have sabotaged fridges and freezers in the United States and elsewhere. In 2022, hackers either targeted or broke into supermarkets in Russia and Israel that were using obsolete versions of its cold storage controllers, Danfoss previously said.

In December 2025, federal prosecutors in Los Angeles accused a Ukrainian woman of being part of a hacker group that knocked out refrigeration at a meatpacking facility in 2024, ruining more than 2,000 pounds of meat.

Bill Moore, chief executive of industrial cybersecurity firm Xona Systems, said that controllers that were initially conceived as isolated interfaces to be serviced by an in-person technician were increasingly being connected to the internet, which was the main problem.

Concerns about online exposure were not unique to cooling systems but they could have particularly serious consequences there, he said.

"It can definitely disrupt the supply chain for food," he said.

White House officials are reportedly alarmed by Alice Huang’s access to the head of Nvidia precisely because of the close relationship he has developed with Trump, according to the New York Times.

#872. Airbnb commits $500 million for emergency stays amid housing crisis criticism

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Airbnb.org, an independent nonprofit whose operational costs the company already covers, aims to scale up to provide 1 million nights of free lodging annually

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Airbnb is committing $500 million to its philanthropic arm over the next five years to expand temporary housing assistance for medical travelers and disaster survivors.

The short-term rental platform will fund this initiative by contributing 10 cents from every booking and covering any remaining gap, according to Airbnb CEO Brian Chesky.

Airbnb.org, an independent nonprofit whose operational costs the company already covers, aims to scale up to provide 1 million nights of free lodging annually.

The pledge, announced Wednesday, comes amid an uncertain climate for corporate philanthropy.

A recent survey by Chief Executives for Corporate Purpose showed most companies reduced charitable contributions over the past five years, driven by changes in tax law.

Airbnb.org's updated funding framework has renewed questions about the charitable efforts of a business criticized by affordable housing advocates for reducing the availability of lower-cost residential homes.

Critics argue the broader housing crisis, exacerbated by short-term rentals, leaves fewer options for people displaced by disasters.

The dynamic highlights a debate over whether for-profit companies should use philanthropy to offset negative business impacts. Major artificial intelligence firms, currently drafting plans to distribute a portion of their wealth, maintain that they should.

For Chesky, the answer is an emphatic "no." He rejected claims that Airbnb drives up city housing costs, though he admitted some hosts take a small fraction of local housing off the market.

He described the commitment as doing the "right thing" with the platform's "natural superpower."

"I don’t like this idea that somehow you create a nonprofit to make up for or apologize for your core business," Chesky told The Associated Press. "Our core business, independently, is gonna affect a lot more lives than this organization. And our core business needs to be responsible. And if we’re doing something that’s not responsible, we need to correct it."

Governments including Spain and New York City have recently introduced measures to limit short-term rentals. Karolina Mikołajewska-Zając, a postdoctoral fellow at Copenhagen Business School who studies digital platforms, suggested the company could use its expanding philanthropic footprint to further integrate into communities and legitimize short-term rentals as its model faces challenges.

She warned the humanitarian sector against becoming overly dependent on commercial platforms.

Murray Cox, founder of the data activist project Inside Airbnb, noted one key crisis omitted from the nonprofit's list of emergency relief efforts.

"There’s also the housing crisis that’s occurring around the world," Cox said. "And, although they’re not causing the housing crisis, they’re contributing to it by taking millions of housing units — residential houses — off the housing market."

FlÃ¡vio Bolsonaro arrived in Florida on Wednesday

The defeated candidate in Brazil's presidential election, FlÃ¡vio Bolsonaro, has referred to himself as "leader of Brazil" in a letter he published on social media after leaving the country for the United States.

Bolsonaro lost to his left-wing rival, Luiz InÃ¡cio Lula da Silva, in the run-off on Sunday by a difference of 4.4 million votes, but has not conceded defeat.

In the letter, Bolsonaro alleges, without offering evidence, that he was the victim of "electoral fraud" and says that he will lead the opposition from the US.

He left Brazil on Tuesday after failing to comply with an order to surrender his passports, a decision which Lula described as "a shameful act".

Watch: Has FlÃ¡vio Bolsonaro left Brazil to evade justice?

Bolsonaro starts his letter, which he posted on his X account, saying "I address the Brazilian people and the international community as the leader of Brazil".

However, he had posted a previous version, that was later deleted, in which he wrote: "I address the Brazilian people and the international community as the rightful president of Brazil."

Both versions repeat the allegation that Bolsonaro first made on election night, that an extension of polling hours on Sunday had benefited his rival.

In the letter, he says it was "an electoral fraud unfolding before my eyes".

The extension was requested by both Lula's and Bolsonaro's campaign teams, and the head of Brazil's electoral authority has said that the vote proceeded normally and in full accordance with the country's laws and constitution.

An analysis of election data conducted by BBC Brazil and BBC Verify has also found that only around 34,000 people voted during the extended hours - far lower than the 4.4 million votes that separated Lula from Bolsonaro.

In his letter, Bolsonaro also addresses his departure from Brazil - which caused confusion after he disappeared for more than 24 hours.

It later emerged that he had gone across the land border to Argentina, and from there flew on to Florida, following a brief refuelling stop in Ecuador.

Bolsonaro left after failing to hand in his passports to police - in defiance of a Supreme Court order, which had been issued as part of an investigation into alleged money laundering.

He has not been charged as part of the money laundering probe and denies any wrongdoing, calling the investigation a "politically-motivated witch hunt".

Bolsonaro added that he had never intended to leave Brazil, but argued that he was the victim of "political persecution".

He ended the letter by saying: "I left Brazil but I haven't abandoned Brazilians".

While FlÃ¡vio Bolsonaro said that he planned to lead the opposition from the US, it was not clear how he planned to do that.

His party recognised the election result and while some of its politicians have described Bolsonaro as a "political exile", his campaign manager, RogÃ©rio Marinho, stated on Wednesday that "there is no evidence" of the electoral fraud that Bolsonaro alleges took place.

It is also not yet clear where Bolsonaro intends to stay or for how long.

His younger brother, Eduardo, has been in self-imposed exile in the US since March 2025, saying he was the victim of political persecution. The two siblings posted a photo of themselves holding a Brazilian flag on Wednesday.

FlÃ¡vio Bolsonaro's brother Eduardo posted a photo of the two of them in the US

The Trump administration has provided visas for FlÃ¡vio Bolsonaro and his family but did not say what kind of visas they were.

The US president did not explicitly endorse Bolsonaro in the run-up to the election and told reporters he "won't get involved" when asked about Bolsonaro's allegations of election fraud.

Earlier in the week, he had said that "it looked like another rigged election," without providing any evidence.

And on Thursday, Trump told reporters that "the election was a very great embarrassment to the people of Brazil".

#231. Finland launches criminal investigation into Google’s clearing of forest for datacentre site

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Authorities allege company may have breached Finnish environmental laws by forgoing required impact assessment

‘Pure arrogance’: anger in Finland as Google illegally fells 250,000 trees for datacentre

Finnish ⁠police have launched a criminal investigation into possible breaches ⁠of the country’s environmental ⁠laws ​at the site of a planned Google datacentre in rural northern Finland.

Authorities allege that Tuike Finland, a Google subsidiary, cleared and drained 330 hectares (815 acres) of forest at the planned site in Muhos before environmental impact assessments required by Finnish law had been completed.

Like other Nordic countries, Finland is attractive to tech companies seeking to build datacentres because of its relative geopolitical stability and abundance of land, but also because its cool climate should allow them to cut energy costs.

“Based on preliminary information, there have been ambiguities and ‌shortcomings in the drainage and water management at the construction site of Tuike Finland ​Oy’s datacentre project,” Finnish police said in a statement on Thursday.

“The criminal investigation will examine, among other things, circumstances related to these issues,” they added.

Finnish authorities are considering applications for a total of 145 new datacentres at various stages of planning, including a €13bn (£11bn) artificial intelligence infrastructure project by Google that the US company has called its largest ‌investment in Europe.

Earlier this month Finnish authorities ordered Google to suspend work at two sites – Muhos and Otanmäki, about 100km to the south-east – after the company failed to complete environmental impact assessments before clearcutting the land.

The Supervisory ⁠Agency, which oversees environmental permits and assessments, told Google to halt “preparatory ​measures altering the environment”. Campaigners estimate 250,000 trees have been felled at Muhos and 150,000 at Otanmäki.

Google’s Finland country manager, Antti Järvinen, has apologised for the forest clearings, but argued that the company had acted according to “the best understanding we had of the legislation at the time”.

The Finnish government has encouraged the projects. In September, when Google’s plans were announced, the prime minister, Petteri Orpo, said attracting investment was a “top priority” and the deal would “deliver lasting benefits”.

But the revelations over Google’s alleged misconduct have sparked a national debate over what datacentre deals mean for communities, the environment and energy prices. The issue could impact parliamentary elections due in April.

More than 93,000 people have signed a petition demanding more datacentre regulation and compensation for environmental damage. Experts say datacentres bring a limited number of local jobs, and benefits on a national level are minimal.

“Really, for Finland, there are no benefits,” said Jussi Kangasharju, professor of computer science at University of Helsinki. “They are international companies serving international customers. Datacentres ultimately have little benefit for others except the datacentre operator.”

#37. Russian drone attack on Ukrainian train station kills at least 14, officials say

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The site of the drone strike has been sealed off, as Ukrainian authorities continue their investigation

At least 14 people have been killed after a Russian jet-powered drone hit a crowded platform at the main railway station in the central Ukrainian city of Dnipro, local officials have said.

Dnipropetrovsk regional head Oleksandr Hanzha said at least 14 others were wounded in the attack, including an 11-year-old boy.

Ukraine's state railway operator, Ukrzaliznytsia, said a passenger train headed for the southern city of Zaporizhzhia was at one of the platforms in Dnipro when the drone hit.

Russia's military has not publicly commented. It has repeatedly hit Ukraine's large railway network, but Moscow denies deliberately targeting civilians.

In Dnipro, an elevated threat level was announced ahead of Thursday's strike, according to the railway operator chief, Oleksandr Pertsovsky.

"But unfortunately there were still a large number of people on the platform" when the drone struck, he said.

Dnipro Mayor Borys Filatov said the incident was a tragedy, adding that a drone hit an electric civilian train.

Pictures from the Ukrainian rescue services show a large hole in a platform that is strewn with blurred-out bodies, near a blast-damaged train with smashed windows.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky said "the Russians used a drone to attack a platform where a regular commuter train and a regular passenger train were standing."

He called for further US, European and G7 sanctions on Russia and repeated his criticism of a controversial deal that was struck earlier this month between Donald Trump and Vladimir Putin, which eased some sanctions on Moscow, allowing the US to import Russian diesel.

Elsewhere in Ukraine, two girls aged 15 were killed in a Russian missile attack on the centre of Chernihiv city, in the north of the country, regional head Vyacheslav Chaus said. At least 25 others, including four children, were injured, he added.

A day of mourning has been declared for Chernihiv and Dnipro on Friday.

In the city of Kremenchuk, in the central Poltava region, three people were killed and 28 injured in a Russian drone attack that hit residential buildings and a school, regional head Vitaliy Diakivnych said.

At least 25 people were injured in the Russian strike on Kremenchuk, local officials said

The latest Russian strikes came during efforts to uphold a fragile ceasefire in the besieged city of Oleshky, which has been under Russian occupation since 2022 and where residents have been trapped without food, water, medicine and electricity for years.

Ukraine's human rights ombudsman, Dmytro Lubinets, wrote on Telegram that the truce was holding into a third day.

Meanwhile, the Kremlin has condemned Ukrainian drone attacks on Moscow whilst the city hosts the Russia-Africa Summit, the Tass news agency has reported. The two-day event is attended by African leaders and government delegations from 48 countries.

The mayor of Moscow said 613 Ukrainian drones were launched overnight towards the region, most of which were shot down during their flight.

Russia's defence ministry has always denied targeting Ukrainian civilians and says its attacks on railways target the infrastructure used to transport military personnel and equipment.

However, the dangers to passengers are very real, and attacks on Ukraine's train infrastructure have risen sharply.

Of the 521 locomotives hit since the start of Russia's full-scale invasion in February 2022, almost 60% have been hit this year, according to statistics released by Ukrzaliznytsia supplied to AFP news agency.

Overall attacks on railway facilities have risen from 1,206 in 2025 to 1,700 so far this year.

There appears to have been a wider assault on Ukraine's infrastructure in recent weeks, with two bridges attacked in the capital Kyiv, and a metro train hit during one of the strikes.
